# TypeScript consistency & maintainability audit — 2026-08-31

Read-only audit of all 19 `packages/*` (~90k LoC TS) by parallel review agents plus
cross-cutting scans. **Tentative worklist — nothing here is committed or verified as
a fix yet.** Severity tags are the reviewers'. `hasOwn` = `Object.hasOwn`.

Legend: `[ ]` todo · `[x]` fixed · `[~]` decided not to fix (won't fix or
deferred — the reason is on the item) · **[high|med|low]** severity · `pkg path:line`.
Section 1 is the exception: its `[ ]`s are declined as a block, see the note there.

---

## 1. Systemic: prototype-chain lookups (`key in obj`, `obj[userKey]`) — THE headline

> **WON'T FIX — settled (user, re-confirmed 2026-09-16).** This whole class is closed:
> a property, GQL function or algorithm name that collides with an `Object.prototype`
> member (`toString`, `constructor`, `valueOf`, `hasOwnProperty`, `__proto__`) is not
> real-world input for this engine, and the cost of auditing every lookup — plus
> teaching the fuzzers to generate such keys, which would then report failures nobody
> intends to fix — is not worth paying against input that does not occur. The items
> below are kept as a written record of WHERE the pattern lives, so a future reader can
> see the class was surveyed and decided rather than missed. **Do not re-raise them**,
> and do not tick them: `[ ]` here means "known and declined", not "todo". Revisit only
> if someone brings a real case of these keys arriving from real data.

A plain object (`{}`) inherits `toString`/`constructor`/`hasOwnProperty`/`valueOf`/…
from `Object.prototype`. `'toString' in {}` is `true` and `({})['toString']` is a
function — with **no** patching, triggered by ordinary user data (a property named
`toString`, a GQL fn `toString`, an MCP algo `constructor`). Two harms: (a) a
**byte-identity break** vs the HashMap-backed Rust engine (which has no inherited
keys); (b) wrong values / mis-dispatch / rollback corruption. Fuzzers miss it (they
never generate reserved-word keys). Fix pattern already exists in-repo
(`executor.ts:284` `propPresent`, `projection.ts:380` `selectStep`, `protocol.ts` `Map`,
`temporal.ts:673`).

- [ ] **[high]** core `Vertex.ts:248` (+`121,158,159,222,230`), `Edge.ts:118` (+`141,176,177,232,240`) — `key in this.properties`: `hasProperty('toString')`→true → byte-identity break **and** rollback corruption (undo restores inherited fn) → `hasOwn`.
- [ ] **[low]** core `Graph.ts:2295,2405,2575,2907` — same in constraint checks (key is a declared-constraint name, lower risk) → `hasOwn`.
- [ ] **[high]** gremlin `dispatch.ts:183,407`; `projection.ts:170,195,261,287,314` — `has`/`values`/`valueMap`/`elementMap` emit inherited fns → `hasOwn` (sibling `selectStep:380` already does).
- [ ] **[med]** gremlin `misc.ts:88-93,225,232` — `math()` `UNARY_FN[name]`/`BINARY_FN[name]`/`MATH_CONSTS[name]` invoke prototype fns instead of faulting → `Object.create(null)`/`hasOwn`.
- [ ] **[high]** gql `scalars.ts:784` `NUM_CONSTANTS` + `759,765,771,713,725` (`UNARY_NUM`/`STR_ARG_POSITIONS`/…) — `RETURN toString(5)` leaks a raw `TypeError` `code:undefined` from the public API (violates LenkeError-code invariant) → build tables with `Object.create(null)`.
- [ ] **[med]** gql `clauses.ts:194` — `_MERGE` key inference `key in properties` on user map → `hasOwn`.
- [ ] **[high]** serialization `csv/index.ts:674` — `!(key in bag)` → encodes `"function Object()…"` for a missing cell → breaks round-trip + byte-identity → `hasOwn`.
- [ ] **[high]** mcp `tools.ts:198` — `ALGORITHMS[name]` invokes prototype fns (schema `enum` not enforced server-side) → `hasOwn` or make `ALGORITHMS` a `Map` (mirrors `protocol.ts`).
- [ ] **[med]** sync `engine.ts:333` — `scopeOf` `!(k in params)` mis-resolves scope to `Object` → `hasOwn`.
- [ ] **[med]** FUZZER GAP — differential/gremlin/codec/write fuzzers never generate reserved-word property keys (`constructor`/`toString`/`__proto__`/`hasOwnProperty`). Add them to the key generator so this class can't regress; re-run `bun run fuzz`.

> NOT bugs (correctly left alone by reviewers): `'from' in element`, `'label' in item`,
> `'hop' in el`, `'id' in v` (internal tagged-union discriminators), and
> `'size' in set` / `'length' in x` (testing an _inherited_ prop — `hasOwn` would be
> wrong there).

## 2. Adapter / codec drift (parity risk)

- [x] **[med]** native `backend-ffi-engine.ts` vs `backend-wasm-engine.ts` `fail()` — FIXED (2026-09-16): the report→`LenkeError` shaping moves to `marshal.ts` as `makeFail(readLastError)`; each backend keeps only its own last-error READ (raw pointers + `lnk_free` on ffi, linear memory + an out-len cell on wasm) and calls `makeFail` with it. One definition of what a failure looks like to a caller — prefix, code, details, generic fallback — which is what `backend-parity-fuzz` compares. Both backends' suites + the parity fuzzer green.
- [x] **[med]** native `graph.ts` — phantom `createIndex edge+hash` — **FIXED** (2026-08-31): `IndexSpec` narrowed to a discriminated union of the valid combos (`vertex+hash` | `edge+interval`), so `edge+hash` (and `vertex+interval`) is now a COMPILE error; removed the vestigial `createEdgeIndex` SchemaOp op + its `applySchemaOp` branch end-to-end. tsc + 63 schema/constraint tests green.
- [x] **[med]** NEW (found during the above) — native `backend-engine.ts:158` `indexOp` ignored `kind`, so a vertex **range** index round-tripped (dump→apply) as a **hash** index — **FIXED** (2026-09-14) by closing the capability gap rather than the mapping alone: TS's index vocabulary now matches the engine's four arms. `IndexKind` gains `'range'` and `'type'`; `IndexSpec` gains the `vertex+range` and keyless `edge+type` members; `SchemaOp` gains `createVertexRangeIndex` + `createEdgeTypeIndex` with their `applySchemaOp` branches; `indexOp` dispatches on `(on, kind)` and **throws** on an unmapped pair instead of guessing, so a future engine kind surfaces as an error, not a silently downgraded index on the replica. `indexKeys` now dedupes (hash + range on one key dump as two lines). Pure-TS `@lenke/core` accepts `'range'` (one structure serves both seeks there) and throws on `'type'` like `'interval'`. Docs corrected in three places that claimed a native hash index answers ranges (PropertyIndex.md, the MCP `indexes` guide, backend-embedded.md). Tests: dump-schema round-trip over both backends (ffi + wasm), 4 core kind tests, 3 new `schema_op` Rust tests (same-key hash+range coexistence, keyed drop taking both, unsupported `on`/`kind` pairs rejected).
- [x] **[med]** serialization streaming trailing newline — **FIXED** (2026-08-31): all three streaming codecs (ndjson/pg-text/csv) now emit the `\n` as a leading separator (no trailing), byte-identical to the sync `encode`; a `serialize === serializeAsync` byte test over an empty / small / 30k-element graph locks it. codec-fuzz still green.
- [x] **[med]** serialization graphson id coercion — **FIXED** (2026-08-31): decode now `String()`-coerces the vertex id, edge id, and the `outV`/`inV` lookup keys, matching pg-json and the Rust codec (which keeps even a numeric ingest id as text). Foreign numeric GraphSON ids now land and resolve.
- [x] **[low]** native rebuild-command doc names — FIXED (2026-09-10): backend-ffi/wasm docs now say build:rust/build:wasm disagree: `abi.ts` says `build:rust`/`build:wasm`, `backend-ffi-engine.ts:4` says `engine:build:rust`, wasm says `engine:build:wasm` → align to the real script; also fix stale "two/both backends" wording now that three consumers share the layer.

The duplication itself stays: the two are parallel by design, mirroring the native engine's naming, and a shared generic would cost more in readability than the duplication costs in maintenance.

## 4. Ad-hoc `throw new Error` vs `LenkeError`+code (~58 across 9 pkgs)

- [x] **[med]** gremlin `filters.ts:17` — FIXED (2026-09-09, this session, plus sources.ts:37 → Syntax) `fail()` step — bare `Error` can't be classified by `code`; error-code parity is an invariant → `LenkeError`+code (also `sources.ts:37` → `ErrorCode.Syntax`).
- [x] **[med]** core `Vertex.ts:186` — FIXED (2026-09-09): coded E_INVALID_GRAPH_OP from the shared #commitProperties funnel in both Vertex and Edge "no graph" is ad-hoc; evicted-element writes throw inconsistent raw `TypeError`s (Vertex has a guard, Edge doesn't) → one coded "write to evicted element" error across all four paths.
- [ ] **[low]** react `StoreContext.ts:62`, `SyncClientContext.ts:80` — provider-missing throws plain `Error` (defensible; decide whether host-wiring errors get a code or stay plain).

## 5. Async robustness (sync)

- [x] **[med]** sync `engine.ts` `runLoad` — **FIXED** (2026-08-31): both branches now use the guarded `safeNotify` instead of a raw `notifyChange`, and the error path records the error + schedules the retry BEFORE invoking the (now `try/catch`-contained) `onLoadError` — so a throwing callback or a dead-socket `send` can no longer abort scheduling or escape as an unhandled rejection. Regression test added (a throwing `onLoadError` still runs the full bounded retry chain).
- [x] **[low]** sync dedupe key forgeability — FIXED (2026-09-16): the dedupe window is namespaced by an authenticated `principal` the embedder passes to `createSyncHost` (a host is per-connection, so it is whatever that connection authenticated as). The key still has to come from the client — it must survive a reconnect onto a different host, so the server cannot mint it — but a client can now only address its own namespace. Omitting `principal` keeps the old shared namespace, documented as "unforgeable only as far as your clients are trusted".

## 6. Per-package correctness smells

- [ ] **[low]** core `Graph.ts:2133-2158` `valueType` Date/Map — DOWNGRADED (exotic): `validatePropertyValue` neither rejects nor recurses a raw `Date`/`Map` (non-Object prototype), so one stored as a property can misclassify as `'string'`. Requires passing a RAW `new Date()`/`new Map()` (not the documented temporal/record path). Cheap right fix = reject at the write boundary (fail loud), not patch the fallback. Deferred pending decision.
- [x] **[med]** fp empty-string dispatch — **FIXED** (2026-08-31): all 14 data-last combinators now dispatch on `iterable === undefined` (not truthiness), so `map(fn, '')` (and filter/take/slice/…) returns an empty result instead of the curried fn. Regression test added.
- [x] **[med]** fp `equals.ts:32` — FIXED (2026-09-09): throws RangeError past the cap — past `MAX_ITERATIONS` (1e6) `return false` even for equal iterables (silently wrong) → throw on cap or document as a precondition.
- [x] **[med]** tree `Trie` entries — **FIXED** (2026-08-31): `entries()` now gates on `isEndOfWord` (the real word marker `has`/`remove` use), not `value !== null` — so a word stored with a `null` value is enumerable, agreeing with `has()`. (The tombstone itself is fine — `remove` already sets `isEndOfWord=false`; `entries` just read the wrong field.)
- [x] **[med]** tree `TreeNode.ts:11-12` — FIXED (2026-09-09): imports UnaryFn/BinaryFn from /fp — local `UnaryFn`/`BinaryFn` with `any` defaults leak into public method sigs; `List` imports the canonical ones from `@lenke/fp` → import from fp.
- [x] **[med]** cli `\o`/`\r` in scripts — **FIXED** (2026-08-31): moved the `\o` tee onto `State` and added `\o`/`\r` cases to `runMeta`, so both interactive and `\i`-script paths share one command table (`\r` is a recognized no-op in scripts). Regression test added.
- [x] **[med]** cli `shell.ts:296` — FIXED (2026-09-09): runMeta wrapped in try/catch — meta lines in `metaInclude` are unguarded while query lines are per-line `try/catch`ed → a throwing `\c`/`\save` aborts the whole include → wrap `runMeta` too.
- [~] **[med]** dev `build.ts:33-40` `.tsx` blind spot — **WON'T FIX** (2026-08-31, user): no more `.tsx` packages are planned, so the perFile+`.tsx` combo won't occur.
- [~] **[low-med]** utils `sortedByKey.ts:10` — **DEFERRED on perf (user)**, re-confirmed 2026-09-16: `Object.keys().sort()` is UTF-16 code-unit order, feeding `toJSON`'s byte-identity promise → diverges from Rust codepoint order only on astral-plane _key names_. A codepoint-aware comparator costs on every sort to fix input nobody has; the divergence is real but unreached. Revisit if an astral key name ever shows up, or fold it in if the sort is rewritten for another reason.
- [x] **[low]** fp `utils.ts:3-6` — FIXED (2026-09-09): real boolean predicate via ArrayBuffer.isView `isTypedArray` — `@ts-expect-error` + `x.BYTES_PER_ELEMENT` as final term returns a number-as-bool → `typeof … === 'number'` for a real predicate.
- [~] **[low]** tree `Trie/TrieNode.ts` (was `TrieNode.ts:52-53`) — **WON'T FIX (user)**, re-confirmed 2026-09-16: `char: string` called with the `root` symbol is an intentional root marker, and the two `@ts-expect-error`s document it at the call site. Widening to `string | typeof root` would push the symbol into every consumer's type for one sentinel.
- [x] **[low]** list `List.ts:34` — FIXED (2026-09-09): readonly length; last() single pass `length` is public-mutable (`list.length=0` corrupts) → `readonly`; `:149` `last()` iterates twice → seed once.
- [ ] **[low]** cli `shell.ts:569` — query errors go straight to stderr, bypassing the `\o` tee/`\format` that results use → pick one policy.
- [x] **[low]** cli `cli.ts:101` — FIXED (2026-09-09): documented in USAGE — `--color` flag undocumented in USAGE.
- [x] **[low]** gremlin `runtime.ts:82-106` — FIXED (2026-09-09): keep-in-sync warning + subPlansOf.test.ts `subPlansOf` — hard-coded plan-field list; sibling `PATH_DEPENDENT_KINDS` carries a keep-in-sync warning, this doesn't → a new plan-bearing field silently disables path tracking → add the warning + a coverage test.

## 8. Error-surface quality (found 2026-09-16, while exercising Gremlin through the CLI)

Both found by running edge-case traversals against `samples/modern.ndjson`, not by
reading code — the CLI is the only wasm-only consumer, so it is where these show.

- [x] **[med]** engine `exec.rs` — an unsupported operator inside a filter body printed
      `{other:?}`: the whole IR subtree (slots, predicates, boxed inputs) dumped at a user
      who wrote a traversal. FIXED (2026-09-16): `Plan::op_name()` names the variant, the
      message says where the shape is unsupported and what to do about it, and it carries an
      `E_UNSUPPORTED:` prefix so the code stops collapsing to `E_INVALID_VALUE`.
- [x] **[high]** native `backend-wasm-engine.ts` — a wasm out-of-memory abort reached the
      caller as the bare string `unreachable`. The row-counted trail/intermediate budgets are
      tuned for the native heap, so on wasm the allocator can give up first, and Rust cannot
      catch its own abort. FIXED (2026-09-16): every export is wrapped so a
      `WebAssembly.RuntimeError` becomes a coded `E_RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED` explaining what
      happened and what to change, and the instance is marked unusable (a trapped module's
      heap is in an unknown state). The budgets deliberately stay identical to native —
      lowering them for wasm would make the same query succeed on one backend and fail on the
      other, which is what `backend-parity-fuzz` exists to catch.

## 9. Planner: the range index has no selectivity check (found 2026-09-17, by benchmark)

- [x] **[high]** engine `opt` — a vertex RANGE index is seeded whenever one exists on
      the key, with no check on how much it will actually filter. Measured on a 200k-row
      `Person` fixture (`simd_index_probe` E40): `WHERE age > 98` (1% pass) is **9.8x
      faster** with the index (303us -> 31us), but `WHERE age > 50` (49% pass) is **3.7x
      SLOWER** (321us -> 1184us) — seeking 98,000 rows through the index costs more than
      scanning 200,000. Same shape with a GROUP BY on top (377us -> 1138us).
      The engine behaviour predates this week, but `IndexKind: 'range'` only became
      expressible from TypeScript on 2026-09-14, so a user can now declare the index and
      make their broad queries four times slower with nothing to show why. `cost.rs`
      already estimates cardinality — the seek decision needs to consult it.

  FIXED 2026-09-23. The seeding rule now consults the measured crossover before
  emitting a `RangeSeek` (`seek_beats_scan`), reusing the `seed_fraction` /
  `range_count_capped` probe built for orientation — the two rules ask the same
  question, so the constant is now `SEEK_MAX_FRACTION` rather than an
  orientation-specific name. Re-measured on the same 200k shape (E74):

  ```text
  query                             no index     indexed    ratio
  age > 98  (1% pass, selective)       220.9us      37.3us   5.92x
  age > 50  (49% pass, broad)          812.9us     813.9us   1.00x
  age > 50, GROUP BY                   407.4us     413.2us   0.99x
  ```

  The win is kept and the loss is gone: declaring an index no longer costs a broad
  query anything. Below `SEEK_FLOOR_NODES` (4,096) the rule declines to decide and
  keeps the old behaviour — a full scan of 1,000 nodes is ~1.9us and the worst seek
  over them ~6us, so choosing differently would churn plans over a difference
  nobody can measure.

  Guarded by two unit tests rather than the fuzzer: both branches are
  answer-equivalent by construction (a `RangeSeek` IS `Scan`+`Filter`), so this is a
  cost decision and `rewrite_fuzz` — whose fixture is below the floor anyway — is
  the wrong instrument for it.

  THE RULE, MEASURED (E64/E65, 2026-09-17). Two flat constants decide it: a scan
  costs **1.88 ns per node scanned**, a seek **12.2 ns per row returned**, both
  independent of selectivity. So for a bare filter the crossover is
  `1.88 / 12.2 = 15.4%` — and the sweep confirms it, the index winning 1.6x at 10%
  and losing at 25% (0.6x). An earlier guess of "~2%" was far too conservative.

  With an EXPAND downstream the index wins at EVERY selectivity measured (3.1x at
  one hop, 15.5x at two). That does not follow from the constants — both plans do
  the same downstream work, which ought to cancel — and the reason is item 11
  below: expanding from a FILTERED frontier costs 4.55-15.65 ns/edge against a
  seek's clean id list. So the second clause compensates for an engine
  inefficiency, not a property of indexes; fix that and the 15% rule likely
  applies everywhere.

  What the planner needs today: seek when estimated selectivity is under ~15%, OR
  when an expand follows. Both clauses use cardinality `cost.rs` already estimates.
  Fix before further index work: it is a regression shape, not an enhancement, and
  it is reachable today.

## 11. Exec: expanding from a filtered frontier is 16x an expand from a seek (2026-09-17)

- [ ] **[med]** engine `exec` — the per-edge cost of an `Expand` depends on what fed it.
      Measured (`simd_index_probe` E48, 200k nodes x 8 edges): 0.28 ns/edge when the
      count shortcut applies, 4.55 ns/edge from a frontier filtered at 99% (i.e. the
      filter removed almost nothing), rising to 15.65 ns/edge at 1%. A `RangeSeek`
      feeding the same expand does not pay this. It is why item 9's second clause
      exists — the index looks unbeatable downstream of a filter partly because the
      filtered path is slow, not because the seek is fast. Worth isolating before the
      seek rule is tuned, or the rule will be fitted to an artefact.
      ISOLATED (2026-09-17, E68). The honest control is the same plan shape with and
      without the residual filter — same seek, same expand, same rows — because E48's
      reference was the count SHORTCUT, not an unfiltered expand. Split that way the
      16x is two costs with two different fixes:

  ```text
  count   NO filter (degree sum)      31.1us
  count   residual IsLabeled         475.5us   15.3x
  project NO filter                  320.7us
  project residual IsLabeled         625.6us    1.95x
  ```

  **~~[high] a defeated count shortcut~~ — WITHDRAWN 2026-09-17, see E71.**
  The 15.3x is NOT a penalty. `count(*)` over a bare `Expand` sums adjacency
  LENGTHS: it is O(sources) and never looks at an edge. Any predicate on the
  hop's endpoint must look at every edge to know which endpoints to test. They
  are different questions, and the "fast" one is the cheaper question. E71
  shows it directly — hold the sources fixed, scale the degree:

  ```text
  degree   edges walked   no filter   filtered   ns/edge
       2          4,072      32.9us    196.4us      40.2
       4          8,101      32.2us    267.8us      29.1
       8         16,065      34.3us    460.4us      26.5
      16         31,901      34.1us    712.0us      21.3
  ```

  The unfiltered count is FLAT while the edge count grows 8x; the filtered one
  tracks edges. Nothing is recoverable unless an endpoint predicate can be
  answered without visiting edges.

  Four attempts are recorded before that conclusion, so none gets re-derived:
  folding the predicate into the degree sum (**3.1x WORSE**; full note next to
  `try_fused_count` in fastpath.rs), a sparse binary-search membership path,
  three settings of the dense/sparse threshold (all under 20%), and blaming
  the fixture's degenerate all-nodes label (E70 — a 2000-node label costs
  331us against the 200k label's 488us, so the bucket is not it either).

  This also withdraws the claim that one fix closed three things: the 8.5x
  `spelling_probe` cliff and item 10's unlabelled-far-node decline share this
  cause, and it is the same non-penalty.

  **[med] The filter itself is 1.95x on a projection.** 305us over 16,065 rows
  is ~19ns/row, roughly a binary search into a 200k-id label bucket. Real,
  small, and the only part of this item still worth doing. E48's per-edge
  numbers (4.55-15.65 ns/edge) stand, and are the same family as E71's 21-40.

## 10. Planner: the native engine does not orient a fixed-length pattern (2026-09-17)

- [ ] **[high]** engine `opt` — a predicate on the FAR end of a fixed-length pattern
      never reaches an index, because the native optimizer does not score both ends and
      reverse. Measured (`simd_index_probe` E58/E59, 200k nodes x 8 edges, range index on
      `age`): `MATCH (a:Person)-[:KNOWS]->(b) WHERE b.age > 98` plans as
      `Filter <- Expand <- Scan` and takes 1349us; the SAME query written
      `MATCH (b:Person)<-[:KNOWS]-(a) WHERE b.age > 98` plans as `Expand <- RangeSeek`
      and takes 31us. **43x at 1% selectivity, 52.7x at 10%, 6.3x at 50%** — identical
      answers, differing by the written direction alone.
      The TS engine already does this (`orient`/`reversePath` in
      `packages/gql/src/executor/matching.ts`, which scores both ends and reverses when
      the far end is more selective), and `PropertyIndex.md` documents the behaviour
      without saying it is TS-only — so this is also a doc claim the native engine does
      not honour. `reversePath`'s own caveat is the guide for scope: a variable-length
      segment keeps its written orientation.
      ONE HOP DONE (2026-09-17). `opt.rs` reverses a one-hop pattern whose far-side
      predicate is seekable (`orient_eligible`/`orient_apply`/`reverse_one_hop`, plus the
      `swap_slots` remap the codebase had deferred at opt.rs:1344), and `lift_seed_label`
      hoists an `IsLabeled` conjunct out of the reversed predicate onto the seed `Scan`
      so the ordinary seeding rule can emit the `RangeSeek` (whose `label` is required).
      Measured on the far-side count (E66, 200k x 8, range index on `age`):

  ```text
  far node UNLABELLED (b)          677us   Filter <- Expand <- Filter <- Scan
  far node LABELLED (b:Person)     499us   Filter <- Expand <- RangeSeek
  hand-written backwards            32us   Expand <- RangeSeek
  ```

  1652us -> 499us, and the `spelling_probe` cliff 40x+ -> 4.4x. Three notes on the
  shape of what is left:

  THE ORDERING WAS THE WHOLE BUG. The pass sits BETWEEN two fixpoints, and both
  halves are load-bearing. `(a:L)-[:T]->(b:M)` arrives as two stacked `Filter`s, so
  running orientation first (as the initial version did) meant the LABELLED form —
  the only form that can produce a seek — never matched the rewrite at all, and
  measured 2.7x SLOWER than the unlabelled one. It needs the first fixpoint to merge
  the filters, and a second one afterwards to seed the reversed scan.

  THE REQUIRED LABEL IS NOW THE USER-FACING RULE, not a blocker: a far-side
  predicate reaches the index iff the far node is written with a label. Worth a doc
  line in `PropertyIndex.md` next to the TS `orient` claim.

  RESIDUAL 499us vs 32us IS NOT AN ORIENTATION GAP. Both plans seek and both expand;
  the difference is the `Filter` the oriented plan keeps above `Expand` to re-check
  the ORIGINAL seed's label on the far end. That is section 11 (filtered-frontier
  expand cost, 4.55-15.65 ns/edge), and 15x is squarely in its measured range.

  IT FIRES ONLY ON A PROVABLE WIN (2026-09-17). Reversing an UNLABELLED far node
  was a regression, and the spelling probe caught it once the fixture was given the
  index it had been missing all along. Without a label the reversed seed is
  `Filter <- Scan { None }`, which no rule can seek, so the reversal buys a
  pre-filter while PAYING a residual `IsLabeled` above the hop — and that residual
  defeats the count shortcut. Which way it trades is pure selectivity, which the
  optimizer does not know here: `b.age > 98` (1% pass) went 1652us -> 669us, but
  `b.age > -1` (100% pass) went 788us -> **3805us, a 4.8x regression**. So
  `reverse_one_hop` now declines unless a label lifts onto the seed. Gating the
  speculative case properly needs a cardinality estimate, and cost.rs has one.

  TWO HOPS DONE (2026-09-17, E72) — and this was the big one.
  `reverse_one_hop` required an `Expand` directly over a `Scan`, so every
  longer pattern kept its written direction. Re-measured after the pushdown
  split, the gap was exactly the 83x that had been filed:

  ```text
  2 hops forwards    178357us  Filter <- Expand <- Expand <- Scan
  2 hops backwards     2155us  Filter <- Expand <- Filter <- Expand <- RangeSeek
  ```

  `reverse_one_hop` is now `reverse_chain`, built on the observation that
  makes the generalization safe: reversing an n-hop chain maps slot `i` to
  `n - i`, and for n <= 2 that is exactly ONE `swap_slots(_, 0, n)` — the
  ends trade places, the middle stays put. At n = 3 it becomes two
  independent swaps, which `swap_slots` cannot express, so three hops
  decline (with a test).

  **178357us -> 2148us, 83x**, and the forwards spelling now produces the
  SAME PLAN as the hand-written backwards one — so `spelling_probe` reports
  "identical optimized plan" rather than two times that happen to agree.

  Two things the work turned up, both worth keeping:

  **The decision is now a TRIAL REWRITE, not a re-derivation.**
  `orient_scan` used to restate `reverse_one_hop`'s conditions, and they
  drifted apart once: the decision said yes, the rewrite declined, and
  consumers were renamed around a pattern that never reversed. It now calls
  `reverse_chain` on a clone and believes the answer, which cannot drift,
  for one clone per candidate at plan time.

  **The integration probe was structurally blind to the bug that mattered.**
  The first two-hop version put the MIDDLE node's label on the far end,
  dropping rows. Every E72 assertion passed anyway, because `social_store`
  labels every node `Person` — so a dropped `(b:Person)` check changes no
  answer there. A four-node unit fixture with a selective middle label
  caught it immediately. Second time on this branch that a probe agreed and
  a unit test disagreed.

  THREE+ HOPS DONE TOO (2026-09-17), and three hops turned out to hold the
  largest gap in the repo: **263502us written forwards against 37us written
  backwards** at 50k nodes (the forwards form does not finish at 200k). Now
  59.5us, **4429x**, and plan-identical to the hand-written form.

  What was capping it was the RENAME, not the peeling. Reversing an n-hop
  chain maps slot `i` to `n - i`; a single `swap_slots(_, 0, n)` is that
  permutation only while n <= 2, and at n = 3 it is the WRONG one — it
  leaves slots 1 and 2 crossed. `swap_slots` is now a thin wrapper over a
  general `map_slots`, and orientation renames through `reverse_slots`,
  which expresses the whole permutation. No hop limit remains.

  A crossed middle returns wrong rows only when the middle nodes are
  DISTINGUISHABLE, so `spelling_probe`'s uniform fixture cannot show it;
  `orient_three_hops_does_not_cross_the_middle_slots` builds a graph with a
  decoy route carrying the same labels in the opposite order.

  THE REQUIRED LABEL IS GONE (2026-09-17, E73), and with it the last
  orientation gate. `RangeSeek`/`IndexSeek` carried `label: String`, which
  quietly meant an unlabelled pattern could never use an index:

  ```text
  MATCH (n:Person) WHERE n.age > 98      30.6us   RangeSeek
  MATCH (n)        WHERE n.age > 98     303.1us   Filter <- Scan
  ```

  Nothing about the index needed it. BOTH property indexes are keyed by
  PROPERTY, not by label, and `range_seek_ids`/`index_seek_ids` applied the
  label afterwards as a post-filter over the candidates — with an explicit
  shortcut for when the label covers every live node. The field is now
  `Option<String>`: **303us -> 26.3us (11.5x)** on a query shape as ordinary
  as `MATCH (n) WHERE n.age > 98`, and the unlabelled far-side hop
  **1710us -> 448us (3.8x)**.

  GATED ON MEASURED SELECTIVITY, NOT ON A PROXY. Removing the label
  requirement invalidated orientation's "no label, no reversal" rule, but
  dropping that outright regressed the unselective case 2.6x (`b.age > -1`,
  0.95ms -> 2.44ms) — reversing something that filters nothing still pays a
  residual check above the hop. The gate is now the thing the label was
  standing in for: the far predicate's actual share of the graph against
  E64/E65's 15.4% crossover (`ORIENT_MAX_FRACTION`), read through a new
  `Store::range_count_capped`, which ABORTS once the count passes the
  threshold — so an unselective predicate costs a bounded probe rather than
  a full index walk (the same early-abort trick `dict_encode` uses). When
  selectivity cannot be known — a parameterized bound — it falls back to the
  old label rule, so a parameterized query behaves exactly as before.

  The `spelling_probe` far-side cliff is 40x -> 9.2x across the three
  changes, and what remains is item 11's non-penalty, not a planning gap.

  A SHIPPED BUG IN THIS, found 2026-09-19 by the new optimizer fuzzer
  (`opt/rewrite_fuzz.rs`) and fixed: `peel_hops` and `shift_slot` both asked
  `max_slot(pred) == Some(endpoint)` where they meant "reads that slot and NO
  OTHER". A predicate over slots {1, 4} has a maximum of 4, so it was attributed
  to hop 4 and renamed as if slot 1 were not there — emerging as a read of slot 4
  at a point in the chain where only slots 0 and 1 exist, which panicked rather
  than answering wrongly, but only by luck. `reverse_chain` documents this exact
  trap for the FAR predicate, where a maximum of 0 is sufficient because 0 is also
  the minimum; a middle slot has no such luck. Now `reads_only_slot`.

  It is reachable only through a DISJUNCTION: a mixed `And` is taken apart by the
  per-conjunct pushdown split (item 12) before orientation sees it, so only a mixed
  `Or` arrives still reading both ends.

  MID-PATTERN SPLITTING DONE (2026-09-27) — see item 24.

## 88. The Gremlin oracle had never generated `order(local)` (2026-10-03)

Item 87 fixed a silent wrong answer — `order(local)` replaced a typed frontier with a boxed
`Col::Gen`, so `g.V().order(local).out('R')` returned ZERO rows — and the reason it survived is
that NOTHING generated the step. Zero mentions of `local` across `gremlin-fuzz` and the 93-case
`gremlin-conformance` suite between them.

`order(Scope.local)` is now in the fuzzer's step pool, ascending and descending, placed last above
the fallback so it takes no other arm's band. Coverage `order(local)=13-21 generated / 12-16
non-empty` of 400, floors at 8/5. NON-EMPTY is the floor that matters: the step lands on whatever
frontier the preceding steps left, and over an empty one it passes whatever the step does.

TEETH, and this is the justification: reverting item 87's fix makes the new arm fail on seeds 1, 7
and 42. Before it, no cross-engine oracle could see that bug at all.

### What it found on its first run, and why half the addition is withheld

The COLLECTION half of the step — `fold().order(local)`, which is the only way to reach the sorting
behaviour, since the step pool's frontier is always a scalar — is NOT in the generator. It is red
against two PRE-EXISTING divergences, both bigger than a generator change, and landing it now would
mean committing a knowingly-red oracle. Both have ground truth from a real TinkerPop console
(`tinkerpop/gremlin-console`, `createModern()`):

**1. A list of ELEMENTS must fault.**

```text
g.E().fold().order(Scope.local)              -> THROWS ClassCastException
g.E().fold().order(Scope.local).by(desc)     -> THROWS ClassCastException
g.V().fold().order(Scope.local)              -> THROWS ClassCastException
```

TS throws; native answers (`g.E().range(1, 2).fold().order(local).by(desc)` returned a
one-edge list). A vertex or edge has no natural order — native already knows this, carrying a
`fault_on_element` flag on the GLOBAL `OrderPage` for exactly this reason. `order(local)` never got
it. Native's bug, with its own fix precedent to follow.

**2. NULL's place in the total order differs between the engines.**

Native's `Value::rank` puts `Null` at 7 — after `Num`(0), `Str`(1), `Bool`(2), `Temporal`(3),
`List`(4), `Record`(5), `Map`(6) — so nulls sort LAST. TS sorts them FIRST. Observed directly:

```text
ts:     [null,null,null,null,null,null,"PERSON",…,"SOFTWARE"]
native: ["PERSON",…,"SOFTWARE",null,null,null,null,null,null]
```

The engines are supposed to share ONE total order (the `cmp_total`/`compareTotal` contract), so one
of them is wrong, and it is not confined to this step: any sort of a null-containing list has it.
TinkerPop cannot settle this one — its `values()` SKIPS a missing property rather than yielding
null (`g.V().values('lang').fold().order(Scope.local)` is `[java, java]`, two entries from six
vertices), so it never builds such a list. That makes it a decision about our own contract rather
than a conformance question, and it is recorded here rather than guessed at.

### It also found a bug in the harness's own classifier

About one run in nine went red on a FALSE divergence: `g.V().order(local).by(desc).out('KNOWS',
'CREATED')` returned the same 7 elements from both engines in a different order
(`FUZZ_SEED=2907042784`). A multi-type hop's per-vertex adjacency order is unspecified and this
suite already compares those as a multiset — but `hasTopLevelOrder` matched `.order(` and so
counted `order(local)` as a stream order, switching the multiset comparison off.

It is not one: `order(local)` sorts WITHIN each traverser's value and leaves the stream's order
exactly as it found it. The helper now excludes it. Twelve fixed seeds and eight random `bun run
fuzz` sweeps are green after, against roughly one failure in nine before — which is the kind of
intermittency that gets a suite's failures dismissed rather than read.

### No bench sweep for this item

Nothing in the engine changed — the diff is the fuzzer and this file — so there is no engine
behaviour to regress and the `bench_all` screen would measure only its own noise. Stated rather
than skipped silently.

## 87. `order(local)` destroyed the frontier it was meant to leave alone (2026-10-03)

Item 86's closing note named `expand` as the next target, on the grounds that it had become slower
than the optional hop it used to beat — 9.02 ns/row against 4.27. **That gap does not exist.**
Min of three uncapped runs: `Expand` 3.94, `OptionalExpand` 4.18, so the plain hop is slightly
FASTER, as it should be (no miss bookkeeping). The 9.02 was a per-row spike — the third run shows
`Expand` at 8.94 while `OptionalExpand` held at 4.24, so the noise hits individual rows rather than
whole runs. Fourth time in this loop a single reading has invented a result; retracted before
spending the iteration on it.

The real find was next to it. `SortLocal` priced at 7.62 ns/row for what is, on a scalar slot, a
NO-OP — and chasing why found a wrong answer.

### The bug

`sort_local_cell` returns anything that is not a `List` or a `Map` unchanged, and ONLY a `Col::Gen`
can hold one: every typed column (`Nodes`, `Edges`, `Num`, `Bool`, `Str`) holds scalars. So over a
typed slot 0 the step was already the identity on the VALUES — while still doing this:

```rust
let sorted: Vec<Value> = (0..n).map(|i| sort_local_cell(batch.slot(0).value_at(i), …)).collect();
let mut slots: Vec<Col> = batch.slots.clone();
slots[0] = Col::Gen(sorted);
```

It replaced the column with `Col::Gen` whatever it held. A `Col::Nodes` boxes to
`Value::Num(id)` — not `Value::Node` — so the frontier stopped being a frontier, and every later
step found nothing:

| on a 40-vertex degree-3 fixture             | before | after |
| ------------------------------------------- | -----: | ----: |
| `g.V().out('R').count()`                    |    120 |   120 |
| `g.V().order(local).out('R').count()`       |  **0** |   120 |
| `g.V().order(local).values('name').count()` |  **0** |    40 |

A silent wrong answer, and 887 tests passed over it.

### Ground truth, not inference

A real TinkerPop console (`tinkerpop/gremlin-console`, `createModern()`) settles what the step
means over a frontier:

```text
g.V().out('knows').count()                    -> 2
g.V().order(Scope.local).out('knows').count() -> 2
g.V().order(Scope.local).count()              -> 6
g.V().order(Scope.local).values('name').count() -> 6
```

`order(local)` is transparent over a vertex frontier. Native now matches; the arm's own comment
("leaving the batch shape and every other slot untouched") had always said so.

FIXED by returning the batch unchanged when slot 0 is not a `Col::Gen`, at both sites (`pull` and
the `pull_body` twin).

|                                   |               before |                         after |                      |
| --------------------------------- | -------------------: | ----------------------------: | -------------------: |
| harness `SortLocal (scalar slot)` |              7.62 ns |                   **0.63 ns** | ~11x, the scan floor |
| bench `order(local) then hop`     | 1,955us / **0 rows** | 16,703us / **1,000,000 rows** |                    — |

The bench row gets SLOWER, and that is the point: the old path was fast because it had destroyed
the frontier and had no hop left to do. The row projects its endpoints rather than counting them so
the ROWS column shows the fix — a `count()` always returns one row and would have hidden it. (First
version of that row did exactly that and had to be changed.)

### CORRECTED (item 88): this fix REMOVED a divergence, and the TS engine was never wrong

The first version of this entry claimed the TS engine raises `E_SYNTAX` for `order(local)` over a
typed frontier, called that a TS conformance bug, and scheduled work against it. **That was wrong,
and the error was in the probe, not in TS.** In the TypeScript API `local` is
`(plan: SubPlan) => StepFn` — Gremlin's `local(<traversal>)` STEP — while the scope constant is
`Scope.local`. The probe passed the step builder where the scope belonged, and the `E_SYNTAX` was
its own type error.

Asked properly, TS is conformant and always was: over an 8-vertex ring,
`V().out('R').count()`, `V().order(Scope.local).out('R').count()` and
`V().order(Scope.local).values('name').count()` are all 8, and
`V().values('name').fold().order(Scope.local)` sorts the list. `orderLocalStep` has carried the
pass-through (`else { yield t }`) all along.

So the real result is better than the one first recorded: before this fix native answered 0 where
TS answered 8, and the fix REMOVED a cross-engine divergence rather than leaving one. Nothing was
testing it, which is the part that still matters —

Neither `gremlin-conformance` (93 cases) nor `gremlin-fuzz` generates `order(local)` AT ALL, zero
mentions in either, which is why a silent wrong answer in one engine went unnoticed against a
correct answer in the other. That gap is item 88.

### Teeth

| mutant                                                                         | caught by                                                                                        |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| the fix reverted (the original wrong answer)                                   | `order_local_over_a_scalar_frontier_is_transparent`                                              |
| the guard widened to pass through ALWAYS, so a real collection is never sorted | `order_local_still_sorts_a_real_collection` and `gremlin::tests::order_local_by_desc_on_numbers` |

Both directions, which is what a narrowing fix needs: the first says the step stops eating the
frontier, the second says it still sorts what it is for. The TinkerPop numbers are in the test's
doc comment so the next reader does not have to re-derive them.

### No regression elsewhere

`bench_all` before and after, 92 comparable rows: zero flagged in either direction.

## 86. An optional hop buffered candidates it had no predicate to mask (2026-10-03)

`plan_cost_by_operator` priced 19 of `Plan`'s 54 variants, and item 85's closing note said a
missing path in an UNPRICED operator was likelier than another win in the four already tuned. So
the harness was widened by ten, and the widening is what found this.

The new rows, against `Scan` as the floor (one capped run, so read the ratios rather than the
absolutes — the cgroup adds overhead across the board):

| operator                  | ns/row | note                                                               |
| ------------------------- | -----: | ------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| `Tree`                    | 899.59 | Gremlin `tree()`, builds a nested map — allocation by construction |
| `Unwind` (2-element list) |  84.63 | evaluates and allocates a list per row                             |
| `VarLength {1,1}`         |  37.18 | **UNREACHABLE** — see below                                        |
| `OptionalExpand` (1:1)    |  18.10 | **2.0x `Expand`'s 9.14 for the same hop**                          |
| `Expand then EdgeVertex`  |  15.00 | item 85                                                            |
| `SortLocal`               |   7.62 |                                                                    |
| `EdgeScan`                |   4.29 |                                                                    |
| `PathRecord`              |   0.63 | free                                                               |

Two of the new rows turned out to measure shapes NO query produces, and both are now labelled as
such in the harness rather than left looking like targets:

- `VarLength {1,1}` — both `{1,1}` and `{1}` flatten to an `Expand` in the optimizer (verified),
  so the walker is never entered with these bounds. It is a reference point for what the walker
  costs per hop, not something to fix. (Item 82's lesson, met again by building the harness case
  before checking the planner.)
- `OptionalExpand` with `keep_source: true` — GQL `OPTIONAL MATCH` always sets `false`, and
  Gremlin's `optional()` lowers to the branch machinery rather than this operator.

### The fix

`optional_expand` ran TWO passes over seven vectors: pass 1 filled `cand_row`, `cand_nbr`,
`cand_eid` and `ranges`, pass 2 read them back into `keep`, `nbrs` and `eids`. That shape exists so
a LANDING PREDICATE can be masked across the whole candidate set before the left-outer decision is
made — which row survives decides whether a source needs its miss row. With no landing predicate
pass 2 is pure copying, and `cand_eid`/`eids` are built even when no edge is bound.

One pass when `landing_pred.is_none()`, writing straight to the output. Emission order is
unchanged because it is the same loop — each source in order, its neighbours in adjacency order, a
single miss row when it has none.

|                                                        |     before |         after |           |
| ------------------------------------------------------ | ---------: | ------------: | --------: |
| harness `OptionalExpand (1:1)`                         |   18.10 ns |   **4.27 ns** |  **4.2x** |
| bench `optional hop`                                   |  7,899.4us | **4,553.1us** | **1.73x** |
| bench `optional hop + miss` (no candidates to buffer)  |    619.2us |       620.7us |         — |
| bench `optional hop + landing pred` (keeps two passes) | 10,168.4us |    10,150.5us |         — |

Min of three each way. BOTH controls behaving exactly as predicted — the all-miss row flat because
there is nothing to buffer either way, the landing-predicate row flat because it still takes the
old path — is better evidence than the headline number that the change does what it says.

It also leaves the optional hop FASTER than the plain `Expand` (4.27 against 9.02) for the same
traversal, which is the next lead: `expand` is worth the same look.

### Teeth

| mutant                                                            | caught by                            |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------ |
| the miss row is never emitted (left-outer becomes inner)          | 5 tests, including two existing ones |
| the miss lands NULL instead of the source (`keep_source` ignored) | NOTHING at first — see below         |

`an_optional_hop_agrees_with_and_without_a_landing_predicate` is the load-bearing one: an
always-true landing predicate (`q.k >= 0`, true for every vertex and not constant-foldable) makes
the SAME question take the two-pass path, so the two implementations are compared row for row and
in order, rather than the new one being compared to a hand-written expectation.

The second mutant passed the entire suite, and the probe said why: nothing produces
`keep_source: true`. A Gremlin test named for that behaviour passed under the mutant because
`optional(out())` never reaches this operator — so it was renamed to say what it actually covers,
and `an_optional_expand_can_pass_the_source_through` builds the plan DIRECTLY, which is the only
thing that can reach the branch. Same treatment as item 81's dotted-key guard: a contract the
lowering may use later, pinned by the only test that can see it.

### A harness case is an unbounded run until proven otherwise

The first widened list included `OptionalScan`, which is a LEFT-OUTER correlated node scan — over
this fixture 200,000 input rows times every matching node, about 13 billion. It had to be killed.
It is now excluded with the reason recorded, and the whole harness runs under the same
`timeout` + `MemoryMax` + `MemorySwapMax=0` scope a mutation run gets. A new measurement case is
arbitrary code until it has been run once; treating it as safe because it is "just a benchmark" is
how the machine gets taken down.

### No regression elsewhere

`bench_all` before and after, 89 comparable rows: zero flagged in EITHER direction, the first
clean screen of the four (items 83, 84 and 85 each had a false positive to chase down).

## 85. An edge-to-endpoint step boxed the id it already had (2026-10-03)

Item 84's table left `Expand then EdgeVertex` at 9.87 ns/row against `Expand`'s 3.98 for the same
frontier, where the only extra work is reading one id and looking up its endpoints. The arm read
that id as `b.slot(*edge_slot).value_at(i)` — `Value::Num(f64::from(eid))` built and then
`x as u32` taken back off it, a `Value` and a float round-trip per row to recover the number a
`Col::Edges` column already held.

FIXED by branching on the REPRESENTATION once instead of per row: `elem_ids` returns the raw
`&[u32]` of a typed element column, and the boxed match stays for the branch/mixed frontier that
needs it (an unboxed `Value::Edge` from another arm, with non-edge cells skipped).

|                                               |     before |          after |       |
| --------------------------------------------- | ---------: | -------------: | ----: |
| harness `Expand then EdgeVertex`              |    9.92 ns |        7.60 ns |     — |
| the operator's own share (row minus `Expand`) |    5.87 ns |    **3.67 ns** | 1.60x |
| bench `outE.outV`                             | 15,026.9us | **12,544.5us** | 1.20x |
| bench `outE.outV.values`                      | 23,636.5us | **21,277.7us** | 1.11x |

Min of six runs before against five after. An earlier 2-sample reading gave 1.24x and 1.26x, and
the 1.26x rested on a single low outlier (18,738.5 against 21,052.7 in the same pair) — these rows
range 3-12%, so two samples is not enough to quote.

### Finding the one spelling the planner keeps

The obvious bench row was `g.V().outE('KNOWS').inV().count()`, and it did not move: 597us against
594us. Instrumenting the arm across the whole Gremlin group gave ZERO calls — the operator is never
reached there. `outE().inV()` is equivalent to `out()` and the optimizer rewrites it, which the
numbers say out loud: `outE.inV` cost 594us and the plain `out` row cost 604us, the same hop.

Checked which spellings keep a `Plan::EdgeVertex`:

| spelling                                                                                                     | keeps it                          |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------- |
| `outE('R').inV()`, `outE('R').otherV()`, `bothE('R').otherV()`, `outE('R').as('e').inV()`, `inE('R').outV()` | no — all rewritten to a plain hop |
| **`outE('R').outV()`**                                                                                       | YES                               |

Stepping from an out-edge BACK to its source is the one step that cannot become a hop, because it
returns the traverser where it came from. So that is the bench row, and the rewritten spellings are
recorded next to it as non-guards.

Three iterations, three ways for a bench row to mislead: item 82's fast path fired only in the
harness because the planner pairs different slots; item 84's row was flat because the operator is a
minority of the query; this one was flat because the planner REWRITES the obvious spelling away.
The harness prices the operator, the bench row proves the planner reaches it, and neither alone is
evidence.

### What the mutants said

| mutant                                                        | caught by                                                                                                                                                                             |
| ------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `elem_ids` always declines, so every row takes the boxed path | nothing in the suite — a PERF-ONLY mutant, and the bench row is its guard: 14,580.2us against 12,544.5us on mins, a 16% separation against an ~11% range. Detectable, not comfortable |
| the typed arm reads `ids[(i + 1) % len]`                      | five `gremlin_ported` cases — and NOT this item's own test until it stopped sorting                                                                                                   |
| the `u32::MAX` sentinel check removed                         | nothing, BECAUSE IT IS REDUNDANT — see below                                                                                                                                          |

The second is the one worth keeping: my test compared SORTED name lists, and every row permutation
preserves a sorted multiset, so a mutant that rotates the rows was invisible to it. It now asserts
the UNSORTED sequence over an explicit chain (`v0->v1->v2->v3`), which also distinguishes `outV`
from `inV` — a regular digraph cannot, since there in-degree and out-degree are both 3 and the two
multisets coincide, which is how the first version of that assertion passed on `dense_store`.

### A redundant check, removed rather than kept

The first version skipped `u32::MAX` in the typed arm, mirroring the boxed path's NULL skip.
Mutating it out left the whole suite green, and the reason is not missing coverage: `edge_endpoints`
is a bounds-checked `.get()` that returns `None` for an unknown eid, so the row is skipped one line
later either way. Item 78 deleted an unreachable guard and item 81 kept one that prevented a latent
wrong answer; this one is simply redundant, so it is gone and the reasoning sits where it was.

### No regression elsewhere

`bench_all` before and after, 87 comparable rows: ZERO flagged over 15%. One row read 0.70x
(`query/counts|filtered`, 523.7us to 367.4us) which this change cannot explain — that row has read
309-367us all session, so 523.7 was the before-run's outlier, the same screen artefact items 83 and
84 recorded.

## 84. The whole-row distinct had the same hash the dedup just lost (2026-10-03)

Item 83's table left `Distinct (whole row)` at 15.09 ns/row next to `DistinctBy (slot 0)` at 4.77 —
the SAME question (dedup one element column) at three times the price. `try_distinct_typed` already
had a `Col::Nodes | Col::Edges` arm, and it held the identical `FnvSet<u32>` that item 83 had just
removed from `dedup`'s path. One fix, two call sites, found a day apart because the operator table
prices them as separate rows.

The arm now uses `SeenIds`. Its `Col::Bool` neighbour has always used a dense `[bool; 2]`, so this
is that idea at element width; `Col::Num` keys on group bits and `Col::Str` on the string, and
neither can be a bitset — they stay hashed and are the control arms.

|                                                  |    before |       after |        |
| ------------------------------------------------ | --------: | ----------: | -----: |
| harness `Distinct (whole row)`                   |  15.09 ns | **5.67 ns** |   2.7x |
| harness `DistinctBy (slot 0)` (item 83)          |         — |     4.78 ns |      — |
| bench `distinct node`                            | 127,938us |   123,587us | 1.035x |
| bench `distinct prop` (a `Dict` column, control) | 3,081.7us |   3,090.2us |      — |

### The query-level number is small, and the reason is structural

3.5% on `MATCH (p:Person)-[:KNOWS]->(q) RETURN DISTINCT q`, because that query returns 198,672
ELEMENT MAPS and rendering them is ~500 ns a row (item 76) — the dedup is a minority of it by
construction. `Plan::Distinct` is almost always terminal in ISO-GQL (`RETURN DISTINCT …` ends the
statement), so its output goes straight to egress and egress dominates. The one non-terminal caller
is `pull_body`, inside a `CALL` body.

So the harness row is the primary guard here and the bench row is a weak witness, which is the
honest position rather than a shape hunted until it flattered the change. What justifies the commit
is not the 3.5%: it is that two code paths answering one question now cost the same, where one was
three times the other.

### The guard that became load-bearing

`a_distinct_over_optional_misses_keeps_one_null` was written in item 83 and did NOT reach `SeenIds`
then — `Plan::Distinct`'s element arm still used a hash set, so the `u32::MAX` sentinel could not
over-allocate there. After this change it does reach it, and that test now catches a mutant that
dedups nothing.

The sentinel branch itself remains a RESOURCE claim, re-verified: removing it leaves the test green
(the ANSWER is unchanged — fresh once, then seen) while allocating 67 million words for one row.
What changed is that the shape reaching it is now a real query (`MATCH (p) OPTIONAL MATCH (p)-[:R]->(q)
RETURN DISTINCT q`) rather than a direct unit call. The word-count assertion in
`seen_ids_answers_exactly_what_a_hash_set_would` is still the only thing that pins it.

| mutant                         | caught by                                                                                                              |
| ------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| the element arm dedups nothing | `a_whole_row_distinct_keeps_first_seen_order_for_every_typed_column`, `a_distinct_over_optional_misses_keeps_one_null` |
| the sentinel branch removed    | the word-count assertion alone, as in item 83 — correctness is unaffected                                              |

`a_whole_row_distinct_keeps_first_seen_order_for_every_typed_column` also pins the untouched arms,
including that `-0.0` and `0.0` are ONE distinct value under the grouping contract — a `Num` arm
regression would otherwise be invisible to a test that only looked at elements.

### The regression screen produced its second false positive

`bench_all` before and after, 85 comparable rows, flagged FOUR — all in `query/shortest`'s
repeat-group block, and moving in BOTH directions: `control` 0.75x, `head(b)` 0.73x, `+ path` 0.77x,
`unread` 1.25x. A dedup bitset cannot speed three of those by a quarter and slow the fourth by a
quarter, so the pattern itself was the answer.

Re-measured on the same binary, `repeat-group control` reads 67,924.9 / 73,025.2 / 79,976.7 — a
17.8% range with nothing changing, which is WIDER than the 15% threshold the diff screens at.

Min against min, both flagged rows are FASTER after, not slower:

| row                    | before (min of 2) | after (min of 3) |       |
| ---------------------- | ----------------: | ---------------: | ----: |
| `repeat-group unread`  |        16,990.8us |       15,148.1us | 0.89x |
| `repeat-group control` |        75,152.8us |       67,924.9us | 0.90x |

So the 1.25x "regression" was an outlier in the after-run, and the 0.73-0.77x "improvements" were
outliers in the before-run. Nothing in that group moved for a reason this change could supply.

Item 83's flag (`project 3` at 1.19x) was the same story. So the diff is a SCREEN, not a verdict:
these 600,000-row rows run inside one `bench_all` process where allocator and cache state carries
across groups, and only min-of-N on the flagged row decides anything. Worth keeping in view
whenever that diff is read.

## 83. Deduping an element frontier paid a hash per row (2026-10-03)

The target item 82's operator table named: `DistinctBy` over ONE slot at 15.58 ns/row, 21x the scan
floor and indistinguishable from deduping the WHOLE row by serialized bytes (14.90). The same
byte-key cause as the join, it looked like — and it was not. `distinct_by_keep` had carried a typed
path keying on the raw `u32` since long before this:

```rust
for (i, &id) in ids.iter().enumerate() {
    if seen_ids.insert(id) {   // seen_ids: &mut FnvSet<u32>
```

The byte keys were already gone. What remained was the HASH: a `FnvSet<u32>` insert per row, which
is about what 15 ns buys. Diagnosing before assuming was the whole difference between fixing this
and rewriting a path that had already been fixed.

FIXED with `SeenIds` — a bit per dense id, plus one flag for the `u32::MAX` OPTIONAL sentinel. It
grows on demand rather than being sized from the store, so it is correct for a node frontier and an
edge frontier alike without being told which it has, and `insert` returns true on first sight
exactly as `FnvSet::insert` does, so first-seen order is unchanged.

|                                            |     before |          after |           |
| ------------------------------------------ | ---------: | -------------: | --------: |
| harness `DistinctBy (slot 0)`              |   15.58 ns |    **4.77 ns** |      3.3x |
| harness `Distinct (whole row)` (control)   |   14.90 ns |       15.09 ns |         — |
| bench `dedup then values`                  | 10,325.7us |  **5,665.3us** | **1.82x** |
| bench `dedup then out`                     | 24,571.3us | **18,651.6us** |     1.32x |
| bench `dedup out`                          |  1,941.6us |      1,910.8us |      1.6% |
| bench `dedup city` (a Str column, control) |    268.5us |        266.7us |         — |

### Which bench row to believe, and why two of them barely move

`g.V().out('KNOWS').dedup().count()` moved 1.6%, because it never builds the set at all — a
`dedup` whose only consumer is a count routes to the distinct-endpoint counting sink, which tallies
reached nodes without a dedup structure. The rows that moved are the ones with WORK AFTER the
dedup (`.values('name')`, `.out('KNOWS')`), where the operator is genuinely on the critical path.

Both are in the corpus now, labelled: the mid-pipeline pair as the guards, `dedup out` as the
counting-shortcut control, `dedup city` as the wrong-column-type control. Finding the shape that
exercises the operator was the work — the same lesson item 82 recorded from the other direction,
where a harness-only win hid a fast path the planner never reached.

### A resource guard, pinned as one

Removing the sentinel branch leaves every test green, because keying `u32::MAX` as a raw id gives
the same ANSWER: fresh once, then seen. What it does instead is resize the bitmap to 67 million
words — 536 MB for one row.

So the thing pinned is the WORD COUNT, not the answer: `SeenIds::words` (test-only) asserts the
bitmap stays empty after a sentinel insert, and sized by one and two words after ids 63 and 64.
Mutation found this: the correctness assertion alone passed with the branch deleted, which would
have shipped a 536 MB allocation behind a green suite.

| mutant                                                          | caught by                                                                                   |
| --------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| freshness read AFTER setting the bit (so nothing is ever fresh) | `seen_ids_answers_exactly_what_a_hash_set_would`, `an_element_dedup_keeps_first_seen_order` |
| the sentinel branch removed                                     | the word-count assertion, and NOTHING else — 880 tests passed without it                    |

`seen_ids_answers_exactly_what_a_hash_set_would` runs the two structures side by side over a
sequence with repeats, the sentinel, and ids far enough apart to grow the bitmap several times,
asserting they agree call for call. That is the contract, so it is checked against the thing it
replaces rather than against a hand-written expectation.

### No regression elsewhere

`bench_all` before and after, 81 comparable rows: ONE flagged over a 15% threshold — `query/gql
project 3` at 1.19x, which this change cannot touch. Re-measured min-of-three each way it is
5,107.4us against 5,141.7us, +0.7%. The flag was a false positive from comparing single runs on a
row whose own range is about 10%, which is worth remembering when reading that diff.

## 82. A hash join keyed on serialized bytes where a u32 would do (2026-10-03)

`expression_cost_by_kind` found items 72 through 77 by enumerating `Expr` against the arms that
dispatch on it and pricing each. `Plan` has 54 variants and ONE `pull` that dispatches all of them,
so `plan_cost_by_operator` asks the same question of operators: each row wraps the same 200,000-row
scan, so what is priced is the operator rather than its input.

The table, in cost order, with `Scan` as the floor (produce the frontier and nothing else):

| operator                                                        |    ns/row |   xScan |
| --------------------------------------------------------------- | --------: | ------: |
| `Join on slot 0`                                                |    207.05 | **282** |
| `Enumerate` (Gremlin `index()`)                                 |    185.50 |     253 |
| `OrderPage` (full sort)                                         |     47.66 |      65 |
| `DistinctBy` (one slot)                                         |     15.58 |      21 |
| `Distinct` (whole row)                                          |     14.90 |      20 |
| `Expand then EdgeVertex`                                        |      9.87 |      13 |
| `OrderPage` (LIMIT 10)                                          |      6.47 |       9 |
| `Aggregate GROUP BY`                                            |      6.05 |       8 |
| `Expand` (1:1 hop)                                              |      3.98 |       5 |
| `Filter`, `Project (3)`, `Sample`                               | 2.97-3.83 |     4-5 |
| `Project (1)`, `MapSlot`, `Union ALL`, `Tail`, `NullPadIfEmpty` | 0.75-1.68 |     1-2 |

`Join` is the most expensive, and `DistinctBy` on ONE slot costing the same as whole-row `Distinct`
(15.58 against 14.90) is the tell that names the cause: both key on BYTES where the key is a dense
id.

`join_key` boxes each cell — `Col::Nodes` becomes `Value::Num(f64::from(id))` — and serializes it
into a fresh `Vec<u8>`, so a join pays a `Value` and an allocation per row on BOTH sides. For an
element frontier that byte string's entire content is a `u32`.

FIXED in two steps, each measured:

|                                                              |   ns/row |
| ------------------------------------------------------------ | -------: |
| byte keys (`Vec<u8>` per row, `FnvMap<Vec<u8>, Vec<usize>>`) |   207.05 |
| a map keyed on the `u32` (`FnvMap<u32, Vec<usize>>`)         |    59.96 |
| a COUNTING index: counts, prefix sums, one flat array        | **5.60** |

The map was already 3.5x, but 200,000 distinct keys is 200,000 bucket allocations. Dense ids mean a
key's bucket can be a slice of one flat array, so the whole index is three allocations rather than
one per key. The `u32::MAX` OPTIONAL sentinel takes the slot just past the largest real id, so it
keys as itself without sizing the index by four billion.

|                              |    before |       after |           |
| ---------------------------- | --------: | ----------: | --------: |
| harness `Join on slot 0`     | 207.05 ns | **5.60 ns** |   **37x** |
| bench `join on a shared var` | 106,950us | **4,561us** | **23.4x** |

### The bench row caught what the harness could not

The first attempt matched `on == [(0, 0)]`, and the harness said 37x while the bench row did not
move AT ALL — 106,950us against 105,828us. The reason is the shape: `MATCH (p)-[:R]->(q) MATCH
(r)-[:R]->(q)` joins on `q`, which is slot **1** on both sides, because each side's slot 0 is its
own start node. The fast path fired in the isolated harness and never once in a real query, because
the harness was measuring a slot pairing the planner does not produce.

Generalized to any single slot pair, the bench row moved 23.4x. Worth recording as the general
lesson: the harness prices the operator, the bench row proves the planner reaches it, and only the
pair of them is evidence. A comma pattern, incidentally, does NOT build a join — it lowers to a
chain; it is a second `MATCH` clause sharing a variable that does.

### Byte-identical by construction, and tested that way

Two claims, both checkable rather than arguable:

- **Same rows.** The key derivation agrees cell for cell: `value_at` maps the sentinel to NULL on
  both paths (so sentinel matches sentinel either way) and every other id to its number.
- **Same ORDER.** The right side is indexed with `j` ascending and probed with `i` ascending, so
  each bucket keeps input order — which is how the byte path's buckets are built too.

`a_dense_join_matches_the_boxed_join_row_for_row` checks both directly. The fixture is the
equivalence: a `Col::Num` holding the same ids boxes to `Value::Num(id)`, exactly what a
`Col::Nodes` cell gives, so joining two `Num` columns takes the general path over identical keys.

**The first version of that test proved nothing**, and the mutation found it: reversing each
bucket's fill order passed, because every output row of one bucket carries the same join VALUE, so
which input rows were paired is invisible. The fixture now carries a distinct TAG column per side,
and the assertion is on the tags — `0/101 0/104 0/105 1/100 …` — which names the pairing.

| mutant                               | caught by                                                                           |
| ------------------------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| each bucket's rows come out reversed | `a_dense_join_matches_the_boxed_join_row_for_row`, once it had tags                 |
| the sentinel is keyed as its raw id  | `a_dense_join_keys_the_optional_sentinel_as_itself` plus three `rewrite_fuzz` cases |
| the out-of-range guard is removed    | both dense-join tests plus `rewrite_fuzz`                                           |

`rewrite_fuzz` already covered `hash_join` and asserts `joins > n/10` of its generated plans, so the
correctness of WHICH rows had an oracle before this; green at 200,000 seeds. It compares multisets,
so the ORDER claim is the tests' alone — and per the settled policy an unordered binding table's
order is unspecified anyway, which makes order preservation a quality-of-implementation property
here rather than a conformance one.

### Still priced, for the next pass

`Enumerate` at 185.50 ns/row is second and allocates a two-element `Value::List` per row by
construction — a Gremlin-only step (`index()`), so low value. `OrderPage`'s full sort at 47.66 is
inherent to sorting and already has a top-k path (6.47 with a LIMIT). `DistinctBy` at 15.58 is the
same byte-key cause as this item and the same fix shape: dedup by one element slot wants a bitset
over dense ids, not a hash of serialized bytes. That is the next one.

## 81. count(property) materialized the column it never reads (2026-10-02)

The target item 77 left standing. `count(expr)` counts NON-NULL values, so for a plain `Prop` it
never needs the values at all — the answer is how many of the scanned nodes hold one. Native built
the whole column first:

|                                                |    before |
| ---------------------------------------------- | --------: |
| `RETURN count(p.name)` — a `Dict`/`Str` column | 1,048.4us |
| `RETURN count(p.age)` — a `Num` column         |   112.2us |

Item 77 took the string row to 853.7us by stopping the per-cell boxing in the fold. What remained
was the materialization: `read_property` built a 200,000-element `Col::Str` — an `Arc` clone per row
— before the aggregate ran.

`try_scan_num_agg` already answered `Count` over a `Prop` without materializing, and had since long
before this — but ONLY for a `Column::Num`, which is the whole explanation for the 9x between the
two rows. The column's TYPE is irrelevant to a count.

FIXED with `try_scan_count_prop`, which takes a column of any kind and counts presence via a new
`Column::has_value_at`. `Count` came OFF `try_scan_num_agg`'s accepted functions, so there is one
implementation rather than a fast one for numbers and a slow one for everything else.

|                                        |    before |      after |           |
| -------------------------------------- | --------: | ---------: | --------: |
| bench `count(prop) str`                | 1,048.4us | **93.0us** | **11.3x** |
| bench `count(prop) num`                |   112.2us | **93.4us** |     1.20x |
| bench `count(DISTINCT prop)` (control) |     166us |      166us |         — |

The two rows landing on the SAME number is the result worth reading: one question, one cost,
whatever the column holds. `DISTINCT` needs the values, so it keeps the general path and does not
move.

### `has_value_at` is not `present_at`, and the difference is the whole correctness question

`present_at` counts a stored present-NULL, because `has('k')` / `PropertyExists` must. A value READ
gates on `present` alone — a present-null reads NULL — so for every typed variant the `present` bit
IS "holds a non-null value", and only `Gen` keeps its null in `data` and has to look. Verified
empirically before writing it, on a `Column::Num` carrying a `SET x = null`: count, sum and avg all
already excluded it.

### The oracles generated NONE of this, and that was most of the work

`count(<expr>)` was generated by no fuzzer in either engine. The differential fuzzer emitted only
`count(*)` — thirteen occurrences, all of them `*`. `rewrite_fuzz` builds `Count` with `arg: None`
and gives its property-argument aggregates `Sum`/`Min`/`Max`/`Avg`. So a fast path for "count the
non-null values of a key" would have shipped with zero cross-engine coverage, which is the class
that produced items 64 through 68.

The differential fuzzer gained a `count(n.<key>)` arm, placed last above the fallback so it takes no
other arm's band. The KEY LIST is the point rather than the shape: `n`/`s` are on every vertex, `st`
and `nan` on two of three, `zz` on none, and `m.k` is a record field — so a value, an absent cell, a
key with no column, and a dotted path all get drawn. Five forms, including a FILTERED count and a
`LET`-bound grouped one (the grouped fold is a different code path and only the scalar one has a
shortcut). Coverage `cntProp=410-433 generated / 359-376 non-zero` of 20,000, floors at 200/150.

`rewrite_fuzz` gained `AggFn::Count` in its property-argument pick list.

| mutant                                                           | caught by                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| ---------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| a stored present-NULL is counted (`has_value_at` → `present_at`) | `count_of_a_property_is_the_same_whatever_the_column_type`, `count_of_a_property_counts_present_cells_only`, `present_null_semantics_characterization`                                                             |
| the whole column is counted, ignoring the label and deletions    | `the_count_shortcut_respects_the_label_and_the_living`, and that test alone                                                                                                                                        |
| the arm matches through a `Filter` and swallows it               | the DIFFERENTIAL FUZZER alone at first — neither `rewrite_fuzz` nor the 874-test Rust suite saw it. A ground-truth assertion was then added for it, because a deterministic claim should not rest on a random seed |
| the dotted-key guard is removed                                  | `the_count_shortcut_declines_a_dotted_key`, and nothing else — see below                                                                                                                                           |

**The `rewrite_fuzz` addition is NOT justified by a mutant.** It catches none of the four. Its
fixtures are single-label with everything live, so a whole-column count coincides with a scanned
count there, and its aggregates sit over hops rather than a bare `Scan`. The kind stays because it
closes a generation gap — `count(prop)` now exists in that generator at all, and raw-vs-optimized
is exactly the question a fast path raises — but the honest statement is that it is shape coverage,
not demonstrated teeth, and the fixture is why.

### A guard that is a contract, not a branch

The dotted-key exclusion (`key.contains('.')`) protects a real wrong answer: `store.column("m.k")`
is `None` because the column is `m`, and the absent-column rule would count a record field as ZERO.
But no query reaches it — verified by removing the guard and watching `count(n.m.k)` still answer
correctly in both engines, so a dotted access does not lower to `Expr::Prop` in an aggregate
argument today.

Item 78 deleted an unreachable guard for exactly this reason. This one is kept instead, because
deleting it leaves a latent wrong answer behind a lowering change rather than merely an untested
line — so it is pinned by a DIRECT unit test on the arm, which is the only thing that can reach it.
That test also asserts the plain key next to it still answers, so it cannot pass by declining
everything.

## 80. A CALL body's element predicate was applied above its hop (2026-10-01)

The differential fuzzer's long-standing intermittent failure, finally localized. It presents as
`CALL (n) { MATCH (n WHERE pred)-[:E]->(m) RETURN m.n AS mn }` where TS raises `E_INVALID_VALUE`
and native answers — and because the fuzzer is random-seeded, CI failed only on a seed that drew
that shape, which is why four clean `bun run fuzz` runs in a row missed it.

Reducing it gave the whole diagnosis in one table. The same pattern, four spellings:

|                                                 | TS     | native      |
| ----------------------------------------------- | ------ | ----------- |
| `CALL (n) { MATCH (n WHERE pred)-[:E]->(m) … }` | raises | **answers** |
| `MATCH (n:T) MATCH (n WHERE pred)-[:E]->(m) …`  | raises | raises      |
| `MATCH (n WHERE pred)-[:E]->(m) …`              | raises | raises      |
| `MATCH (n:T) WHERE pred …`                      | raises | raises      |

Only the CALL-body spelling diverged, so this was never about `CALL` correlation or the executor —
it was the lowering of the body's START node. The plan says it outright:

```text
CallInline { input: Scan{T}, body: Filter { input: Expand { input: Row, … }, pred } }
```

`gql.rs`'s `call_arm` parsed the element-pattern `WHERE` from a captured token range AFTER
`extend_chain` had built the hop, because the predicate has to resolve against the SUBQUERY scope
and that scope was not installed until then. So the `Filter` landed ABOVE the `Expand` — and there
an imported row whose hop matched nothing never had its predicate evaluated at all. A predicate
that FAULTS on such a row therefore faulted in TS and not here.

FIXED by parsing it early: the subquery scope is installed for the parse and restored before
`extend_chain` runs (which takes `sub_scope` explicitly and must not see the swap), and the filter
is applied to the start node before the chain hops away from it. `(n WHERE pred)` constrains THAT
ELEMENT, so that is also the reading ISO gives it — and it is the cheaper plan, since the hop no
longer runs for rows the predicate rejects.

### The fixture is the test, and the first one proved nothing

A predicate that faults on EVERY vertex does not discriminate: the vertex WITH an edge reaches a
filter placed either side of the hop, so it faults either way and the bug hides. Verified rather
than assumed — with `(n.k % 0) > 0` on every vertex, reinstating the exact original ordering left
the whole suite green (870 passed).

The discriminating fixture puts the fault on a vertex the hop never yields: three vertices with
`x` of 2, 0 and 5, only `k = 1` carrying an outgoing edge, and `(n.k % n.x) > -1` — which divides
by zero on `k = 2` alone, the vertex with no edge. Reinstating the ordering bug against THAT is
caught by three tests.
