| test                                                                | what it pins                                                                                                                                    |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `a_call_body_element_predicate_is_evaluated_for_every_imported_row` | the fault happens, and with the SAME error the plain spelling gives                                                                             |
| `the_call_body_and_plain_spellings_of_an_element_predicate_agree`   | four predicates, each compared across the two spellings — the equivalent-spellings invariant that failed here                                   |
| `a_fresh_call_body_start_node_also_filters_before_its_hop`          | the OTHER lowering branch — a fresh `(q:P WHERE …)` start node is a correlated `Scan`, not a `Row`, and nothing covered its predicate placement |
| `a_call_body_element_predicate_still_filters`                       | pushing the predicate below the hop did not quietly drop it                                                                                     |

Dropping the predicate entirely is caught by all four plus `gql::tests::inline_where_on_call_subquery_start`.

### Not a registry entry

This was the open candidate for the first declared divergence (item 79) and it would have been the
wrong one. `US008`/`UA004` would arguably have permitted native's behaviour — the predicate's value
on a row the hop discards is an inessential part — so it could have been declared as an
`evaluation-order` entry and closed. It was a lowering bug with a cheaper correct plan, and
declaring it would have hidden that. The registry's own test asserts this exact case stays
uncovered, with the failing seed as a literal.

## 79. A registry for declared divergences between the engines (2026-10-01)

Byte-identity is the development strategy that has caught most of this engine's wrong answers —
two independent implementations of one spec, compared on generated input — but it is not a product
requirement. The engines have genuinely different capabilities: the Rust core is 1.5-4x faster on
most shapes and ~250x faster per path on the var-length walker (measured in item 78), it can take
parallelism a single-threaded JavaScript host cannot, and it does not block an event loop. Holding
one back to match the other buys nothing in those places.

`packages/native/src/divergence-registry.ts` is where those places are written down. The reason to
write them down is what it replaces: an `if` buried in `differential-fuzz.test.ts` with a
thirty-line comment, which nothing audited and nothing would notice ceasing to be true.

### The axes, and the one that does not exist

An entry names an AXIS. Three of the four are things ISO/IEC 39075 explicitly leaves to the
implementation, which is why they are the axes rather than a list of convenience:

| axis               | basis                                                                                                                               |
| ------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `resource`         | which queries hit a limit — the engines pay different amounts for the same work                                                     |
| `evaluation-order` | whether an exception is raised from an INESSENTIAL part of an expression — ISO `US008` (order of expression evaluation) and `UA004` |
| `order`            | the sequence of records in an unordered binding table — ISO `US001`                                                                 |
| `float-reduction`  | the association order of a floating-point reduction, which a parallel sum changes; bounded to the last places                       |

There is deliberately **no `value` axis**, and `accept` refuses a value divergence before the
registry is consulted at all. If the engines disagree about the answer to a determinate question,
that is a bug in one of them and no entry may excuse it. That rule is the thing that stops this
file becoming a way to turn red into green.

### What an entry must be

**Narrow**: `matches` pins the shape AND the magnitude, so small cases still have to agree exactly
and only the pathological tail is excused. An entry that matches a whole operator is a bug being
hidden.

**Monitored**: usage is counted, printed on every fuzzer run (`DECLARED <id>=<n>`), and `unused`
reports an entry that nothing exercised — so an entry cannot outlive the behaviour it describes.

**Not fixable**: being fixable disqualifies an entry, and both recent candidates were fixable. The
var-length count asymmetry looked like a `resource` divergence and was a misaligned guard (item
78). The `CALL`-body case looks like an `evaluation-order` divergence and is a laziness bug. Had
either been declared, the registry would have hidden a defect on its first day.

### The one entry, and why it is the right first one

`boolean-context-dynamic-operand-under-seek`, migrated from the inline `if` rather than newly
excused. Both engines run the same STATIC boolean-context type check, so a statically non-boolean
value in a truth position is rejected by both before execution. One case is irreducible in a
schemaless engine: a dynamically typed operand AND-ed with a comparison that a selective seek
narrows to zero rows, where the row-dependent `as_truth` reject fires on the engine that evaluates
the operand and not on the one whose seek eliminated every row first. `US008`/`UA004` make both
conformant, and fixing it means abandoning the seek.

It is narrow in exactly the way the file demands: ALWAYS `E_INVALID_VALUE` on one side against an
EMPTY result on the other — "malformed predicate" against "no rows", never against rows. Used 23-28
times per 20,000-query run.

### Teeth, which a registry needs more than most code

A registry whose entries are never tested excuses whatever it happens to match. 18 tests, and the
two mutants that matter:

| mutant                                                                                                                    | caught by                                                                                                                                                |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| the unconditional `value` refusal is removed                                                                              | `a plain wrong answer is refused`                                                                                                                        |
| the entry is widened from "against an EMPTY result" to "against any success" — i.e. so it would cover the `CALL`-body bug | `the CALL-body laziness bug is NOT covered by the boolean residual entry` AND `a value divergence is refused even though an entry would match its shape` |

The second is the important one. It carries the real failing case (`FUZZ_SEED=3583567550`) as a
literal, so if a future entry ever starts matching it, the test says that a laziness bug has been
declared as a capability difference.

Also pinned: a multiset comparison that counts duplicates, so a wrong CARDINALITY cannot pass as a
reordering (`[{x:1},{x:1}]` against `[{x:1}]` is a value difference, not an `order` one); a numeric
difference beyond the ulp tolerance is a value difference; two different error codes is a value
difference, not a capability one.

### Wiring

`differential-fuzz.test.ts` routes BOTH divergence branches through `accept` — the one-sided case
and the both-succeeded case — so an `order` or `float-reduction` entry would apply there if one is
ever declared. With none, those classify and report exactly as before, now with the observed axis
named in the report (`(evaluation-order: no registry entry covers this shape)`), which is how the
`CALL` bug now presents.

The conformance suite is NOT wired yet: its 199 cases are hand-written `expect`s rather than a
divergence loop, so there is no single site to route. That is the next step if an entry is ever
declared that a deterministic case would exercise — and it is the point of the `deterministic` flag
on `Entry`, which lets `unused` treat an unexercised entry as a failure rather than a warning.

## 78. The two engines' trail budgets were never the same quantity (2026-10-01)

Item 50's fix (the group-count shortcut) made the conformance suite fail, and running the CI job
list — not `bun run fuzz`, which does not include it — found it after 23 commits. The failing case
is `gql-conformance.test.ts`, "dense multi-element unit expansion faults": on a 64-vertex complete
digraph, `MATCH (s:N) ((a)-[:R]->(b)-[:R]->(c)-[:R]->(d)-[:R]->(e)){1} (t) RETURN count(*)` — TS
raises `E_RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`, native answered 1,007,435,520 in 6.5 seconds.

The answer was CORRECT (brute-forced against K4/K5/K6: 240/1080/3360, which native matches), so this
was never a wrong answer. It was native answering a count TS refuses, which is the one thing the
byte-identity rule forbids: the engines must not disagree about WHICH queries raise.

### The root cause is older than the commit that exposed it

`limits.trail` governs a DIFFERENT QUANTITY in each engine, and a comment in `varlen.rs` claimed
they were the same ("the TS engine's guard, same default"):

|        | quantity                                                                                      |   default |
| ------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------: |
| native | total emitted ROWS, across all sources                                                        | 1,000,000 |
| TS     | per-SOURCE hop STEPS (`steps` is declared inside `trailEndsUnit`, which runs once per source) | 1,000,000 |

Same number, different thing — so they diverged in BOTH directions, and both were measured:

|                                                   | TS                                | native before item 50 |
| ------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------- | --------------------- |
| random degree-3, 20,000 vertices, 1,796,508 paths | answers (3.3 s, ~90 steps/source) | threw                 |
| K64, 1,007,435,520 paths from 64 sources          | throws (15.7M steps/source)       | threw                 |

Item 50 removed the row cap from the count path, which fixed the first row and opened the second.
The idea was right — a bare count materializes nothing, so a MATERIALIZATION guard has no business
bounding it — but it removed the wrong guard instead of replacing it with the right one.

### Fixed by counting the quantity TS counts

`VarlenEmit` gains `start_seed` and `note_step`; `StepBudget` is a per-source hop counter against
`limits.trail`, carried by both the counting sink and the materializing one. The hop is counted at
the points TS counts it — after the edge-label filter and the mark-collision test, and INCLUDING a
closing hop and a hop whose per-repetition `WHERE` failed, because TS's `steps += 1` sits below
`hopCollides` and below the `WHERE` (which suppresses the emit, not the step).

The counts are EQUAL rather than close, which is the point: the two walkers explore the same tree in
the same order (`trailEndsUnit`'s own comment says "Mirrors native `reachable_each_unit`"), so
counting at the mirrored point makes the threshold decide the same way. An approximate alignment
would have created fresh divergences at the boundary, on exactly the axis that must not drift.

The count fast path DECLINES when the budget blows, rather than raising: the `try_*_count` chain is
`Option<Batch>` and has no error channel. Falling through to the materializing walk, which counts
the same hops at the same points, raises from the channel that does. It costs the blown source's
budget twice — bounded by `limits.trail`, and only on a query that is about to fail.

|                                                    |                                                                                                      |
| -------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| conformance test                                   | **passes**, and in 2.04 s rather than 6.6 s — native now declines instead of walking a billion paths |
| `a_group_count_is_not_bounded_by_the_trail_budget` | still passes; the sparse win from item 50 is kept                                                    |

### What it costs, measured

One increment per hop on the hottest recursive path in the engine. Min of three runs each way:

| bench row              |    before |     after |       |
| ---------------------- | --------: | --------: | ----: |
| `varlen control`       |  15,506us |  15,876us | +2.4% |
| `repeat-group control` |  66,410us |  68,570us | +3.3% |
| `k2-group count`       |  56,027us |  57,777us | +3.1% |
| `repeat-group count`   |     875us |     895us | +2.3% |
| `nested-group count`   | 149,640us | 150,013us | +0.2% |

Consistently positive across all five, so it is real rather than noise, and ~3% is the honest price
of the guard. It buys back 6.5 s on the query that should never have run.

### Teeth

- `a_group_count_raises_when_one_source_exceeds_the_hop_budget` — the budget applies at all.
  Removing the increment entirely is caught by this and nothing else (865 of 866 passed).
- `the_hop_budget_is_per_source_not_per_query` — many cheap sources must answer even though the
  query's TOTAL hop count is far over the limit. A global counter is caught by this plus
  `a_group_count_is_not_bounded_by_the_trail_budget` and
  `an_endpoint_filtered_count_is_not_bounded_by_the_trail_budget`.

### Still open, and NOT this

The differential fuzzer has a pre-existing, intermittent failure on the `CALL`-body shape
(`CALL (n) { MATCH (n WHERE pred)-[:E]->(m) … }` — TS raises `E_INVALID_VALUE`, native answers,
because native evaluates the imported filter only for rows whose hop matched). Verified pre-existing
by reproducing it with `FUZZ_SEED=3583567550` against this item's change REVERTED. Because the
fuzzer is random-seeded, CI fails only on a seed that reaches that shape, which is why four clean
`bun run fuzz` runs missed it. It is the same CLASS as this item — a laziness difference that
changes which queries raise — so it wants fixing, not accepting.

## 77. Boxing a cell to ask whether it was NULL (2026-10-01)

Found by scanning the bench corpus for an ASYMMETRY rather than for a big number. `query/gremlin`
had `values` at 1,378.9us just to COUNT the values of a property over 200,000 vertices, against
`V count` at 0.2us, so the GQL twin got priced:

|                                                |           |
| ---------------------------------------------- | --------: |
| `RETURN count(p.name)` — a `Dict`/`Str` column | 1,048.4us |
| `RETURN count(p.age)` — a `Num` column         |   112.2us |
| `RETURN count(*)`                              |     0.3us |

**10x between two rows that ask the same question of the same vertices.** `AggFn::Count` tested
`!col.value_at(i).is_null()`, and `value_at` BOXES the cell: `Col::Str(v) => Value::Str(v[i].clone())`
is an `Arc` clone per row, while `Col::Num(v) => Value::Num(v[i])` is a register move. The whole
cost was constructing a value in order to throw it away.

FIXED with `Col::is_null_at`, which asks the representation. The case analysis IS the proof that it
is exactly `value_at(i).is_null()`:

```rust
pub fn is_null_at(&self, i: usize) -> bool {
    match self {
        Self::Nodes(v) | Self::Edges(v) => v[i] == u32::MAX,
        Self::Num(_) | Self::Bool(_) | Self::Str(_) => false,
        Self::Gen(v) => v[i].is_null(),
    }
}
```

`Num`, `Bool` and `Str` have NO null representation — a column that would need one is built as
`Gen`, since `Col` has no nullable typed variant — so only the `u32::MAX` OPTIONAL-MATCH sentinel
and a boxed `Value::Null` can be null. A `Num` cell holding NaN is NOT null, matching `value_at`,
and `count()` has always counted it.

Three call sites, all of which asked the boxed question: `AggFn::Count`, `AggFn::Count` under
`DISTINCT` (where the cheap test now comes first, so an all-null group never boxes), and the
evaluator's `IS NULL` arm.

|                                   |    before |       after |           |
| --------------------------------- | --------: | ----------: | --------: |
| bench `count(prop) str`           | 1,048.4us | **853.7us** |     1.23x |
| bench `count(prop) num` (control) |   112.2us |     111.2us |         — |
| bench `project is null str`       | 1,367.1us | **844.1us** |     1.62x |
| bench `project is null num`       | 1,220.6us | **594.1us** | **2.05x** |

Min of two runs each way. The numeric `IS NULL` row gains MORE than the string one, which looks
backwards and is not: a `Num` column cannot be NULL at all, so the answer is a constant the loop no
longer has to index for, while the string case still walks the column.

What remains in `count(prop) str` is the column MATERIALIZATION — `read_property` builds the whole
`Col::Str` before the aggregate runs — and that is a separate question: a `count` of a plain `Prop`
need not materialize anything, since the answer is the number of present-and-not-null cells among
the batch's ids. Its own item.

### Teeth

| test                                                            | the mutant                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| --------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `is_null_at_agrees_with_boxing_the_value_for_every_column_kind` | any arm disagreeing with `value_at(i).is_null()` — checked cell by cell over one column of each kind. Catches `Num` treating NaN as NULL (nothing else in the suite does), the sentinel arm, and the `Gen` arm |
| `count_of_a_property_counts_present_cells_only`                 | a present-NULL being counted, or an absent cell being counted — over a property on every row, one absent on one row, a stored present-null, and a key nobody carries                                           |
| `is_null_reads_the_same_cells_count_skips`                      | the same cells through `IS NULL`/`IS NOT NULL`, plus the OPTIONAL-MATCH miss, where the unmatched element IS null rather than FALSE                                                                            |

Mutants run: NaN-as-NULL (caught by the equivalence test ALONE — 863 of 864 passed), the sentinel
no longer NULL (3 tests), a boxed NULL no longer NULL (10 tests). The first is the one that matters:
without the equivalence test, treating NaN as NULL would have changed what `count()` returns and
passed everything else.

## 76. The element renderers rebuilt three constants per row (2026-10-01)

`RETURN p` is the most idiomatic shape there is and the most expensive row in the whole bench
corpus — 530 ns/row against 2.4 for `RETURN p.age`, a factor of 220 — and nothing in the corpus
said why. Decomposing it over 200,000 vertices:

| piece                                                                             | ns/row |
| --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -----: |
| `labels_of` — a `String` clone per label, sorted, then sorted AGAIN by the caller |   48.0 |
| the property map via `has_prop` + `prop` — two key hashes per key per row         |  141.6 |
| the three field-name `Value`s, built from literals                                |   28.6 |
| `node_ext_id`                                                                     |    1.3 |

The field names were the surprise: `Value::Str("id".into())` allocates, and `{id, labels,
properties}` meant three allocations per node row and five per edge row, for three and five
compile-time constants. 28.6 ns/row, the single largest removable share, and invisible because it
looks like nothing.

FIXED, three ways, at all three renderers (`node_result_value`, the batch `render_nodes`, and
`edge_result_value`):

- `field_key(Field::…)` reads a process-wide `OnceLock<[Value; 5]>`, so a field name is a refcount
  bump: **28.6 → 7.9 ns/row** for three of them.
- `labels_of_refs` borrows each name and sorts once, where `labels_of` cloned a `String` per label
  and sorted and then the caller sorted again: **48.0 → 36.5 ns/row**.
- One key lookup per property instead of two. `has_prop` IS `present_at`, so `present_at`/`read` on
  the resolved column answer exactly what the pair did, present-nulls included:
  **141.6 → 95.7 ns/row** for nodes. For edges `edge_prop_keys()` was worse still — it allocated a
  `Vec<String>` and sorted it PER ROW, and the result was then sorted a second time; the new
  `Store::edge_prop_keys_arc` is the cached `Arc<str>` twin of `prop_keys_arc`:
  **80.4 → 59.2 ns/row**.

|                                      |       before |            after |       |
| ------------------------------------ | -----------: | ---------------: | ----: |
| harness `node_result_value`          |     273.6 ns |     **237.3 ns** | 1.15x |
| harness `edge_result_value`          |     267.4 ns |     **207.9 ns** | 1.29x |
| harness `render_nodes` (batch)       |     311.6 ns |     **273.2 ns** | 1.14x |
| bench `element p` (200,000 vertices) | 538.3 ns/row | **493.7 ns/row** | 1.09x |
| bench `element r` (1,000,000 edges)  | 392.9 ns/row | **346.6 ns/row** | 1.13x |

Min of three runs on both sides, because `render_nodes` ranged 311.6 / 627.1 / 629.3 ns on ONE
binary — it allocates a 200,000-`Value` output vector and the allocator's state dominates. That row
is the noisy one; the other two are steady to a few percent.

### `element_render_cost` is a kept harness, and the reason is the noise floor

The query-level gains are 9% and 13%, which is at this repo's ~10% noise floor, so a bench row
could not decide them. `render_nodes` and `node_result_value` are `pub(super)` and an example can
only reach them through a whole query, so the harness is a `#[ignore]`d test — same arrangement as
items 70 and 72.

Two things it cost to get right, both the harness's own bugs:

- **`.map(…).count()` measures nothing.** Clippy caught it (`map()` won't have an effect on
  `count()`): the closure's result is dropped and the work can be optimized away. The property rows
  now `collect()` into the `Vec` the renderer actually builds — which moved `has_prop + prop` from
  95.0 to 141.6 ns/row, so the earlier number was measuring a fraction of the real cost.
- **The edge rows get their OWN store.** Adding edge properties to the node fixture moved
  `render_nodes` from 270 to 694 ns/row without touching a line of it: a bigger heap makes its
  output allocation cost more. Two fixtures, two sections.

### Teeth, and a real gap it closed

| test                                                                  | the mutant                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |
| --------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| `a_node_element_map_omits_absent_properties_and_keeps_present_nulls`  | the node property map not filtering on presence, so an absent key renders as `{k: null}` instead of being omitted. **Caught by nothing before this** — the whole Rust suite passed with the filter removed, and the shape is a byte-identity break with the TS engine                                                          |
| `an_edge_element_map_omits_absent_properties_and_keeps_present_nulls` | the same for edges (also caught by `gremlin::tests::fused_edge_map_json_matches_value_tree_bytes`)                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| `the_batch_and_per_node_element_renderers_agree`                      | the batch renderer's labels coming back unsorted. Two implementations of one map — a bare node frontier takes the batch path while a `Gen` cell, a path and the JSON sinks take the per-node one — and nothing pinned them against each other, including the OPTIONAL-MATCH null sentinel that only the batch path knows about |
| `the_cached_element_field_names_are_in_the_declared_order`            | `from` and `to` swapped in the cached array, which is now an index rather than a literal at each site (also caught by `gql::tests::return_edge_renders_element_map`)                                                                                                                                                           |

### What is left in `RETURN p`, and why it is not a missing path

After this, `element p` is 493.7 ns/row (98,735us for 200,000 vertices). CORRECTED (item 77's
pass): the renderer accounts for essentially ALL of it, not half — decomposed, `try_run` of
`MATCH (p:Person) RETURN p` is 268.1 ns/row against `render_nodes` alone at 260.6 and the scan at
0.1, so there is no cost hiding in the `Rows` plumbing. The bench row reads higher than the
isolated harness because the bench store also holds 1,000,000 edges: the SAME `render_nodes` over
the SAME vertices costs 286.1 ns/row in a node-only store and 402.4 in that one. The gap is
allocator state under a bigger heap, which is also what made the harness row range 311.6 / 627.1 /
629.3 on one binary.
The rest is three `Arc` allocations per row — the outer map, the property map, the labels list — plus
a boxed `Value` per property. That is the "allocates per row by construction" class, not a missing
path: a typed arm buys dispatch only. Narrowing it means changing what a result row IS, not how it
is built.

## 75. Two spellings of one empty predicate differed by 570x (2026-10-01)

Item 74's first attempt at a bench guard was `MATCH (p:Person) WHERE p.no_such_key = 5 RETURN
p.name`, and it measured 7,452us against 7,449us — dead flat, with the new arm never called. Both
facts turned out to be about a different path, and that path is this item.

The planner lowers an equality on a property to a `Plan::IndexSeek`. Without an index the seek
degrades to a scan, which has typed fast paths for a plain key — `Str`/`Dict` against a string,
`Num` against a number, `Bool` against a bool — and a boxed tail for everything else:
`store.prop_path(id, key)` per row, building a `Value`, then `value::equals`. Over 200,000 vertices
that tail is 37 ns a row, and it is reached for exactly the cases where no row can match:

| query                                    |  before |              and the `Filter` spelling |
| ---------------------------------------- | ------: | -------------------------------------: |
| `WHERE p.no_such_key = 5`                | 7,328us | `WHERE p.no_such_key > 5` → **12.9us** |
| `WHERE p.age = 'fifty'` (a `Num` column) | 7,427us |                                      — |

**570x between two spellings of the same question**, both returning 0 rows — the exact shape the
spelling-equivalence section of `CLAUDE.md` is about, and found the same way: not by suspecting it,
but because a bench row that should have moved did not, and asking why.

`try_filter_keep` has carried half the answer since before the typed masks existed:

```rust
let Some(column) = store.column(key) else {
    return Some(Vec::new()); // property absent everywhere → UNKNOWN → all dropped
};
```

The seek's fallback never got it. FIXED by asking the store's own schema, before reading a vertex:

```rust
match store.column(key) {
    None => return Vec::new(),
    Some(col) if !col.accepts(value) => return Vec::new(),
    Some(_) => {}
}
```

Two cases, both decided by the schema. No column at all means nobody carries the key, so every cell
reads NULL and `value` is non-null here. A still-TYPED column of another type cannot hold `value`
either: writing a value of a different type promotes the column to `Gen` (`Column::set`), so a
column that is still `Num` holds only numbers, and `value::equals` is false across types.
`Column::accepts` is already exactly that question and is now `pub(crate)` — and it says YES for
`Gen` and for a same-kind `Temporal`, which are precisely the columns the typed arms do not cover
and which must stay on the boxed path.

|                                                 |  before |     after |
| ----------------------------------------------- | ------: | --------: |
| bench `pred absent key`                         | 7,328us | **0.2us** |
| bench `seek type mismatch`                      | 7,427us | **0.2us** |
| bench `seek absent range` (control, a `Filter`) |  12.9us |    14.2us |

The seek is now FASTER than the filter spelling, because it answers from the schema and never
builds the 200,000-row scan the filter still has to discard.

### Teeth

Two tests, and the point of both is the shapes that must STILL be scanned:

- `a_seek_with_no_index_skips_the_scan_only_when_the_schema_decides_it` — the five decided-empty
  forms, and then the four that must still find their row: a same-kind `Temporal`, both types in a
  `Gen` column (mixed writes), and a DOTTED path, which has no column of its own.
- `an_indexed_seek_and_an_unindexed_one_agree_on_a_type_mismatch` — the same predicates with an
  index on the key, so the seek takes `index_lookup` instead of the fallback. The
  equivalent-spellings invariant applies to a predicate an index merely makes faster.

| mutant                                                                    | caught by                                                                 |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| the guard returns empty for EVERY column (`Some(_) => return Vec::new()`) | both new tests and 10 others                                              |
| the guard no longer excludes a dotted path                                | the new test and `opt::tests::dotted_field_eq_seeds_index_both_spellings` |

### What was reverted, and why

`range_seek_ids` has the same boxed tail and the same rule would apply to it — a plain key with no
column is UNKNOWN on every row. The guard is NOT there. Mutating it in and then removing its dotted
exclusion left the entire suite green (857 tests), which is what unreachable looks like: the planner
emits a `RangeSeek` only where a range index can serve it, so a range on an absent key stays a
`Filter`, and that is why the control row costs 12.9us rather than 7,000. An unmeasurable,
untestable guard is worse than none, so the reason is recorded at the call site instead.
`a_range_seek_on_a_dotted_path_is_not_mistaken_for_an_absent_key` stays — it pins the dotted
behaviour with and without a range index either way, which nothing covered for a range op.

## 74. A key no vertex carries, and a property only some of them carry (2026-10-01)

Item 72's cost table left two rows standing, both flagged there as the best remaining targets
because neither is an allocation problem — they are missing paths:

| kind                  | ns/row | against                                    |
| --------------------- | -----: | ------------------------------------------ |
| `Prop (half-present)` |   7.36 | 1.05 for a property present on every row   |
| `Compare absent/lit`  |  10.20 | 0.90 for the same compare on a present key |

Diagnosing the first: the STORE column stays typed, so the cliff is not a de-opt. `Col` has no
nullable typed variant, so `read_property`'s typed `gather` bails on the first absent cell and
falls to its boxed `general` closure — and that closure called `store.prop(id, key)`, which
re-resolves `key` through a hash map FOR EVERY ROW. The already-resolved `column` was sitting in
scope. The gather is not the difference; the key lookup was the whole cliff.

The second is the same question seen through a comparison: `store.column(key)` is `None` when NO
vertex carries the key, so `typed_num_mask` / `typed_str_mask` declined, both sides were boxed, and
the generic compare walked a column of NULLs one cell at a time. The answer needs no column at
all — a key no vertex carries is UNKNOWN on every row.

FIXED, both:

```rust
// read_property's boxed fallback
let general = || Col::Gen(ids.iter().map(|&i| column.read(i as usize)).collect());

// typed_num_mask / typed_str_mask
if store.column(key).is_none() {
    return Some(vec![None; ids.len()]);
}
```

|                              |   before |       after |       |
| ---------------------------- | -------: | ----------: | ----: |
| harness `Prop (half)`        |  7.36 ns | **4.70 ns** | 1.57x |
| harness `Compare absent/lit` | 10.20 ns | **0.41 ns** |   25x |
| bench `project absent cmp`   |  4,152us |   **819us** | 5.07x |

### The bench row has to be a PROJECTION, and finding that out was the work

The obvious guard is `MATCH (p:Person) WHERE p.no_such_key = 5 RETURN p.name`, and it measured
7,452us against 7,449us — dead flat. Instrumenting the new arm across the whole bench group gave
ZERO calls, which is the same answer item 71's `fold_operand` instrumentation gave, for a different
reason each time. Two reasons here:

1. The `WHERE` spelling never reaches the masks. `try_filter_keep` has carried this exact rule for
   a plain `k <op> lit` since before they existed — `let Some(column) = store.column(key) else {
return Some(Vec::new()) }`, commented "property absent everywhere → UNKNOWN → all dropped". The
   masks are reached from a PROJECTION, where `eval` routes its comparison through them (item 72),
   and from the predicate shapes `try_filter_keep` declines.
2. That query's 7,452us is a DIFFERENT cliff, on a path neither of these changes touches — the
   plan is an `IndexSeek` on a key with no index, and its fallback scan boxes every row. Its own
   item.

So the guard is `RETURN p.no_such_key = 5 AS f`, and it is a real one: 5.07x.

### Teeth

Three focused tests, and all three catch mutants that the previous suite missed:

| test                                                      | the mutant                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| --------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `a_comparison_against_an_absent_key_is_unknown_not_false` | the numeric mask answering `Some(false)` instead of `None`. Only visible through `NOT`: an UNKNOWN stays UNKNOWN under negation and the row still drops, while a FALSE flips to TRUE and every row survives |
| `an_absent_key_comparison_projects_null`                  | the same, and the STRING mask answering `Some(true)` — which the `WHERE` side cannot see at all, since `try_filter_keep` absorbs it before the mask runs                                                    |
| `a_half_present_property_reads_its_own_values_and_null`   | the boxed fallback reading the wrong row (`i + 1`), over both a numeric and a string half-present column                                                                                                    |

The first two mutants were caught by the differential fuzzer on the previous pass and by NOTHING in
the Rust suite (851 passed, twice). They are caught by the Rust suite now, which is where a
three-valued rule this small belongs; the fuzzer stays as the general oracle. `genPred` and the
cross-slot arm both gained a nonexistent key `zz` for that, since every key the fuzzer used existed.

### Still priced, for the next pass

`Cast to STRING` 92.11 ns/row, `Call upper` 40.70, `Call contains` 32.46, `List [p,p]` 29.51 — all
allocate per row by construction, so a typed arm buys dispatch only. The `IndexSeek`-without-an-index
scan found above is the live one.

## 73. Property against property had no typed comparison path (2026-10-01)

Item 72's cost table flagged `Compare Num/Num` at 11.19 ns a row, but measured it with one slot and
one key, which is degenerate. The shape a query actually writes is `WHERE a.x < b.y` across two
bound variables, so the harness grew a cross-slot section over a two-slot batch from a hop:

| predicate         |   `eval` | `eval_mask` |                              |
| ----------------- | -------: | ----------: | ---------------------------- |
| `a.num < b.num`   |  8.62 ns |     8.65 ns | no typed path on EITHER side |
| `a.num < lit`     |  1.26 ns |     0.89 ns | typed                        |
| `a.name < b.name` | 19.10 ns |    19.14 ns | no typed path                |

`typed_num_mask` and `typed_str_mask` matched `Prop` against `Lit` only, so a comparison between two
properties declined and both evaluators boxed each side. The two evaluators costing the SAME is the
tell: item 72's gap was one evaluator missing a path, and this is both missing it.

FIXED with `typed_num_prop_mask` and `typed_str_prop_mask`. Each resolves its two sides
independently — different slots, different keys, and for numbers one side a node and the other an
edge — and is three-valued exactly as the boxed path: a NULL element (`u32::MAX`) or an absent cell
on EITHER side makes the row UNKNOWN. A `Dict` column is read through its dictionary rather than by
code, because codes are only comparable within one column and the two sides may have unrelated
dictionaries; the literal path keeps its code-compare.

|                                    |   before |        after |       |
| ---------------------------------- | -------: | -----------: | ----: |
| harness `a.num < b.num` (`eval`)   |  8.62 ns |  **2.06 ns** |  4.2x |
| harness `a.name < b.name` (`eval`) | 19.10 ns |  **5.27 ns** |  3.6x |
| bench `cmp prop/prop`              | 20,518us | **11,785us** | 1.74x |
| bench `cmp str prop/prop`          | 39,360us | **21,084us** | 1.87x |
| bench `cmp prop/lit` (control)     |  6,048us |      6,108us |     — |

The bench rows are lower than the harness because they also pay for the hop and for projecting
~496,000 rows: the predicate is a minority of the query. Both are real — the harness prices the
expression, the bench prices a query containing one.

### The oracle had to be extended first, and that was the work

A new comparison path with no oracle coverage is the class that produced items 64 through 68. Before
trusting these: `rewrite_fuzz` generates **zero** property-against-property comparisons, and the
differential fuzzer had exactly one (`WHERE m.n <> x.n`, inside a per-repetition arm) — plus
`genPred`'s `(n.n <op> n.x)`, which covers the dense same-slot numeric case and nothing else.

Three additions, and each is justified by a mutant it alone catches:

| addition                                                                                 | the mutant it catches                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `(n.nan <op> n.n)` in `genPred` — `nan` is present on two vertices only                  | the numeric path ignoring the RIGHT side's presence: `ts: null` against `native: false`                                                                                                                                                |
| a CROSS-SLOT arm, `MATCH (a:T)-[:E]->(b:T) WHERE (a.k <op> b.k2)` and the projected form | reading the two slots SWAPPED. Every `genPred` arm reads ONE variable, so a same-slot comparison was the most it could reach, while the masks resolve each side's slot independently                                                   |
| **a sparse STRING property** (`st`, on vertices 1 and 3)                                 | the string path returning FALSE instead of UNKNOWN for an absent right side. `nan` plays that role for numbers; without a string twin this mutant passed the entire suite, because every other string property here is on every vertex |

None of the three mutants is caught by the Rust suite — all three are three-valued questions that
only the cross-engine oracle can see. Coverage pair for the new arm: `cross=576-640 / 378-428` of
20,000, floors at 250/120.

### What the widening reached, and why it is not in the tree

Adding the matching kind to `rewrite_fuzz` was tried and REVERTED, with the reason recorded next to
`KIND_COUNT`. An extra kind reshuffles the whole RNG stream, and seed 523781 then produces
`Project{ Distinct{ Project{ VarLength … } } }` whose outer projection reads `path_length(p)`. For
one endpoint the hop has several paths of different lengths, `Distinct` keeps one of them, and WHICH
one depends on traversal order — which the optimized plan changes by seeding from a `RangeSeek`. ISO
makes that unspecified (`US001`, the sequence of records in an unordered binding table), so the plan
has no determined answer and the comparison is asking the wrong question.

Verified pre-existing rather than assumed: it fails with this item's engine change reverted. Fixing
it means teaching the generator not to read a path accessor above a `Distinct`, or teaching
`ordered_output` that such a plan is undetermined — its own item. The kind stays out rather than
leaving the sweep red, and the prop-vs-prop coverage comes from the differential fuzzer, which is
the stronger oracle for three-valued semantics anyway.

### Still priced, for the next pass

Unchanged by this work: `Cast to STRING` 92.11 ns/row, `Call upper` 40.70, `Call contains` 32.46,
`List [p,p]` 29.51 — all allocate per row by construction, so a typed arm buys dispatch only.
`Prop (half-present)` 7.36 against 1.05 for a fully-present property is the remaining MISSING-PATH
candidate, and the one worth diagnosing first: a 7x cliff on sparse properties, which are the norm
in real graphs. `Compare absent/lit` at 10.20 is probably the same cause seen through a comparison.

## 72. Two evaluators answered the same question and only one had been taught (2026-10-01)

`expression_cost_by_kind` prices every `Expr` variant per row, which is the method that paid off on
the retired core engine — enumerate the enum's variants against the arms that dispatch on them, and
price each so the next target is chosen rather than guessed. `eval` has an arm for all 35 variants
here, so unlike that engine nothing silently falls back; the question is which arms are expensive.

It immediately found that `eval` and `eval_mask`, which both answer "does this row pass", differ by
an order of magnitude over 200,000 rows:

| predicate         |   `eval` | `eval_mask` |       gap |
| ----------------- | -------: | ----------: | --------: |
| `Compare Num/lit` |  7.93 ns |     0.90 ns |  **8.8x** |
| `Compare Str/lit` | 15.68 ns |     3.17 ns |      4.9x |
| `And(Cmp,Cmp)`    | 21.88 ns |     1.78 ns | **12.3x** |
| `IsLabeled`       |  0.51 ns |     0.52 ns |       1.0 |
| `PropertyExists`  |  5.39 ns |     5.41 ns |       1.0 |

`eval_mask` carries `typed_num_mask` / `typed_str_mask` / `typed_strsearch_mask`; `eval`'s `Compare`
arm boxed both sides and called the generic `compare`, and its `And`/`Or`/`Xor`/`Not` arms went
through `zip_bool`, which called `eval` on each operand. The gap was invisible because both are
correct.

FIXED by routing. `eval`'s boolean connectives delegate the whole expression to `eval_mask` and
convert with `truth_to_col` — which yields the same representation `zip_bool` did, `Col::Bool` when
every row is decided and `Col::Gen` with NULLs otherwise. `Compare` may NOT delegate: `eval_mask`'s
fallback is `eval`, so a comparison would recurse forever. It tries the same typed masks inline and
keeps the boxed path as its own fallback. `zip_bool` and `map_bool` are now dead and deleted — the
untyped, non-narrowing boolean path is gone.

|                          |   before |       after |       |
| ------------------------ | -------: | ----------: | ----: |
| `eval` Compare Num/lit   |  7.93 ns | **1.37 ns** |  5.8x |
| `eval` Compare Str/lit   | 15.68 ns | **3.85 ns** |  4.1x |
| `eval` And(Cmp,Cmp)      | 21.88 ns | **2.33 ns** |  9.4x |
| bench `project bool`     |  1,627us |   **559us** | 2.91x |
| bench `project bool AND` |  3,336us |   **920us** | 3.63x |

The two bench rows are the standing guard: a pure evaluator speedup breaks no test, and mutation
confirms it — removing the typed numeric path from `eval` again leaves the whole suite green.

### The latent bug this exposed in item 71

The differential fuzzer failed on `RETURN ((NOT (3 IS UNKNOWN)) OR 'nan')`: TS raised
`E_INVALID_VALUE`, native answered. The first operand is true on every row, so `fold_operand`
narrowed the second to a ZERO-ROW batch, `eval` short-circuits on an empty batch, and the boolean
coercion of `'nan'` never ran and never faulted.

Item 71's transparency check reasoned about one hazard and there are two:

1. **Evaluation** can raise — arithmetic, a call, a CAST. That was handled.
2. **Boolean coercion** can raise independently. An `AND`/`OR` operand goes through `as_truth`,
   which faults on a non-boolean, so a `Lit` or a `Prop` as a DIRECT operand is not narrowable even
   though evaluating it is perfectly safe.

So the top-level operand must now be something that definitely YIELDS a boolean — a comparison, a
label or property test, an `IS NULL`, an `EXISTS`, or a combination of those — and only the inside
of a comparison may be a plain read. `operand_cannot_raise` is that inner whitelist.

The hole was in the tree from item 71 and was most likely unreachable then: `eval_mask` is reached
from `filter_keep`, and a non-boolean predicate in a `WHERE` is rejected statically. Routing
`eval`'s boolean arms through the same code made it reachable from a PROJECTION, where there is no
such check. Pinned by
`a_non_boolean_operand_still_faults_when_the_other_decides_every_row` over both the `OR`/true and
`AND`/false shapes, and `a_comparison_and_an_exists_remain_narrowable` pins that the tightening did
not buy correctness by switching the optimization off.

### Still priced, for the next pass

From the same table, unchanged by this work and in cost order:

| kind                  | ns/row | note                                                                           |
| --------------------- | -----: | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| `Cast to STRING`      |  92.11 | formats a number per row                                                       |
| `In (literal list)`   |  45.99 | the optimizer desugars a literal list to an OR-chain, so likely unreachable    |
| `Call upper`          |  40.70 | allocates per row                                                              |
| `Call contains`       |  32.46 |                                                                                |
| `List [p,p]`          |  29.51 | allocates a `Vec` per row                                                      |
| `Compare Num/Num`     |  11.19 | **prop-vs-prop has no typed path** — the typed masks take prop-vs-literal only |
| `Compare absent/lit`  |  10.20 | an absent property falls off the typed path                                    |
| `Prop (half-present)` |   7.36 | a property on half the rows costs 7x one on all of them                        |

`Compare Num/Num` and `Prop (half-present)` look like the best next targets: both are common shapes
(`WHERE a.x < b.y`, and sparse properties are the norm in real graphs), both are 7-11x a plain
typed read, and neither is an allocation problem — they are missing paths, which is the kind that
has paid every time in this run.

## 71. Narrowing an AND/OR's second operand to the rows the first left undecided (2026-09-30)

`eval_mask`'s `And`/`Or` arms evaluated both operands over every row, always. `fold_operand` now
evaluates the second over only the rows the first left undecided — `false` settles an `AND`, `true`
settles an `OR`, and `UNKNOWN` settles neither.

Three measured results, and they are three different numbers for three different questions.

**The isolated harness** (`predicate_elimination_floor`, item 70), second operand a correlated
subquery that walks the whole adjacency:

| size                |                  6% surviving |                   29% |                   53% | 100% (control) |
| ------------------- | ----------------------------: | --------------------: | --------------------: | -------------: |
| 10,000 / degree 2   |      296 -> **30.0us** (9.9x) |   291 -> 103.8 (2.8x) |   295 -> 170.5 (1.7x) |   296 -> 292.7 |
| 100,000 / degree 2  |     2,951 -> **326us** (9.0x) | 2,970 -> 1,034 (2.9x) | 2,974 -> 1,710 (1.7x) |      unchanged |
| 100,000 / degree 16 | 18,716 -> **1,268us** (14.8x) |                     — |                     — |              — |

**A real query**, A/B against the same binary without the narrowing — `pred AND exists` on the
200,000-vertex bench fixture: **1,030us -> 551us, 1.87x**. Lower than the harness because that
`EXISTS { (p)-[:KNOWS]->() }` stops at the first neighbour, so it is close to a degree check. The
gain scales with what the subquery costs per row; both numbers are real.

**And a shape that does not change at all**: `two preds`, a conjunction of two plain compares,
225.8us -> 226.0us. Which is the most useful of the three results.

### Where it fires, which is not where I expected

Instrumenting `fold_operand` across the whole `query_bench` gave **zero calls**. An `AND` of two
compares never reaches `eval_mask` — the filter fast paths (`try_filter_keep_dict` and the rest)
absorb it, and a two-slot conjunction is split into separate `Filter`s by pushdown, which already
narrows by gathering between them. Probing nine realistic shapes:

| shape                           | reaches `eval_mask` | narrows |
| ------------------------------- | ------------------- | ------- |
| `AND` of two compares, one slot | no                  | —       |
| two-slot conjunction over a hop | no (split)          | —       |
| `OR` of two compares            | yes                 | yes     |
| `(A AND B) OR C`                | yes                 | yes     |
| `AND` / `OR` with an `EXISTS`   | yes                 | yes     |

So the first version of this — a whitelist that excluded subqueries — was nearly dead code: the
shapes that reach `eval_mask` are the ones it declined. Admitting `Exists` (when its body cannot
raise) is what made it both reachable and worth 9-15x. The lesson is narrower than "measure first":
measuring the FLOOR said 2.1x was available for a cheap operand, and that was true of `eval_mask`
and irrelevant to the engine, because nothing arrives there with that shape.

### The two safety conditions, and the oracle each needs

Narrowing cannot change a VALUE — Kleene `AND` is false whenever either side is — but it changes
whether a row that would RAISE is ever evaluated, and which slots are read.

- **Transparency.** Only operands that cannot raise are narrowed. ISO permits the alternative
  (US008 / UA004, see `research/iso-39075/`) and the optimizer takes that latitude elsewhere, but
  the TS engine does not narrow and cross-engine byte-identity is a stricter promise than
  conformance. Dropping this check survives the ENTIRE Rust suite and the 200,000-seed sweep, and
  the differential fuzzer catches it on the first run: `ts: err E_INVALID_VALUE` against
  `native: ok` for `FILTER ((replace(null, n.s, 'inf') IS UNKNOWN) OR …)`.
- **Three-valued keep.** `!= Some(settled)`, not `== Some(!settled)`: an UNKNOWN row still needs
  the second operand. Writing it the other way is caught by four `rewrite_fuzz` cases.

Each condition is load-bearing and each needs a DIFFERENT oracle — one is invisible to the row
comparison, the other invisible to the cross-engine one.

### Three things the measurements changed about the design

1. **Decide from a positional sample, not a count.** Counting the whole mask first cost a 10%
   regression on the DECLINED case (a cheap operand at 53% surviving: 32.1us against 35.2us), and
   bailing out of a partial build only halved it. The first 1,024 rows decide, which is O(1) either
   way, deterministic (byte-identity needs that), and wrong only about performance.
2. **Fold in place.** A settled row already holds the combination's answer, so it needs no write
   and the result needs no second allocation. That took the implementation from 32% above the
   harness floor to 1.03x of it, and made even the DECLINED path faster than the original, which
   always built a fresh `Vec` via `collect`.
3. **One threshold cannot serve both operand costs.** At 47% settled, eliminating saves ~1,442us of
   a walked subquery for a ~20us one-column gather, and ~70us of a plain compare for the same
   gather — a win and a small loss. So the bar is 1/16 settled when the operand holds a subquery
   and 1/2 otherwise, which is the only thing known statically about its cost.

### Why narrowing is by SLOT and not by batch

`Batch::gather` copies every column. Measured at 1,000,000 rows keeping 6%: one `Col::Nodes` costs
35us, sixteen cost 2,231us, one `Col::Gen` 159us and sixteen **11,554us** — against a cheap
operand's whole prize of ~1,600us. Narrowing the batch would have turned a 2.1x gain into a 7x
loss. `narrow_to_slots` gathers only the columns the operand reads and gives the rest cheap
same-length placeholders, which is why `slots_read` is a whitelist: a missed slot reads a
placeholder and is a wrong answer, while an over-approximation only costs a copy.

## 70. An isolated harness for the AND/OR elimination question (2026-09-30)

`eval_mask`'s `And`/`Or` arms, and `zip_bool`'s, evaluate BOTH operands over EVERY row, always.
Nothing short-circuits and nothing narrows. The question is what that costs, and three attempts to
size it with a whole-query bench each measured something else:

- an **indexed** cheap conjunct reports "identical plan" at 0.02ms — seek extraction pulls it out
  whichever side it was written on, so **index seeding already does cost-ordering** and that
  spelling measures nothing;
- a **`count(*)`** form hits a count shortcut that bypasses the general filter path, at 0.18ms;
- the row-returning non-seekable form has **identical plans**, so conjunct order is already
  normalized on that fixture.

Which is what the corpus README means by "anything under ~10% needs its own isolated harness": in a
whole query the predicate is a minority of the total.

`predicate_elimination_floor` in `exec/tests.rs` is that harness — `#[ignore]`d and printing a
table, in the shape of `rewrite_fuzz::dump_seed`. It is a TEST rather than an example because the
honest floor includes the GATHER (elimination means building a smaller batch of the survivors and
evaluating the second operand on that), and `eval_mask`, `pull` and `Batch::gather` are all
crate-private — the same wall that left the eval-vs-columnar floor unmeasured in `examples/README.md`.

### What it measures

`both` is what the engine does today. `elim` is the floor: operand A over the batch, then a gather
of the rows where A is not `Some(false)` — `UNKNOWN` settles nothing for an `AND`, so it must stay
— then operand B on those. Min of 7. Sizes straddle the cache transition the README puts between
200k and 1M, and degree is swept because it is what makes an `EXISTS` body expensive.

| B                   |     pass | 10k/d2 | 100k/d2 |  100k/d16 | 1M/d2 |
| ------------------- | -------: | -----: | ------: | --------: | ----: |
| `EXISTS-deep`       |       6% |   9.5x |    9.1x | **14.8x** |  6.1x |
| `EXISTS-deep`       |      29% |   3.0x |    2.9x |      3.3x |  2.8x |
| `EXISTS-deep`       |      53% |   1.8x |    1.8x |      1.9x |  1.8x |
| `EXISTS-deep`       | **100%** |   0.99 |    0.98 |      0.99 |  0.98 |
| `compare` (cheap B) |       6% |   2.1x |    2.1x |      2.1x |  2.1x |
| `compare`           | **100%** |   0.90 |    0.84 |      0.90 |  0.88 |

Three things follow:

1. **The prize is not confined to expensive right-hand sides.** A plain typed compare as B gives
   2.1x at 6% selectivity, at every size — today's `both` runs B over all rows, the floor runs it
   over 6%.
2. **The downside is bounded and measured.** `pass=100%` is the control, where nothing is
   eliminated and the gather is paid for nothing: 1-2% for an expensive B, 10-16% for a cheap one.
3. **No cost model is needed for the elimination half.** The mask for A is already in hand, so
   counting its rejects is free: narrow only when the reject fraction clears a threshold. That is
   deterministic, single-threaded and byte-identical across engines given the same threshold, which
   sidesteps both the duplicated-cost-model problem and the sampling idea. ORDERING is the separate
   lever that does need estimates — an expensive operand placed first is paid in full whatever
   happens next, so cheapest-first is what makes elimination pay.

### Two fixture errors it made first, and the assertion that ends them

The first version used `dense_store`, which sets only `name` — there is no `num`. So the cheap
operand evaluated to UNKNOWN on every row, nothing was ever eliminated, the pass-fraction labels
were fiction, and every ratio came out 1.00 for a reason unrelated to the question. `survivors > 0`
passed precisely BECAUSE everything survived. It also made `t_a` look 14x more expensive than it is
(125us against 9us at 10k), because an absent property routes through the boxed path.

Before that, the "expensive" operand was a bare `EXISTS { (n)-[:R]->() }`, which stops at the first
neighbour — over a degree-2 graph that is a degree check, and it cost LESS per row than the cheap
compare beside it. `EXISTS-deep` wraps an inner predicate that matches nothing, so the whole
adjacency is walked.

The durable fix is neither of those edits: it is that the harness now asserts the MEASURED pass
fraction against the label it printed, so a fixture that does not exercise the stated selectivity
fails loudly instead of reporting 1.00. That is the fourth time in this run a fixture failed to
exercise its claim (items 56, 63, 68), and the first time the check is in the harness itself.

### Open

The gather in this harness runs over a ONE-column batch (a bare scan). `Batch::gather` copies every
column, so the real cost scales with batch width, and the threshold in (3) cannot be set from these
numbers alone. Measuring gather against width is the next step, and if it is steep the answer is to
narrow only the slots the second operand reads — the mini-batch trick `CountSink` already uses — or
to give `eval` a selection vector, which is a larger change.

## 69. Optimizing turned an answer into an error (2026-09-30)

A random differential-fuzz seed disagreed on a `%` inside a `CALL`, and narrowing it found something
larger underneath, in native alone:

```text
MATCH (n:T)-[:E]->(m) WHERE ((2 % n.x) IS NOT TRUE) RETURN m.n
  raw:       2 rows
  optimized: E_INVALID_VALUE: division by zero
```

Three vertices; only the third has `x = 0`, and the third has no outgoing `:E` edge. The hop removes
it — so in the unoptimized plan the division never sees the zero. The per-conjunct pushdown moves
the filter BELOW the hop, where that row still exists.

This is a direct violation of the optimizer's stated contract, and the violation is of a kind no row
comparison can express: `rewrite_fuzz` compares row MULTISETS, and the optimized plan produces no
rows to compare. Its generated predicates only ever compare, so the whole raising class was outside
the generator and the invariant held vacuously over it.

FIXED for pushdown with `can_raise`, consulted by a single `pushable(conjunct, bound)` rule:
arithmetic (a zero divisor or a non-numeric operand), a function call (the numeric and string guards
fault rather than coerce), and a CAST. It deliberately does NOT claim a bare comparison is safe —
a cross-type ORDERING faults too — because blocking those from pushdown would block index seeding,
which is what pushdown is for.

Measured cost: none. The full `query_bench` is unchanged row for row, the indexed seek still shows
671x over the scan, and `spelling_probe` reports zero cliffs before and after (one ✗ on the first
run was timing noise on a 0.2ms query and did not reproduce).

### The guard had to be ONE rule, and the tests had to actually fault

Mutation found both problems in the first attempt.

The condition was written twice — in the precheck `any_pushable` and in `split_pushable` — and
removing it from either left the other declining, so both mutants survived. It is now one function
that both call.

Worse, the extra cases in the first test were VACUOUS. On a numeric fixture almost nothing raises:
`2 / 0` is infinity, `sqrt(0)` is 0, `(0 + 1)` is 1, and casting a number to a string always works.
Only `%` faults on a zero. So "the same for a call and a cast" was asserting that two non-faulting
plans agree. The fixture now gives the ELIMINATED vertex a STRING property, which makes an
arithmetic operand, a numeric function argument and a numeric CAST each fault — and
`a_raising_predicate_still_raises_when_it_is_reached` asserts that each really does fault when every
row reaches it, so the cases cannot go quiet again.

| mutant                                                        | caught by                                            |
| ------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- |
| `can_raise` forgets `Arith` / `Call` / `Cast` (three mutants) | the pushdown test, one case each                     |
| `can_raise` does not look inside a `Compare`                  | the pushdown test                                    |
| `pushable` drops the guard                                    | the pushdown test                                    |
| `pushable` blocks everything                                  | `an_ordinary_comparison_is_still_pushed_below_a_hop` |

### NOT fixed, and this is the part that needs a decision

Widening `rewrite_fuzz`'s generator to emit a raising predicate — the obvious durable guard — fails
immediately on a DIFFERENT rewrite, seed 11:

```text
raw:       Filter{ Filter{ group }, age >= 17 }  then  (score = 1 AND 2/age < -1)
optimized: Filter{ group, (score = 1 AND 2/age < -1) AND age >= 17 }
```

Merging two filters into one conjunction moves the raising conjunct alongside a predicate that used
to run FIRST and remove its rows. And `AND` here is vectorized rather than short-circuiting, so both
sides are evaluated for every row that reaches the filter.

So this is a CLASS, not an instance: the engine's error surface is plan-dependent wherever a
predicate can raise, and at least pushdown and filter-merging are affected. Three ways out, and they
differ in cost enough that it is not my call:

1. **Plan-independent errors.** Guard every reordering rewrite the way pushdown is now guarded. Cost
   unknown and possibly large — filter merging is not optional in the way pushdown is.
2. **Document that data exceptions are plan-dependent**, as SQL engines do, and scope the
   `rewrite_fuzz` invariant to rows-when-both-succeed. Cheap — but the TS and native engines would
   then be free to differ on which queries raise, and cross-engine byte-identity is described as a
   hard invariant.
3. **Short-circuiting AND**, which makes the raw order meaningful. A real semantic and performance
   change to the evaluator.

The generator widening is written and reverted rather than left red, because leaving it in would
make the suite red on a decision that has not been made.

### RESOLVED by the standard (2026-09-30)

The trilemma above was argued from first principles, and it did not need to be. ISO/IEC 39075
publishes its implementation-dependent list as a FREE digital artifact, and it answers this
directly:

| code      | text                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| --------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **US008** | "The actual order of expression evaluation."                                                                                                                                            |
| **UA004** | "Whether or not that exception condition is actually raised when the evaluation of an **inessential part** of an expression or search condition would cause an exception to be raised." |
| **UA006** | Which additional path bindings are probed to establish whether they might also raise, once the implementation has terminated a selective path pattern's evaluation.                     |
| **UV003** | Which `‹value expression›` raises `22G12 invalid value type`.                                                                                                                           |

So: a conforming GQL implementation may evaluate operands in any order, and may raise or not raise
from a part whose value is not needed. **Short-circuiting is conformant. Not short-circuiting is
conformant. An optimizer that surfaces an exception the unoptimized plan avoided is conformant.**

That removes the conformance horn of the trilemma entirely. What remains is a choice about our OWN
two invariants, which are stricter than the standard requires:

- `rewrite_fuzz`'s "optimizing must not change the answer" — ours, not ISO's.
- TS-vs-native byte-identity — ours, not ISO's, and the one worth protecting.

The resolution that follows, and it is cheap:

1. **Scope both invariants to rows-when-both-succeed**, citing US008/UA004 in the fuzzers
   themselves so the next reader does not re-derive this. Byte-identity on ROWS stays hard; the
   error surface becomes explicitly plan-dependent, as the standard permits.
2. **Keep the `can_raise` guards as a quality-of-implementation choice, not a correctness fix.** The
   three cheap ones (hop pushdown, filter merge, join pushdown) cost nothing measurable and make the
   common cases behave predictably, which is worth having for users even when the standard does not
   demand it. Reversal stays unguarded — it is the site where guarding costs something real, and now
   there is no obligation to pay it.
3. The guard comments must stop claiming they fix a conformance violation. They reduce surprise;
   they are not required.

### And the `%` question is settled the other way

`-conditions.xml` lists **`22012 division by zero`** as a defined GQL data exception. So `%`
raising on a zero divisor is the CONFORMANT behaviour, and the proposal to make it return NaN was
wrong. The deviations are the other two:

| our behaviour         | ISO                                                |
| --------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
| `2 / 0` → infinity    | `22012 division by zero`                           |
| `mod(2, 0)` → NaN     | `22012 division by zero`                           |
| `2 % 0` → raises      | matches `22012`                                    |
| `ln(-1)` → NaN        | **`2201E invalid argument for natural logarithm`** |
| `power(…)` edge cases | **`2201F invalid argument for power function`**    |

The `ln(-1)` one is worth flagging because this session used `ln(-1)` as a convenient NaN source in
two fixtures and in the differential fuzzer's stored-NaN setup. Under ISO it should raise. Changing
it is a cross-engine behavioural change and both engines currently agree, so it is recorded here
rather than acted on — but "we produce NaN where the standard names an exception" is now a known
deviation with a code, not an unexamined choice.

Cross-type comparison has a code too: **`22G04 values not comparable`**, which is the ISO name for
what [[cross-type-comparison-no-error]] settled behaviourally.

### Also open, from the same seed

The original differential divergence is NOT this bug and is still live:
`MATCH (n:T) CALL (n) { MATCH (n WHERE ((2 % n.x) IS NOT TRUE))-[:E]->(m) … }` — TS raises, native
answers. Native's raw and optimized plans AGREE there (both answer), so it is not an optimizer
question: native's CALL body evaluates the inline filter only for rows whose hop matches, where TS
evaluates it eagerly. A cross-engine evaluation-order difference, and the differential fuzzer will
keep hitting it on rare seeds until it is settled — it reproduced twice in about 16 runs.

## 68. A generative oracle for the aggregate fast paths, and the wrong answer it found (2026-09-30)

Items 64, 65 and 67 were three wrong answers in one day through one gap, and the gap was not a
missing assertion. It was that **no oracle ran on a fixture where the shortcut is chosen**:

- `rewrite_fuzz` asks the right question for a rewrite (raw against optimized) over graphs of a few
  dozen nodes, and the algebraic degree-sum count is gated on `est_paths > 2 * (nodes + edges)`. That
  branch never ran under it. Not once.
- The differential fuzzer compares TS against native over a THREE-vertex fixture, for the same
  reason.
- The benches use 50,000-vertex fixtures, which do select the branch — and they count rows and
  report times. They do not check answers. `varlen count` reported a wrong number quickly for as
  long as it existed.

So the missing ingredient was a FIXTURE SIZE. `exec/fastpath_fuzz.rs` sweeps sizes across that
threshold on purpose and computes the expected aggregate from the materialized rows itself, in the
test, so the oracle shares no code with what it tests:

```text
for every pattern P:   <agg> over P  ≡  the same <agg> computed from the rows of P
```

It covers `count(*)`, `count(DISTINCT …)`, `sum`, `min` and `max` over a var-length hop, across five
path modes, five direction/type spellings including an unknown type, quantifiers with `min` of 0,
and six endpoint tails including one that reads both ends.

### It has teeth, which is the only reason to trust it

All three bugs re-introduced, each caught, and the seed says how hard each was to reach:

| re-introduced bug                                        | first failing seed |
| -------------------------------------------------------- | -----------------: |
| item 64 — the zero-length term dropped from the algebra  |                136 |
| item 67 — the algebra ignoring the endpoint predicate    |                146 |
| item 65 — an unknown edge type answering 0               |                540 |
| item 68 — a trail admitted into the set-reachability BFS |               3249 |

That spread set the default seed count. 400 seeds would have missed two of the four, because each
needs a CONJUNCTION of draws. The default is 5,000 (3.2s); `LENKE_FASTPATH_FUZZ_SEEDS` goes deeper
and 100,000 runs clean in 58.7s.

Two things made that affordable. Fixtures are BUILT ONCE and reused — there are only 108 distinct
ones — and the largest is 1,500 vertices rather than 4,000, which selects exactly the same branch
(24,000 against 15,000 on the estimate) and costs 2.7x less on the materialized side. Seeds bought
that way are worth more than vertices.

### The wrong answer it found on its first real sweep: a trail counted as a walk

Seed 3249, on clean code:

```text
MATCH (x)-[:R]-{2,3}(y) RETURN count(DISTINCT y)
  shortcut: 36     distinct endpoints in the rows: 28
```

`varlen_distinct_endpoint_count` computes reachability with a BFS, and a BFS frontier may re-cross
an edge — so what it computes is WALK reachability. Its gate admitted TRAIL as well, and the comment
explains the slip exactly: it reasoned about NODE reuse, ruled out `Simple`/`Acyclic` for forbidding
that, and did not consider that a TRAIL forbids EDGE reuse, which is the constraint a BFS frontier
quietly breaks.

Reduced to two nodes and one edge, hand-derivable: `(x)-[:R]-{2,3}(y)` matches NOTHING, because a
trail of length two needs two distinct edges. The BFS walks `a -> b -> a` and reports two distinct
endpoints.

FIXED with `set_reachability_is_exact(mode, max)`, shared by both call sites: `Walk` at any bound,
`Trail` only at `max <= 1` (where no edge can be reused, so the two notions coincide), never
`Simple` or `Acyclic`. Anything larger for a trail needs per-path edge sets, which is not a BFS.

| mutant                                  | caught by                                    |
| --------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- |
| `Trail => true` (the bug)               | the hand-derived test + `fastpath_fuzz`      |
| `Trail => max <= 2` or `max <= 3`       | the hand-derived test (via its `{2,2}` case) |
| `Trail => false` (declining everywhere) | the one-hop shortcut test                    |
| `Walk => false`                         | the walk shortcut test                       |

### Two test-shape traps worth recording

**`{1,1}` is not a var-length hop.** The first version of the "a one-hop trail keeps the shortcut"
test used `{1,1}`, which lowers to a plain `Expand` and never reaches this shortcut at all — so it
passed whatever the trail gate said, and `Trail => false` survived. `{0,1}` is the spelling that
produces a `VarLength` with `max == 1`.

**The first thing this fuzzer flagged was the ORACLE.** `sum` over no rows is `0` in this engine, not
NULL — `Agg::null_on_empty` records that explicitly, and it exists for GREMLIN's `sum()` (no
traversers, no value) rather than for GQL. My expected-value fold had encoded SQL's rule from memory.
A generative test encodes a policy whether or not its author looked the policy up, and getting that
backwards makes the test fail on correct code, which is the cheap direction to be wrong in.

### Open, and not caused here

A random-seeded differential run failed on
`MATCH (n:T) CALL (n) { MATCH (n WHERE ((2 % n.x) IS NOT TRUE))-[:E]->(m) RETURN m.n AS mn } …`:
TS raises `E_INVALID_VALUE` where native returns four rows. Confirmed pre-existing by stashing this
item's changes and replaying the seed on HEAD. Native's `%` is correct in isolation — it throws on a
zero divisor, throws on a string operand, and yields NULL for a null one — so the divergence is
about the inline `WHERE` on an IMPORTED variable inside a `CALL` scope, which smells like item 51's
mini-batch shape. Its own item.

## 67. The degree algebra learned the endpoint predicate (2026-09-30)

The rest of item 63's lead. A `{1,2}` single-direction unit flattens to `VarLength`, whose
`count(*)` is an ALGEBRAIC degree sum in O(V+E) — and that path had no way to take an endpoint
predicate, so an endpoint filter dropped it to enumeration.

Sized in two steps rather than one, which was worth doing. Peeling the filter and tallying in the
DFS (item 63's machinery, extended to `try_varlen_count`) got 12,029us -> 7,316us. The floor
measured with the filter ignored outright was 759us, so most of the prize was in the algebra, not
the peel.

| row (50,000 vertices) |   before | peel only |           + algebra |
| --------------------- | -------: | --------: | ------------------: |
| `group count :label`  | 12,029us |   7,316us | **1,032us** (11.7x) |
| `group count .prop`   | 11,073us |   7,181us | **1,050us** (10.5x) |

### How a predicate folds into a degree sum

The algebra never walks a path, so it cannot ask whether a path's endpoint passed. It asks about
nodes instead: a node's contribution as a hop TARGET is either counted or not, so `outdeg[v]`
becomes a PASSING out-degree — the count of `v`'s out-edges whose target passes. Then the 1-hop term
counts only passing targets, the 2-hop term reads the passing out-degree, and the zero-length term
counts only sources that pass (its endpoint IS the source). The trail correction subtracts the
reused-self-loop path only when that path's endpoint would have passed.

Every one of those four is a separate mutant and all four are caught — three of them by
`rewrite_fuzz` as well as by name, since this is now a rewrite that changes an answer if it is wrong.

### The fallback, and why it is not optional

`node_pass_mask` asks the predicate about every node, not only reached ones, because the algebra has
no reached set. That is exactly the equivalence item 63 recorded as NOT holding: a predicate can
throw on a value the walk would never have visited. So the algebra sits in a labelled block and
breaks out to the enumerate-and-tally path when the mask cannot be built.
`the_algebraic_count_falls_back_when_a_predicate_cannot_be_evaluated_everywhere` is the big-fixture
twin of item 63's test — the small one cannot exercise this, because it never selects the algebraic
branch at all.

### Two mutants that needed a different kind of observable

| mutant                                                           | caught by                                                                            |
| ---------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| `outdeg` does not apply the predicate                            | 3 named + 3 `rewrite_fuzz`                                                           |
| the 1-hop term does not apply it                                 | 3 named + 3 `rewrite_fuzz`                                                           |
| the zero-length term does not apply it                           | 2 named + 3 `rewrite_fuzz`                                                           |
| the self-loop correction ignores it                              | 3 named                                                                              |
| the DFS tally attributes every path to node 0                    | 1 named + 3 `rewrite_fuzz`                                                           |
| `node_pass_mask` skips the last node                             | the TIMEOUT — the mutant makes the block loop stop advancing and it never terminates |
