A pure performance change has no correctness test that fails when it stops firing, so
`repeat-group head(b)` was added to `query/shortest`. But its numbers are NOT the measurement
here: across three baseline runs that row ranged 107,761-140,606us, a **30% spread**, because
the bench group runs many cases in one process and the allocator state varies. The isolated
probe above ranged under 3%. Per the noise-floor rule, the bench row is the regression
tripwire — a return to ~142,000us means the borrow stopped happening — and the probe is the
number.

### Two mutants, and the second needed a new assertion

| mutant                                   | caught by              |
| ---------------------------------------- | ---------------------- |
| borrow slot 0 instead of slot `k`        | both named tests       |
| borrow the LAST slot instead of slot `k` | **initially survived** |

The second survived because every query in the new tests referenced `b`, which IS the last
slot — so "the last column" and "slot `k`" were the same column and the mutation was invisible.
Fixed by also asserting `head(a)` differs from `head(b)`: `a` is bound at a LOWER slot and holds
the source nodes rather than the targets. With that assertion the mutant fails. Same shape as
item 48's surviving wrong-slot mutant, except there the wrong slot made the fast path DECLINE
(so the answer stayed right) and here it silently reads the wrong column — worth distinguishing.

Poisoning the borrow branch showed exactly two tests reach it: the two added here. Nothing else
in the suite calls a scalar function with a bare-slot argument, which is worth knowing — the
broad scalar-function tests pass either way and are not evidence for this path.

### Still open

`Value::List` remains `Vec<Value>`, so the PER-ROW value clone in
`buf.extend(cols.iter().map(|c| c.value_at(i)))` is untouched — that is the 14-18ms these
functions still sit above the floor. Removing it needs either `Arc<Vec<Value>>` for
`Value::List` (touching equality, ordering, rendering and TS/Rust byte-identity, so the full
fuzzer suite) or a by-reference `call_scalar` taking `&[&Value]`. Neither attempted; the table
above is the size of what is left.

## 48. Reading a list's length deep-copied the list — 1.44x (2026-09-30)

The other half of item 47's decomposition. `size()` cost ~73ns a call (44.1ms over 599,998
rows) where reading a `Vec` length should be nearer 1ns. Two causes, both in getting the
ARGUMENT rather than in the function:

- `Value::List` is `Vec<Value>`, not an `Arc`, and `Col::value_at` is
  `Col::Gen(v) => v[i].clone()`. So the generic per-row call loop DEEP-COPIED each list —
  allocate a fresh `Vec`, copy every element, read `len()`, drop it.
- `eval` on an `Expr::Slot` is `batch.slot(n).clone()`, which clones the whole COLUMN. For a
  boxed list column that is one `Vec` allocation and copy per row, paid before the function
  runs at all. **That clone is on the path of every function over a boxed column, not just
  this one** — see the open lead below.

FIXED with a by-reference arm for `size`/`cardinality` over a `Col::Gen`: borrow the column
when the argument is a bare slot, and map lists to their length without materializing a
`Value`. Isolated A/B, only the arm disabled, three runs each, min-of-3 (the baseline's own
spread was 9%, so this is the conservative reading):

| row                    | baseline | with the arm |   speedup |
| ---------------------- | -------: | -----------: | --------: |
| `repeat-group control` |   99,254 |   **68,826** | **1.44x** |
| `varlen control`       |   15,203 |       15,368 | unchanged |

Both halves of item 47's `((a)-[:R]->(b)){1,2} RETURN size(b)` row are now addressed: the list
BUILDING when unread (item 47, 4.46x) and the list-length READ here.

### Two dead arms, found by mutation and then deleted

The first version reproduced `call_scalar_checked`'s full semantics inline — a `Value::Str`
arm counting UTF-16 units, and a hard `E_INVALID_VALUE` for anything else, since `size()` of a
number is a data exception and NOT a null. Mutating both showed neither was reachable:

| mutant on the first version                    | result                       |
| ---------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------- |
| count a string's BYTES instead of UTF-16 units | **survived the whole suite** |
| turn the type error into a null                | **survived the whole suite** |

A string argument evaluates to `Col::Str`, not `Col::Gen`, so the fast path never sees one; and
no query shape puts a non-null non-string non-list into a `Col::Gen`. So both arms were dead
code duplicating semantics that already had one correct implementation.

Replaced by a pre-scan: take the arm only when every value is a list or null, and let anything
else fall through to the generic path. That deleted the duplication AND measured FASTER —
68,826us against 78,002us for the version with the extra arms, because the inner loop lost its
per-value error branch. The pre-scan itself is a discriminant check per row with no allocation,
against the ~51ns a row the clone was costing.

### The pre-scan guards a REACHABLE bug, and needed a test built for it

Dropping the pre-scan initially survived everything too, which would have made it look
defensive. It is not: a `CASE` yields `Col::Gen` of `Value::Str`, so without the pre-scan a
boxed string column maps every non-list to null and
`size(CASE WHEN … THEN 'ab' ELSE 'cde' END)` returns NULL instead of 2 and 3.
`a_boxed_column_of_strings_still_gets_its_string_length` was written for exactly that and
catches the mutant alone.

| mutant on the shipped version     | caught by                |
| --------------------------------- | ------------------------ |
| off-by-one on the list length     | 14 tests                 |
| drop the lists-and-nulls pre-scan | its named test, alone    |
| borrow slot 0 instead of slot `k` | **survives — see below** |

The last one deserves naming rather than a fix. Borrowing the wrong slot makes the arm DECLINE
(slot 0 holds `Col::Nodes`, not `Col::Gen`), so the generic path runs and the answer is right,
just slow. No test here can distinguish it, because a single-hop group's two bound lists always
have the SAME length — `size(a)` and `size(b)` agree by construction, so reading the wrong one
is invisible. It is a latent correctness hazard only if some future shape puts a differently
sized `Gen` column at slot 0.

### Open: `eval` on a Slot clones the whole column

> **FIXED — item 49 (2026-09-30), 1.26-1.45x across the list functions.** The per-ROW value
> clone below it remains.

`Expr::Slot(n) => batch.slot(*n).clone()` (exec/evaluator.rs) is the general form of the second
cause above. For typed columns that is a `Vec<u32>`/`Vec<f64>` memcpy; for `Col::Gen` of
`Value::List` or `Value::Map` it is a per-row allocation. Every scalar function over a boxed
column pays it, and only `size`/`cardinality` now avoid it. The principled fixes are an
`Arc<Vec<Value>>` payload for `Value::List` (touches equality, ordering and byte-identity, so
not lightly) or a borrowing argument path in the evaluator. Neither attempted; the measurement
above is the size of the prize for ONE function.

## 47. A quantified group built its per-rep lists even when nothing read them — 4.5x (2026-09-30)

Item 46 left RepeatGroup ~6x a var-length hop at baseline. That gap had THREE confounds stacked
in one comparison, so the first step was separating them rather than optimizing anything —
50,000 nodes, degree 3, 599,998 rows, min-of-3 each:

| variant                                     |       ms | what it isolates                         |
| ------------------------------------------- | -------: | ---------------------------------------- |
| D var-length, unlabelled, project a literal |     13.6 | the floor                                |
| C var-length, unlabelled, `y.name`          |     14.9 | a property read: **1.3**                 |
| A var-length, LABELLED, `y.name`            |     17.0 | the label: **~0** (unlabelled is faster) |
| **E group, unlabelled, project a literal**  | **82.3** | **the group's list columns: 68.7**       |
| B group, `size(b)`                          |    126.4 | **`size()` on a list: 44.1**             |

So the "6x" was two unrelated costs, not one: **68.7ms of per-rep list building** and **44.1ms
of `size()`** (599,998 calls, ~73ns each — high for reading a `Vec` length, and untouched here).
The label difference contributed nothing.

Row E is the finding: it projects a LITERAL, so nothing reads the group's lists, and they were
built anyway.

FIXED by generalizing item 46. That item's rewrite fired only under a bare `count(*)`; this
replaces it with `drop_unread_group_binds`, a top-down pass that accumulates the highest slot
any expression above reads (via `max_slot`) and drops the bindings when every group slot is
above that high-water mark. A single-hop group with its lists gone IS a var-length hop, so it
flattens to one and picks up the counting and frontier fast paths too.

Isolated A/B, only the pass disabled so the bench rows are identical both sides, three runs
each, min-of-3:

| row                   | baseline | with the pass |   speedup |
| --------------------- | -------: | ------------: | --------: |
| `repeat-group unread` |   69,478 |    **15,593** | **4.46x** |
| `repeat-group count`  |   64,239 |       **615** |  **104x** |
| `varlen control`      |   14,751 |        15,005 | unchanged |

`repeat-group unread` now matches `varlen control` to within 4%. The count row is item 46's
106.7x reproduced through the general pass, which is the evidence that it subsumes the special
case — as is the fact that all five of item 46's tests still pass with that code deleted.

A bench row was added for this (`repeat-group unread`: `((a)-[:KNOWS]->(b)){1,2} (t) RETURN
t.name`), because the existing rows cannot see it — `repeat-group control` reads `size(b)` and
so keeps its lists by design. A pure performance change has no correctness test that fails when
it stops firing, so that row IS the guard: back to ~69,000us means the rewrite went quiet.

### Conservative by construction

Dropping a binding something reads is a wrong answer; declining costs only time. So the walk
uses a SHORT whitelist (`Project`, `Aggregate`, `Filter`) and stops at anything else, and it
leans on `max_slot` being exhaustive over `Expr` with no wildcard arm — it already reports
`usize::MAX` for any path read and `outer_width - 1` for a correlated subquery, so both block
the rewrite without needing a special case.

`Distinct` and `OrderPage` are deliberately EXCLUDED despite looking harmless: `Distinct` dedups
on every column, so it reads the lists whether or not an expression names them, and a page below
the output would carry them into the result.

`k == 1` and no `per_rep_pred` are still required to FLATTEN to a var-length hop (the reasons are
in item 46). Both shapes still get the lists dropped, which is the larger of the two costs.

| mutant                           | caught by                     |
| -------------------------------- | ----------------------------- |
| treat every binding as unread    | 13 tests, incl. 4 named ones  |
| ignore the `Filter`'s expression | its named test + 5 fuzz tests |
| descend through `Distinct`       | **fuzzer only**               |

The `Distinct` one is worth naming: `a_distinct_above_a_group_keeps_its_lists` does not catch
it, because in that query `DISTINCT` sits ABOVE the projection that already narrowed the
columns, so dropping the lists really is harmless there. The generated plans put `Distinct`
directly over the group, which is the shape that breaks. Third time in this run that the fuzzer
was the only catcher.

### Still open

- **`size()` on a list costs ~73ns per call** (44.1ms over 599,998 rows). Reading a `Vec`
  length should be nearer 1ns, so something is allocating or boxing per call. Untouched, and
  the `repeat-group control` row measures it. **FIXED — item 48 (2026-09-30), 1.44x:** the
  argument was being deep-copied twice over, once per row by `Col::value_at` and once per
  column by `eval` on a `Slot`.
- `RepeatGroup` still has no `path_need` field, so its size-only PATH read is unimproved
  (1.25x). This pass does not subsume that: it fires when the group's lists are unread, which is
  a different condition from the path being read only for its size.

## 46. `count(*)` over a quantified subpath group enumerated every row — 106x (2026-09-30)

Item 45 pointed at RepeatGroup's ~6x baseline. Dumping both optimized plans side by side
before touching anything found something larger and unrelated: a var-length hop shortcuts
`count(*)` and a quantified subpath group had no such path, so it walked and materialized all
599,998 rows to return one number.

| query                                  | 2,000 nodes | 50,000 nodes |  answer |
| -------------------------------------- | ----------: | -----------: | ------: |
| `count(*)` over `(x)-[:R]->{1,2}(y)`   |      0.1 ms |   **0.8 ms** | 599,998 |
| `count(*)` over `((a)-[:R]->(b)){1,2}` |      3.5 ms |  **79.1 ms** | 599,998 |

Identical answer, same shape, ~99x apart.

FIXED in the `Aggregate` rewrite arm: a BARE `count(*)` reads no column, so a single-hop
group's per-rep list columns are provably unread beneath one — and without them the group IS a
var-length hop, which unlocks every counting fast path the group executor lacks. Isolated A/B,
only the rewrite disabled so the bench rows are identical both sides, three runs each,
min-of-3:

| row                  | baseline | with rewrite |    speedup |
| -------------------- | -------: | -----------: | ---------: |
| `repeat-group count` |   64,655 |      **606** | **106.7x** |
| `varlen count`       |      568 |          563 |  unchanged |

606us against 563us is within 8%: the two spellings of the same count now cost the same, which
is what the equivalent-spellings rule asks for.

### Two guards, both reachable, both mutation-verified

- **`k == 1`.** A multi-hop unit emits only at rep BOUNDARIES (`len % k == 0`) while a
  var-length hop emits at every hop, AND the equivalent hop bounds are `min * k ..= max * k`
  rather than `min ..= max` — two separate ways the answer would change. This is REACHABLE:
  `((x)-[:R]->(m)-[:R]->(y)){1,2}` over one edge type lowers to `RepeatGroup { k: 2 }`. (The
  same shape written with two DIFFERENT edge types lowers to `NestedGroup` instead, which the
  rewrite never matches — a different plan node, not this guard. The first attempt at the test
  was written that way and proved nothing, which is how the distinction came up.)
- **`keys.is_empty()` and every agg a bare `Count` with no argument.** Anything else reads a
  column, so the group's bindings may be live.

| mutant                          | caught by                     |
| ------------------------------- | ----------------------------- |
| drop the `k == 1` guard         | its named test, alone         |
| drop the bare-`count` condition | 3 fuzz tests                  |
| drop `keys.is_empty()`          | its named test + 3 fuzz tests |

### A correction, and a correction to the correction

Item 45 recorded "RepeatGroup's CONTROL is 91,079us against VarLength's 15,822us, ~6x more
expensive at baseline". Dumping the plans showed that comparison was not valid as written:

- The group query's projection was `RETURN b.name`, and `b` is a GROUP variable bound to a
  LIST. A bare `.prop` on a list is null by design, so that row returned 599,998 NULLs — a
  control that measured a query doing no useful work against one doing real work.
- The two plans also disagreed on the scan: `Scan { label: None }` for the group against
  `Scan { label: Some("Person") }` for the hop, because the inner label had been dropped to
  avoid `E_NOT_IMPLEMENTED`.

The bench row is now `RETURN size(b)`, which reads the list properly. **And with that valid
control the gap is still about 6x** (97,418us against 15,744us), so the original observation
survives — what was wrong was the comparison's validity, not its magnitude. That remaining 6x
is the group-binding machinery plus a per-row `size()`, and it is still uninvestigated.

### Still open

`RepeatGroup` has no `path_need` field, so its size-only path read (1.25x, gap 22,613us) is
unimproved; this rewrite does not subsume it, since it only fires under a bare `count(*)`,
where no path is read at all. And the ~6x baseline above remains open with a now-valid control
to measure against.

> **SUPERSEDED — item 47 (2026-09-30).** The bare-`count(*)` rewrite here was replaced by a
> general unread-bindings pass (4.46x on a non-count shape, and it reproduces this item's 106x).
> The ~6x baseline decomposed into 68.7ms of list building, now fixed when unread, plus 44.1ms
> of `size()` at ~73ns per call, still open.

## 45. `SHORTEST k` only needed the mode FORWARDED — 1.43x (2026-09-30)

The third of item 44's measured candidates. `SHORTEST k` shares `Plan::ShortestPath`, so the
optimizer's mark already covered it; `shortest_path` simply was not passing `path_need` on to
`shortest_k_path`, which is its only caller. Four lines of forwarding plus the emit site.

A throwaway hack sized it first, because the structure differs again: `collect_trails` CLONES
each trail's node and edge chains into `per_end` whatever the plan reads, so unlike the BFS
(where the walk itself was skippable) and unlike `VarLength` (where the DFS stacks are the
chain), here the enumeration is unavoidable and only the final copy out can go. The hack said
115,275us -> 83,119us and the finished change landed on it.

Isolated A/B — only the suppression disabled, bench rows identical on both sides, three runs
each, min-of-3:

| row                           | baseline | count-only |   speedup |
| ----------------------------- | -------: | ---------: | --------: |
| `shortest-k + path_length(p)` |  116,524 | **81,416** | **1.43x** |
| shortest-k control            |   76,815 |     76,801 | unchanged |

81,416us against a 76,801us lineage-free control closes the gap to within 6%. That completes
the family for the three operators worth doing: **ShortestPath 3.13x (item 43), VarLength 2.56x
(item 44), SHORTEST k 1.43x**, each now within 6% of projecting an ordinary property over the
same rows.

`the_shortest_k_traversal_is_guarded_too` (item 40) needed the same correction items 43 and 44
made: it asked for `path_length(p)`, which no longer materializes, so it had nothing to charge
against the ceiling. Now `nodes(p)`.

### The regression guard here is the BENCHMARK, not a test

Two mutants, and the pair is the point:

| mutant                                          | result                                |
| ----------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------- |
| suppress unconditionally, ignoring `count_only` | caught by 3 unit tests + 3 fuzz tests |
| do NOT forward the mode (pass `Full`)           | **SURVIVES the whole suite**          |

The second is not a gap to be closed. Forwarding or not forwarding is a pure performance
change: the rows are identical either way, so no correctness test can distinguish them and none
should pretend to. What guards it is the `query/shortest` bench case — the shortest-k row
returning to ~116,000us is the signal, which is why that row exists and why the measured numbers
are in this entry rather than only in a commit message.

That asymmetry is worth keeping in mind for the rest of this family: the count-only mode's
CORRECTNESS is fuzzer- and unit-tested, its PRESENCE is only ever benchmark-tested.

### Still open in the family

`RepeatGroup` (1.25x, gap 22,613us) shares `var_length` but has no `path_need` of its own. It is
the smallest of the three ratios, and the more interesting number about it is that its CONTROL
is 91,079us against `VarLength`'s 15,822us for the same 599,998 rows — about 6x more expensive
at baseline for reasons that have nothing to do with lineage. That is the better prize and it
has not been investigated.

> **INVESTIGATED — item 46 (2026-09-30).** That control was invalid as written (it projected
> `b.name` off a group LIST, which is null by design, so it returned 599,998 nulls). With a
> valid control the ~6x survives and is still open — but looking found something bigger next
> door: `count(*)` over a group enumerated every row, now **106x** faster.

## 44. A size-only var-length read stops copying the chain out — 2.56x (2026-09-30)

Item 43 left three operators still materializing for a size-only read. Measuring all three
FIRST reordered them, and it also showed the anchored+bounded shape those queries were first
written with is useless for the question: `WHERE x.name = 'name1'` over `->{1,2}` returns
**12 rows**, where the `*` shortest path reaches 47,007. At 12 rows nothing is visible. The
numbers below are unanchored — every source, two hops, 599,998 rows — against the same
lineage-free control (a property projection over the same rows):

| operator      | control | + `path_length(p)` |     ratio |      gap |
| ------------- | ------: | -----------------: | --------: | -------: |
| **VarLength** |  15,822 |             58,891 | **3.72x** | 43,069us |
| SHORTEST k    |  75,588 |            116,613 | **1.54x** | 41,025us |
| RepeatGroup   |  91,079 |            113,692 | **1.25x** | 22,613us |

So `SHORTEST k` DOES pay (item 43 guessed it might not, since it enumerates trails rather than
running one BFS) but VarLength pays most, and that is what this item does.

One structural difference from item 43, sized with a throwaway hack before any real code:
`CollectEmit::emit` receives `node_stack` / `edge_stack` already built, because the DFS's stacks
ARE the chain. So the walk cannot be skipped here, only the COPY out of it. The hack said
58,891us -> 22,662us, and the finished change landed on it.

Isolated A/B — only the marking disabled, the bench rows identical on both sides, three runs
each, min-of-3:

| row                       | baseline | count-only |   speedup |
| ------------------------- | -------: | ---------: | --------: |
| `varlen + path_length(p)` |   55,152 | **21,530** | **2.56x** |
| varlen control            |   14,780 |     15,152 | unchanged |

`PathBufs` gains `elem_counts`; `Plan::VarLength` gains `path_need`; `set_path_need`'s marking
arm now covers both producers. Both arms STOP the descent, so at most the topmost path producer
in a plan suppresses and any producer below it keeps materializing — which is what keeps a
suppressed lineage from ever becoming the input of an operator that would rebuild it (the seed
632 failure in item 43).

`RepeatGroup` shares `var_length` but has no `path_need` of its own and passes `Full`;
`SHORTEST k` routes to `shortest_k_path` and still materializes. Both remain open, with numbers
above and an oracle waiting.

### Tests

Three new (771 total), plus a helper correction. Mutation-verified under `timeout` +
`MemoryMax=16G -p MemorySwapMax=0`:

| mutant                                   | caught by                    |
| ---------------------------------------- | ---------------------------- |
| varlen counts omit the input path PREFIX | **fuzzer only**              |
| marking descends past the first producer | 2 named tests + 3 fuzz tests |

The prefix mutant being fuzzer-only is the SECOND time in two items (see item 43), so it is a
pattern rather than an accident: every unit test in `exec/tests.rs` seeds from a plain `Scan`,
whose path prefix is one node and zero edges, which makes the wrong expression numerically equal
to the right one. Only generated plans stack hops beneath the producer. Anything touching path
SIZE arithmetic should assume the unit tests cannot see it.

`an_operator_that_rebuilds_the_path_above_it_forces_full_lineage` (item 43) began failing here,
correctly: it asserted `!any_count_only(plan)`, and with `VarLength` now markable that helper
answers "yes" for a marked var-length hop sitting above an unmarked shortest path — a true
statement about the plan but not what the test meant. Split out `shortest_path_need`, which
names the operator it is asking about.

## 43. A size-only path read no longer builds the chain — 3.1x (2026-09-30)

The payoff item 41 measured and item 42 made safe to build. `path_length(p)` and
`cardinality(p)` need a number, and the BFS's `dist[&node]` already holds the hop count — yet
every emitted row walked back through the predecessor map to rebuild a chain that was then
reduced to its length.

`Plan::ShortestPath` gains `path_need: PathNeed`, set by `set_path_need` as the LAST step of
`optimize_indexed` (after pushdown and orientation, since the answer depends on what reads the
path in the final tree). `CountOnly` records each row's `(node count, edge count)` in a new
`Lineage::elem_counts` and skips the chain walk entirely.

Clean A/B against HEAD, three runs each side, same fixture (50,000 nodes, degree 3), min-of-3:

| row                             | baseline | count-only |                 speedup |
| ------------------------------- | -------: | ---------: | ----------------------: |
| anchored + `path_length(p)`     |   21,905 |  **6,999** |               **3.13x** |
| both ends anchored (early stop) |   17,237 |  **5,422** |               **3.18x** |
| `y.name` (lineage-free control) |    6,744 |      6,655 |               unchanged |
| anchored + `nodes(p)`           | ~281,000 |   ~281,000 | unchanged, as predicted |

`path_length(p)` now costs within 4% of projecting an ordinary property over the same 47,007
rows. Cumulative over items 41 and 43: **27,687us -> 6,999us, 4.0x.** `nodes(p)` is untouched
because its cost is `path_node_values` rendering element maps, which suppression cannot reach —
item 41 predicted exactly that, and the number confirms it.

### Three representations, one accessor

`elem_counts` empty means the elements are materialized and the sizes are the `offsets` /
`edge_offsets` spans. Non-empty means the elements were never built and the counts are the
truth. `Lineage::node_count_at` / `edge_count_at` are the ONLY readers that know which, so
`PathPart::Length` and `Cardinality` (exec/evaluator.rs) now go through them instead of
`edges.len()` — correct in both representations, and unconditional.

`offsets` stays an honest prefix-sum over the empty `values`, all zeros. That is the whole
difference from item 41's sketch, which would have carried the lengths IN the offsets: `path_at`
slices `values[offsets[i]..offsets[i + 1]]`, so non-zero offsets over an empty `values` is an
out-of-range slice. `try_shortest_early_stop` gathers directly, so an ordinary
`WHERE y.name = …` after a shortest path would have panicked.

Derivers: `gather` reorders the counts, `extend` adds (1 node, 1 edge), `extend_nodes` adds
(1 node, 0 edges), `push_step` carries them, `concat` normalizes MIXED arms to suppressed by
taking the materialized arms' sizes through the accessors, and the four seeds materialize so
they carry none.

### What the marking may and may not descend through

`scan_path` in exec/render.rs is now shared by `needs_lineage` and a new
`needs_path_elements`, parameterized by a leaf predicate. Sharing it is the safety property:
that walk enumerates every `Plan` and every `Expr` variant with no wildcard, so a variant added
later fails to compile until classified — a second hand-written walk would instead keep
answering "no element read" for the new shape, and suppressing a path somebody reads is a wrong
answer.

`CountOnly` is set only when `needs_lineage && !needs_path_elements`, so a size read ALONGSIDE
an element read keeps everything materialized (one sidecar serves both).

The marking walk descends only through operators that leave the path alone or merely GATHER it
(Project, Filter, Aggregate, Distinct, OrderPage, Tail, Enumerate, Sample, DistinctBy) and
stops at anything else. A whitelist, not a blacklist, because the directions are asymmetric:
not marking costs performance, marking under an operator that REBUILDS the path is a wrong
answer.

### Two bugs the new oracle caught, on its first run

Both were found by `rewrite_fuzz` within seconds of the mode compiling, and neither by any
unit test at that point.

1. **`elem_counts` ran short of the row count** — `index out of bounds: the len is 14 but the
index is 14` inside `Lineage::gather`. `shortest_path` emits from FIVE places (the Any and
   All arms plus three in the cycle-closing block) and only Any had been taught the mode. Fixed
   by threading the mode into `push_path` itself, so every call site is handled by construction
   and the compiler enumerates them — a mode branched per site is a mode one site forgets.
2. **Seed 632: a `VarLength` above the `ShortestPath`.** It rebuilds the path through
   `path_at`, which on a suppressed lineage reads an EMPTY prefix, so everything the shortest
   path contributed vanished: `cardinality(p)` came back 3/5/7 raw and 0/2 optimized. This is
   what produced the whitelist above.

### Tests, and one the unit tests could not catch

Six new tests in `exec/tests.rs`; the four ceiling tests from item 40 now use `nodes(p)`
(`ALL_PAIRS_NODES`) since `path_length` no longer materializes and so no longer tests element
accounting. Mutation-verified under `timeout` + `MemoryMax=16G -p MemorySwapMax=0`:

| mutant                                                  | caught by                     |
| ------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------- |
| `push_counts` swaps nodes/edges                         | 3 unit tests + 3 fuzz tests   |
| Any arm drops the input path PREFIX                     | **fuzzer only**               |
| marking descends through `VarLength` (re-adds seed 632) | its named test + 4 fuzz tests |
| `gather` drops `elem_counts`                            | its named test + 3 fuzz tests |

The second row is worth recording plainly: every unit test here seeds from a plain `Scan`,
whose path prefix is 1 node and 0 edges, so `dn + 1, dn` is accidentally equal to the correct
`node_count_at(row) + dn, edge_count_at(row) + dn`. No unit test in this file can distinguish
them; the generated plans, which stack hops beneath the shortest path, can. That is the second
time in two items that building the oracle first was what made the optimization safe.

### Deliberate behavior change: the ceiling is higher in this mode

Item 40's guard counts rows plus materialized path elements, so with the elements suppressed a
size-only query is charged rows only. That is the honest accounting rather than a hole: a
suppressed row costs one `(u32, u32)` plus two offsets, about 24 bytes, against ~94 bytes per
path ELEMENT materialized. At the 50,000,000 default that is ~1.2 GB, the same order as the
~2 GB bound measured for the materialized path in item 40.
`a_size_only_path_clears_a_ceiling_the_materialized_one_trips` pins it.

### Not done

`SHORTEST k` routes to `shortest_k_path`, a separate trail enumerator, which ignores
`path_need` and materializes. Safe (the accessors fall back to the spans) and simply
unoptimized. `VarLength` and `RepeatGroup` likewise build full lineage for a size-only read;
they have no `path_need` field. Both are follow-ups, and both now have an oracle waiting.

> **VarLength DONE — item 44 (2026-09-30), 2.56x. `SHORTEST k` DONE — item 45, 1.43x.**
> Only `RepeatGroup` (1.25x) is left, and its unexplained ~6x baseline is the better prize
> there.

## 42. The optimizer fuzzer had never once compared a path (2026-09-30)

Item 41 recorded that putting the count-only decision on `Plan::ShortestPath` "buys a free
oracle", since `rewrite_fuzz` compares raw against optimized as multisets. **That oracle did
not exist.** `grep -n 'PathAccess\|path_length\|PathPart' opt/rewrite_fuzz.rs` matched nothing:
the generator emitted `ShortestPath` (346/2000 plans), `VarLength` (429/2000) and
`RepeatGroup` (315/2000), and then projected only slots, `count(*)` and numeric aggregates. So
every lineage-producing operator was generated and its lineage was never read. A count-only
mode built behind that oracle would have been built behind nothing.

This is the same wall as items 38 and 39, in a new place: the operator was reachable, the
PROPERTY was not.

FIXED: `Gen` gains `tracks_path`, set wherever `VarLength` / `ShortestPath` / `RepeatGroup` is
generated, and the canary arm of the terminal projection now also projects
`Expr::PathAccess { part }` one time in three when the plan produces a path. It rides the
canary rather than taking an arm of its own, so the slot-permutation sensitivity that arm
exists for stays in the same rows, and the seed-to-plan mapping shifts as little as possible.

`Length` and `Cardinality` only, deliberately: both are plain numbers, so comparing them as a
multiset cannot report a false difference the way comparing rendered element maps might.

> **The worry was UNFOUNDED — item 58 (2026-09-30).** An element map is byte-deterministic
> (labels sorted, properties in already-sorted `prop_keys()` order), so the element accessors are
> generated now too. Checking it also found that NO oracle read a rendered path element.
> `nodes(p)` / `edges(p)` / `Elements` return lists of maps and are left for a widening that
> means to take on map-ordering as a question. `tracks_path` is also deliberately FALSE across a
> join, since a join's lineage handling is its own question and a path projected above one would
> be testing that instead.

Measured density: **path-proj 80/2000 plans**, 0 faulted. Floor asserted at half the measured
value (`n / 50`), alongside the other density floors — a generator change that stopped emitting
path projections would otherwise restore the original blindness invisibly, which is exactly how
the mid-pattern-split coverage nearly drifted away (see the note on that floor).

Deep sweep clean: `LENKE_OPT_FUZZ_SEEDS=200000` green in 42s. No existing lineage bug — the
coverage is new, the engine was already right.

### Teeth, proved in both directions

The usual mutation check is not enough here, because the thing to establish is that the NEW
coverage is what catches the bug. A lineage bug in shared code (say `PathPart::Length` reading
`edges.len() - 1`) is invisible to this oracle by construction — it corrupts raw and optimized
identically, and the two sides are all it compares. So the mutant has to be one OPTIMIZING
introduces. Injected in `optimize_indexed`: after the rewrites, rewrite any projected
`PathPart::Length` to `PathPart::Cardinality`. Row count untouched; only the projected path
VALUE differs between the two sides.

- Mutant **with** the path projection: `optimizing_preserves_rows_{with,without}_indexes`,
  `_across_shapes` and `_across_sizes` all FAIL.
- Same mutant with the path projection disabled (`if false && …`): all four PASS. The only
  failure is `the_generator_actually_reaches_the_rewrites`, and that is the new floor
  reporting the coverage loss — the floor doing precisely its job.

That second run is the evidence that matters: it is the old generator, and it is blind.

### Why the count-only optimization is still not built

Item 41's plan was to suppress the lineage value vectors and carry `offsets` as a length
prefix-sum. **That design is unsafe and would have panicked in production.** `Lineage::gather`
(lenke-engine-core/src/batch.rs:235) rebuilds values with
`values.extend_from_slice(self.path_at(i))`, and `path_at` slices
`values[offsets[i]..offsets[i+1]]` — so empty values with non-zero offsets is an out-of-range
slice, not a clever encoding. `try_shortest_early_stop` calls `sp_batch.gather(&keep)` directly
(exec/reverse.rs), so an ordinary `WHERE y.name = …` after a shortest path would have hit it.
The invariant "nothing calls `path_at` in this mode" cannot be held by a plan predicate, because
the derivers call it regardless of what the plan reads.

So the mode needs the hop counts carried EXPLICITLY (a parallel `Vec<u32>` on `Lineage`, with
`offsets` kept as honest indices into an empty `values`), and then the failure mode of a deriver
that forgets to propagate it is a WRONG ANSWER rather than a panic. That is the failure mode this
audit's own equivalent-spellings section calls the worst kind, and it is why the oracle had to
come first. It now exists, with verified teeth, so the mode can be built behind it.

## 41. The shortest-path chain walk allocated two Vecs per emitted row (2026-09-30)

Following item 40's open lead, and it corrects that item's diagnosis. `shortest_path`'s emit
loop called `first_pred_chain(node, start, &preds)`, which returns `(Vec<u32>, Vec<u32>)` — so
a whole-graph traversal allocated two fresh `Vec`s per (source, target) pair. Added
`first_pred_chain_into`, writing into buffers declared once outside the row loop.

Clean A/B, three runs each side, same binary path and fixture (50,000 nodes, degree 3),
min-of-3:

| row                             | baseline | with reuse |   speedup |
| ------------------------------- | -------: | ---------: | --------: |
| anchored + `path_length(p)`     |   27,687 |     22,750 | **1.22x** |
| both ends anchored (early stop) |   22,285 |     17,265 | **1.29x** |

The reuse side is also much tighter run-to-run (22.7-24.7k vs 27.7-29.2k), which is itself the
allocator noise going away. No semantic change, so it helps every path shape rather than one
projection.

### Correcting item 40's open lead

Item 40 said `path_length(p)`'s 4.5x would be recovered by suppressing the value vectors, and
that doing so "would cost it nothing". Both halves were wrong, established by two throwaway
hacks that measured the ceiling before any real code was written:

| variant                         | best_us | vs lineage-free control (6,714) |
| ------------------------------- | ------: | ------------------------------: |
| today (before this fix)         |  30,321 |                            4.5x |
| suppress the value vectors only |  19,075 |                            2.8x |
| ALSO skip the chain walk        |   7,070 |                           1.05x |

So suppression alone recovers under half the gap. The dominant cost is reconstructing the chain
at all — and `dn = dist[&node]` in the emit loop ALREADY holds the hop count, so a query that
only needs a length never needs the walk. That is the remaining prize: ~22,750 -> ~7,070, a
further 3.2x, and it lands within 5% of a query that projects an ordinary property.

Also corrected: item 40 read `nodes(p)`'s 42x as materialization too. It is not — `PathPart::Nodes`
calls `path_node_values(store, nodes)` (exec/evaluator.rs:411), building a full element map per
node, and this fix moved that row not at all (~280k before and after). `nodes(p)` is a RENDERING
cost and suppression cannot touch it.

### Why the count-only mode was not built yet

It needs the plan-global fact "the path is read, but only as a count" to reach
`varlen::shortest_path`. Three routes were considered and the cheap one was taken first:

- Widen `track: bool` to a mode enum — 133 sites take `track`, 36 use it as a bool. Mechanical
  but wide, and a wrong edit silently changes lineage behavior.
- A field on `Lineage` — only 4 struct literals, but 9 derivers (`seed`, `seed_edges`,
  `seed_steps`, `empty`, `push_step`, `gather`, `extend`, `extend_nodes`, `concat`) would each
  have to propagate it, and one that drops it suppresses values that a reader still wants.
- **A field on `Plan::ShortestPath`, set by the optimizer.** The decision is plan-global
  analysis, which is where that belongs, and it buys an oracle: `rewrite_fuzz` compares raw
  against optimized as multisets, so the raw plan (full lineage) and the optimized plan
  (count-only) must return identical rows.

  > **CORRECTION (item 42).** That oracle did NOT exist — the generator projected no path of
  > any kind, so it had never compared lineage at all. It has since been built and its teeth
  > proved in both directions. Also corrected there: the offsets-as-length-prefix-sum design
  > sketched below is UNSAFE and would have panicked, because `Lineage::gather` rebuilds values
  > through `path_at` regardless of what the plan reads.

The third is the intended route. Whichever is used, suppressing the value vectors makes
`offsets` stop being an index into `values` and become a length prefix-sum, so `path_at` /
`edges_at` must never be called in that mode — an invariant held only by the plan predicate.
`PathPart::Length` and `PathPart::Cardinality` should therefore read the offset DIFFS rather
than `edges.len()`, which is equivalent in both modes and makes the evaluator change
unconditional.

### Tests

Three in `exec/tests.rs`, all mutation-verified under `timeout` + `MemoryMax=16G -p
MemorySwapMax=0`:

- `the_buffer_reusing_chain_walk_matches_the_allocating_one` — every length 0..200 against the
  allocating form.
- `the_reused_chain_buffer_does_not_inherit_the_previous_row` — walks LONG then SHORT, which is
  the only ordering that exposes a missing `clear()`. Dropping `chain.clear()` fails this plus
  the match test plus two pre-existing `gql::tests` path tests; dropping `echain.clear()` fails
  six.
- `the_reused_chain_buffer_picks_the_same_branch` — on a branching predecessor DAG both forms
  must take `preds[cur][0]`, not an equally-short alternative. Taking the LAST predecessor
  instead fails this test and only this test.

## 40. `ANY SHORTEST` with a path projection OOM-killed the process at 6,000 nodes (2026-09-30)

An unbounded `MATCH p = ANY SHORTEST (x)-[:R]->*(y) RETURN path_length(p)` emits one row per
(source, reachable target) — quadratic in node count — and nothing bounded it. Measured on a
degree-3 fixture, peak RSS read from the cgroup's `memory.peak` per run:

| nodes |       rows | path elements | post-run RSS |           **true peak** |
| ----: | ---------: | ------------: | -----------: | ----------------------: |
| 1,000 |    945,058 |     5,706,847 |      44.7 MB |                  560 MB |
| 2,000 |  3,748,158 |    25,095,526 |     157.6 MB |                 2.41 GB |
| 4,000 | 15,072,270 |   110,513,050 |     611.5 MB |            **10.43 GB** |
| 6,000 |          — |             — |            — | **OOM-killed at 16 GB** |

Three things this measurement settled, each of which had been guessed wrong first:

- **Streaming was never the protection.** The intuition was that shortest path is intensive
  so it must be streamed. It is not: `streaming_chain` (exec.rs:2270-2385) accepts exactly
  eight plan nodes — `Scan`, `IndexSeek`, `RangeSeek`, `Filter`, `Expand`, `VarLength`,
  `Project`, `EdgeVertex` — and `ShortestPath`, `RepeatGroup` and `OptionalExpand` fall to
  `_ => None`. Shortest-path plans are MATERIALIZED. Nor would streaming have helped: the
  block loop accumulates with `acc.push` and concatenates, so peak includes the full result
  either way; it shrinks per-hop intermediates, and shortest path has none to shrink (its BFS
  state is per-input-row and dropped each iteration).
- **A post-hoc guard could not have worked.** `guard_intermediate` (exec.rs:2154) reads
  `batch.rows()` once the batch already EXISTS, which is fine where the batch is cheap and the
  risk is a later `read_property` — but here the batch IS the allocation that fails. The check
  has to be incremental.
- **Rows are the wrong unit, by 20:1.** At 4,000 nodes the query was 15.1M rows but 110.5M
  path elements, and peak tracked the elements (~94 bytes each), not the rows. Rows at the
  6,000-node kill were ~34M — BELOW the 50,000,000 default — so a row-counting guard would
  not have fired before the OOM at all. The guard's own message shows the split on the bench
  fixture: `2,397,360 rows plus 48,562,936 path elements`.

FIXED: `varlen::guard_path_growth`, checked once per input row at the top of the loop in BOTH
`shortest_path` and `shortest_k_path` (the `SHORTEST k` trail enumerator is a separate
function and needed its own call). Unit is rows + materialized path elements, charged against
the existing `intermediate` ceiling — one knob, already plumbed through
`ConfigId::LimitsIntermediate`, and counting values is as portable as counting rows (exact
integers, identical on 32- and 64-bit). `shortest_path`/`shortest_k_path` now return
`Result<Batch, String>`; `try_shortest_early_stop` returns `Option<Result<Batch, String>>`.

After: peak is bounded FLAT at ~1.98 GB at 2,000 / 4,000 / 6,000 nodes (vs 2.41 GB / 10.43 GB
/ OOM), and the 6,000-node case reports `E_RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED` instead of killing the process.

**Behavior change, deliberate:** 2,000 nodes now fails where it previously completed using
2.41 GB. Accepted — an unbounded all-pairs shortest path is quadratic and must fail at SOME
size; the only question is whether it fails cleanly, and 2.4 GB already aborts the wasm module
(32-bit) rather than erroring. The realistic shape is untouched: an anchored source over
**200,000 nodes** is 188,191 rows, 164 ms, 321 MB peak — 4.3M charged against a 50M ceiling.

**Native-only, consistent with precedent.** The pure-TS engine enforces no `intermediate`
ceiling anywhere, so a query that now throws on native still succeeds in pure-TS. That is the
pre-existing and settled shape of `guard_intermediate` (native/wasm anti-runaway, not
semantics) rather than a new divergence class — see [[wasm-oom-intermediate-guard]]. On the
CLI, which sets `intermediate` to 10,000,000 for wasm, this is strictly an improvement: a
catchable error instead of an `unreachable` that kills the REPL.

### The bench gap that let it hide

`ANY SHORTEST` had **zero** cases in the whole bench corpus — `grep -i shortest
examples/*.rs` matched one unrelated comment. Added `query/shortest` (support/query.rs),
including the unbounded case, which is expected to report `E_RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED` rather than a
time: if that row ever prints a number, the ceiling has stopped working. At 50,000 nodes,
degree 3, anchored source:

| shape                                          |                best_us |   rows | vs control |
| ---------------------------------------------- | ---------------------: | -----: | ---------: |
| anchored `count(*)`                            |                  6,162 |      1 |          — |
| **anchored + `y.name`** (lineage-free control) |              **6,785** | 47,007 |   **1.0x** |
| anchored + `path_length(p)`                    |                 30,321 | 47,007 |   **4.5x** |
| anchored + `nodes(p)`                          |                284,306 | 47,007 |    **42x** |
| both ends anchored (early stop)                |                 21,931 |      1 |          — |
| unbounded                                      | `E_RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED` |      — |    guarded |

The `y.name` row is the control, and it is why the table has one: comparing a path projection
against `count(*)` conflates lineage with output size, since `count(*)` returns one row and the
path rows return 47,007. Projecting an ordinary property over the SAME 47,007 rows costs
6,785 us — only 10% over `count(*)`. So output size is nearly free here, and the 4.5x and 42x
are lineage in full rather than partly row volume.

Also fixed a harness gap this exposed: `time_query` called `exec::run`, which `.expect()`s, so
one faulting plan panicked the whole benchmark binary mid-table. It now uses `try_run` and the
existing per-row error printing handles it.

### Open, next: `path_length(p)` pays 4.5x for a number it does not need

> **NOW FIXED — item 43 (2026-09-30), 3.13x, landing within 4% of a lineage-free control.**
> **PARTLY FIXED and PARTLY WRONG — see item 41 (2026-09-30).** The chain walk's per-row
> allocation was the cheap half and is fixed (1.22x). The diagnosis below is wrong in two
> places: suppressing the value vectors recovers under HALF the gap, not all of it, and
> `nodes(p)`'s 42x is a RENDERING cost (`path_node_values`) that suppression cannot touch.
> The real remaining prize is skipping the chain walk entirely, since `dist` already holds
> the hop count.

`needs_lineage` (exec/render.rs:440) is a single coarse bool, so `path_length(p)` — which
needs only an integer — pulls in full node AND edge lineage. Measured against the lineage-free
control, the 4.5x is entirely the cost of materializing chains that are then reduced to their
length, and the 42x for `nodes(p)` shows edges are materialized even when only nodes are read.

The shape of the fix is visible in `PathPart::Length => Value::Num(edges.len())`
(exec/evaluator.rs:416): `path_length(p)` reads only the LENGTH of the edge slice, so it needs
`edge_offsets` and neither value vector. Suppressing `values`/`edges` while still advancing the
offsets as virtual lengths would cost it nothing. That makes offsets no longer an index into
values, sound only because a plan predicate proves no reader exists — so it wants a `cfg(test)`
override and a suppressed-vs-full A/B, the same shape as `with_dense_cut`, `with_label_mode`
and `with_stream_threshold`. Splitting it into "needs nodes" /
"needs edges" / "needs only a count" should cut both, and raises the ceiling proportionally
for free. Not attempted yet.

### Tests, and one that could not have teeth

Seven tests in `exec/tests.rs` over a 60-node chain fixture. Mutation-verified under
`timeout` + `MemoryMax=16G -p MemorySwapMax=0`:

- neutering the `shortest_path` guard — caught by 4 tests;
- changing the unit to rows only — caught by 4, including
  `the_ceiling_counts_path_elements_not_just_rows`, which is the test that exists to pin the
  unit;
- removing the `shortest_k_path` guard — caught by exactly one,
  `the_shortest_k_traversal_is_guarded_too`.

A fourth mutant **survived**: making `try_shortest_early_stop` swallow the error and fall back
instead of propagating it. Poisoning the early-stop path with a `panic!` proved the test does
reach that code, so this is not the usual unreached-test trap — the behavior genuinely is not
observable. If the bounded early-stop BFS trips the ceiling, the general path accumulates
strictly more and trips it with the same message, so propagate-vs-swallow differs only in
doing bounded work twice. The test was renamed to
`the_endpoint_anchored_early_stop_is_also_guarded`, which is what it actually verifies, and
both it and the code comment now say propagation is an efficiency choice that no assertion
here can pin.

## 39. The streamed top projection had no coverage, and the mutants that test it can hang (2026-09-28)

`pull_top_output_streamed` projects in 8,192-row blocks and concatenates instead of
materializing — a win only once the fan-out is huge, so its gate is an ESTIMATE OF A MILLION
ROWS. The block loop and `concat_batches` had no coverage at all.

**CORRECTION (2026-09-29).** The first version of this entry, and the commit message of
`a10905a3`, said generated plans "never clear" the million-row gate. That is wrong, and the
real reason is more interesting. Measured: at 20,000 nodes **16 of 123** top-level projections
have estimates over 1M, the largest 3.7 BILLION; assertion injection immediately after the
gate fires in four tests, so execution does get past it.

What actually blocks it is the NEXT condition, `streaming_chain`, and the two are
ANTI-CORRELATED. An estimate that large comes from `ShortestPath` / `RepeatGroup` /
`OptionalExpand` fan-out — and those are exactly the operators `streaming_chain` has no arms
for. Qualifying by SIZE means carrying the shapes that disqualify you by STRUCTURE. Verified
by asserting `streaming_chain(..).is_none()` past the gate: it holds for every ordinary
generated plan, and fails only in the new test, which forces the threshold to 0 so that
small, structurally streamable chains take the path instead.

**That is also a question about the engine, not only the test.** If the only plans big enough
to clear the gate are ones `streaming_chain` cannot stream, the streamed projection may
rarely or never fire in production either — no generated plan that cleared the gate was
accepted. Generated plans are not real workloads, so this is a lead rather than a verdict:
worth measuring against a real query corpus before either lowering the threshold or deleting
the path.

- [x] **[med]** engine `exec.rs` + `opt/rewrite_fuzz.rs` — FIXED 2026-09-28. The threshold is
      overridable under `cfg(test)` (`with_stream_threshold`), and
      `the_streamed_and_materialized_projections_agree` runs 250 generated plans on a
      20,000-node graph with streaming forced on (`0.0`) and off (`f64::INFINITY`), comparing
      bags. Third instance of the same technique after the dense count frontier (item 37) and
      the `IsLabeled` implementations (item 38).

  The fixture is large enough to MATTER: a scan seeds ~18,000 ids, so the block loop runs
  three times and `concat_batches` really concatenates. The test asserts at least one result
  exceeds one block, or the concatenation would be untested.

  THREE MUTANTS, all caught by the new test ALONE:

  ```text
  the loop skips its final partial block      CAUGHT
  `start = end + 1` (drops an id per block)   CAUGHT
  the first id of each block is skipped       CAUGHT
  ```

### A mutant took the machine down, and the lesson is about mutant DESIGN (2026-09-28)

The first attempt at the block-boundary mutant was `min(ids.len())` → `min(ids.len() - 1)`.
That does not produce a wrong answer — it stops the loop ADVANCING. `start = end` where `end`
is capped at `len - 1` means once `start` reaches `len - 1`, `end == start` forever, and the
loop pushed a `Batch` per iteration until RAM was gone, then spent hours filling a 192 GB
swapfile with nothing killing it. The machine needed a hard reboot, and the reboot wiped the
`/tmp` backup, so the mutated line had to be reconstructed from `git diff`.

Two things came out of it, both now in `CLAUDE.md` under "Changing a planner rewrite":

1. **A loop bound, a `while` condition and an index cap are where an off-by-one stops being a
   wrong answer and becomes non-termination.** Check the advancement expression before
   running. For this loop the property is `end > start` strictly; the replacement mutant
   `start = end + 1` probes the same boundary and advances by 8,193.
2. **Every mutant runs under a timeout AND a memory cap**, because the two catch different
   failures — a non-allocating infinite loop never trips `MemoryMax`. Both were verified
   empirically on this box before being relied on: an allocator bomb dies in under a second
   with exit 137 and swap stays at 0 MB, a bare `while true` is killed with exit 124. The cap
   is 16G, sized from measurement rather than a round number — peak RSS is 1.86 GB for the
   whole suite and 1.53 GB for a 200k-seed sweep, so 16G is ~8x the real ceiling while
   leaving ~45 of this box's 61 GB free, which is what keeps the machine usable.

A THIRD SURVIVOR EXPLAINED, since two of the three original mutants did not change anything.
`end = start + BLOCK - 1` survived and SHOULD have: it makes blocks 8,191 wide instead of
8,192, and they still tile `[0, len)` exactly with no id skipped or repeated. Block size is
arbitrary. (The other non-result was a mis-applied mutation — an anchor that matched three
sites, and a shell that split the replacement on the `::` in `Batch::single`. A mutation that
did not apply reports as "survived" and means nothing; assert the anchor is unique and check
that the edit landed.)

## 38. The three `IsLabeled` implementations were never compared (2026-09-27)

A node-column `hasLabel` is served by one of three implementations — a tautology
short-circuit, a membership bitset, or a per-row bucket probe — chosen by a COST estimate
(`rows * 190` against `node_count / 4 + bucket * 3`, item 20).

Both the bitset and the probe ARE reached by the fuzz suite; assertion injection put 6 tests
on the bitset and 2 on the probe. So this is not a reachability gap, which is what made it
worth checking: the suite ran whichever the estimate picked and never compared them, so a
divergence would surface only on the graphs and frontier sizes that happen to select the
broken one. The crossover also MOVES with graph size — the allocation term is
`node_count / 4` — so one size passing is not evidence about another.

- [x] **[med]** engine `exec/evaluator.rs` + `opt/rewrite_fuzz.rs` — FIXED 2026-09-27. The
      implementation is forceable under `cfg(test)` (`with_label_mode`), and
      `the_three_is_labeled_implementations_agree` runs 400 generated plans on a 6,000-node
      graph down all three paths, comparing bags. A guard firing is fine as long as it fires
      for all three; one path succeeding where another fails is a failure.

  POISON-VERIFIED, and the probe is the one that needed it:

  ```text
  bitset off-by-one (`id > 0 && member[id]`)        caught by 4 tests, incl. existing ones
  probe off-by-one  (`id > 0 && bucket.contains`)   caught by the NEW test ALONE (750 pass)
  short-circuit ignores the u32::MAX sentinel       caught by an existing unit test
  ```

  The probe is the less-taken branch — 2 tests against the bitset's 6 — so a bug in it is
  exactly the kind that hides until a particular graph shape selects it. `exec/tests.rs`
  already compared both against `store.is_labeled` per row on a 2,000-node fixture at two
  frontier sizes; this is the generative counterpart, over plan shapes that carry frontier
  sentinels and multi-label nodes the hand-written cases do not.

  `exec::evaluator` is now `pub(crate)` so the test can reach the knob. Contained to the
  crate; the two `cfg(not(test))` shims keep the production decision byte-for-byte what it
  was.

## 37. The DENSE count frontier had no coverage at any graph size (2026-09-27)

`Counts` switches from a sparse list to a dense array at `max(n/16, 1024)` active nodes, and
its own doc claims the switch is byte-identical: "counts are exact integers (< 2^53), so the
f64 sums are order-independent and the representation switch is byte-identical". That claim
had NO test. Assertion injection at every dense entry point — the `Scan` arm's
`seed.len() > dense_cut`, the `Expand` arm's `est_next > dense_cut`, and `dense_from` for an
unlabelled scan — showed all of them unreached, at 24 nodes and at 20,000, and with 200 plans
at 20,000.

WHY, and it is a side effect of our own work: a dense frontier needs a LARGE active set, but
since the selectivity gate landed (item 18) a seek is by construction selective. So the only
remaining source of a large frontier is a bare `Scan` with no seedable predicate on the seed
— which optimization almost always turns into a seek. Making seeks selective made the dense
path nearly unreachable.

- [x] **[med]** engine `exec.rs` + `opt/rewrite_fuzz.rs` — FIXED 2026-09-27. The switch is now
      overridable under `cfg(test)` (`with_dense_cut`), and
      `the_sparse_and_dense_count_frontiers_agree` runs 600 generated plans with the cut
      forced to 0 (always dense) and to `usize::MAX` (always sparse), comparing the bags. A
      guard firing is fine as long as it fires on both sides; one side succeeding where the
      other fails is a failure.

  ALL FOUR DENSE ENTRY POINTS ARE NOW GUARDED, each verified by poisoning:

  ```text
  Expand dense scatter (`next[nbr] += c` -> `= c`)   caught by the NEW test alone
  Scan dense arm (seed count 1.0 -> 2.0)             caught by the NEW test alone
  seek arm (`+= 1.0` -> `= 1.0`)                     already caught by an exec unit test
  dense_from (skip an id)                            already caught by a gremlin test
  ```

  TWO MISTAKES ON THE WAY, both instructive:

  1. The first explicit plan was `count(*)` over `Expand(Scan)` with no frontier filter — which
     takes the degree-sum shortcut and never builds a `Counts` at all. So it compared two runs
     of code that was not under test. The `Scan`-arm poison surviving is what exposed it; the
     fix was adding the frontier `IsLabeled`, which is what routes to `try_frontier_count`.
  2. A poison that skipped the FIRST seed id survived, and that is not a coverage gap — that
     node has no `T1` out-edge, so the mutation was a genuine no-op. Re-poisoning with a
     value change (1.0 -> 2.0) caught it immediately. A surviving mutant needs its
     explanation checked before it is treated as a hole.

## 36. Hunt at 12k-50k nodes: no correctness bugs, one cost finding (2026-09-27)

With the shape and size axes in place, a hunt across 12,000 / 25,000 / 50,000 nodes x seven
shapes, raw against optimized.

NO DIVERGENCES. Twenty-one (size, shape) combinations, every one clean. That is the result,
and it is worth recording as a result rather than as an absence.

- [x] **[low]** engine `opt/rewrite_fuzz.rs` — a repetition cap for unbounded hops, and the
      route to it is a correction worth keeping. The sweep showed the "no deletions" shape at
      **31.0s against 2.3s** for the baseline at 50k nodes, same edge count. Fewer tombstones
      running 13x SLOWER looked like a real pathology.

  It is not one. Timing `check` per seed found the worst offender at **+2678ms but only 1.6x**
  — a `ShortestPath <- OptionalExpand <- OptionalExpand <- Scan` plan taking 4.4 SECONDS in
  absolute terms. So the cost is a handful of plans whose unbounded-hop fan-out explodes at
  scale, and the deletion rate is a mild multiplier on them (10% more live nodes, compounded
  over hops). The first read — "13x, counterintuitive, therefore a bug" — was wrong, and the
  ratio-vs-absolute distinction is what showed it.

  The generator already suppresses those hop kinds for long chains via `deep`. The same
  reasoning applies to graph SIZE, which chain length cannot see, so `with_rep_cap` lowers
  the repetition bound for the large-size tests. Measured 89.3s to 53.0s across the hunt, and
  it bought a FOURTH size in the permanent sweep (20,000 nodes) while the test got cheaper:
  4.65s for three sizes to 2.57s for four. The operators stay covered at scale — 1-2 reps
  instead of 1-3 — rather than being excluded, which is the point of testing at scale.

  A METHOD NOTE, because it cost a step: `frontier_counts`' dense-representation branches were
  first read as "reached" from a `grep` for the injected panic's message. The match was
  rustc's own UNREACHABLE-CODE WARNING quoting the source line, not a runtime panic. With
  `#[allow(unreachable_code)]` added, neither dense branch is reached by generated plans even
  at 9,000 nodes. Grep for the test VERDICT, not for the panic text.

## 35. Fuzzer: the sixteen "different" fixtures were structurally IDENTICAL (2026-09-27)

Raised by the user — fuzzing one 24-node graph gets you only so far. Measured first, and the
starting position was worse than the size problem in item 34:

```text
fixture(0)   live=22  Node=22 Half=11 Wide=7 Rare=3  age on 20  tags=3  edges=63
fixture(1)   live=22  Node=22 Half=11 Wide=7 Rare=3  age on 20  tags=3  edges=62
fixture(7)   live=22  Node=22 Half=11 Wide=7 Rare=3  age on 20  tags=3  edges=58
fixture(15)  live=22  Node=22 Half=11 Wide=7 Rare=3  age on 20  tags=3  edges=57
```

Identical in every structural dimension. Label density, property presence, tag cardinality,
degree and the deletion rate were all fixed functions of the node INDEX, so only the edge
wiring varied. `optimizing_preserves_rows_across_fixtures` claimed to guard against "a
rewrite that is only wrong at a particular density or label distribution" and varied neither.

- [x] **[med]** engine `opt/rewrite_fuzz.rs` — FIXED 2026-09-27. A `Shape` struct now carries
      every dimension that was fixed, and `SHAPES` holds eight hand-picked shapes, each
      named for the threshold it crosses: the historical baseline, universal `Half`, no
      tombstones, no absent properties, a tag column too high-cardinality to dict-encode, a
      single edge type, a hub, and a sparse graph where hops empty the frontier. Hand-picked
      rather than randomized because a random shape lands in the middle of every range, and
      the middle is where nothing switches over.

  `the_shapes_differ_in_the_dimensions_they_name` pins that they really do differ — live
  count, label density, property presence, degree spread, and `tag` being `Dict` in some and
  `Str` in others. It DISCRIMINATES: collapsing the shapes to copies of the baseline fails it
  ("no shape gives a label to every live node"). Without that guard the list can decay back
  into copies, which is exactly what the sixteen `fixture(n)` calls silently were.

  EQUAL TIME PER SHAPE, not equal plans, and the first two attempts at that were wrong:

  ```text
  equal plans                    39.7s   (the old 24-node sweep was ~2s)
  weighted by edge count         36.7s   a bad proxy — cost is fan-out per hop, not edges
  weighted by MEASURED cost       4.1s
  ```

  The measurement is why: the hub shape was 95.8 ms/plan against 0.1-0.2 for every other,
  because a 4-hop chain over degree 7 explodes. 600x the cost for one property is not a trade
  worth making, so the shape was tamed to degree 2-4 with a hub to every third node — still
  "one adjacency dominates", now 2.5 ms/plan — and the per-shape costs are recorded in the
  struct with a note to re-measure if a shape changes.

  WHAT THIS DOES NOT BUY, stated because the same honesty applies as in items 29 and 34: no
  poison was found that ONLY the shape sweep catches. Two were tried — skipping a node in the
  whole-graph `DISTINCT` loop, and in `scan_visit`'s whole-graph branch — and both were caught
  by hand-written tests while every fuzz test passed, because the generated plans do not
  route through those particular fast paths. The justification is that a test claiming
  variation it did not have now has it, with a guard against decay; not a named bug.

  ONE SHAPE'S RATIONALE WAS WRONG AND IS CORRECTED IN PLACE: "universal label" was added for
  the tautology short-circuits, but `Node` is on every node in every shape, so those were
  always reachable. What it actually varies is label DENSITY, which is what the `IsLabeled`
  bitset-vs-probe estimate weighs against the row count. Renamed and re-justified rather than
  left with a plausible-sounding reason that does not hold.

## 34. Fuzzer: all the row-checking ran BELOW the seek cutover (2026-09-27)

Raised by the user, and correct. `SEEK_FLOOR_NODES` (4,096) is not a tuning knob, it is a
regime change: below it `seek_beats_scan` returns `true` unconditionally so every seedable
predicate seeds, above it the gate decides and about half are declined (318 plans per 2,000
contain a seek at 24 nodes against 158 at 5,000). Plan counts before this change:

```text
optimizing_preserves_rows_with_indexes       25,000 plans    24 nodes
optimizing_preserves_rows_without_indexes    25,000 plans    24 nodes
optimizing_preserves_rows_across_fixtures    25,000 plans    24 nodes (16 label spreads)
across_block_boundaries                      40-400 plans    9,000 nodes
```

~75,000 plans below the cutover, ~400 above it, and real graphs are above it.

- [x] **[med]** engine `opt/rewrite_fuzz.rs` — `across_block_boundaries` is replaced by
      `optimizing_preserves_rows_across_sizes`, which sweeps three sizes and STRADDLES the
      floor: 4,400 raw nodes (4,000 live, just below), 5,000 (4,546, just above) and 9,000
      (8,182, well above).

  SIZES ARE CHOSEN BY LIVE COUNT, which is what the gate reads. The fixture deletes one node
  in eleven, so 4,000 raw is 3,637 live — it would sit on the wrong side of the cutover while
  looking like the right one. The straddle is ASSERTED, and the assertion discriminates:
  moving the sizes so none is below gives "0 below, 3 above" and fails.

  Budgets are per-size rather than shared, because cost is ~1.1us per node per plan —
  measured at 0.12ms/plan at 24 nodes, 5.1 at 5,000, 9.6 at 9,000, 22.5 at 20,000. Equal time
  per size, not equal plans; 4.6s at the default seed count, scaling with
  `LENKE_OPT_FUZZ_SEEDS`.

  WHAT IT DOES NOT BUY, and this is stated at the test rather than implied: a demonstrated
  correctness bug the 24-node tests miss. That was attempted — a poison that drops a conjunct
  when no seed is taken — and the 24-node tests caught it too, because the no-seed branch is
  shared with "the predicate was never seekable". Every property of the floor itself is a
  PERFORMANCE choice, so this oracle cannot judge it; item 32's decision test is what does.
  The sweep is diversity in a genuinely different planner regime, plus the host that item
  32 needs, plus the straddle guard. Not a guard with a name.

## 33. Fuzzer: the Expand-rooted density — measured and DECLINED (2026-09-27)

- [~] **[low]** engine `opt/rewrite_fuzz.rs` — raising the Expand-rooted-and-reseeded
  density above its 5 of 2,000 deterministic seeds. Attempted by making an intermediate
  predicate SEEKABLE half the time, which is what creates an interior pivot: measured
  **8/2000 against 5/2000**. Three more plans, for a generator change that widens every
  chain in the suite.

  Declined on the same grounds as the identical change measured earlier in this sweep
  (58/2000 against 54/2000 for splits generally, item 24): the shape is already
  poison-verified by two unit tests in `opt/tests.rs`, so extra fuzz plans would buy
  combinations those two lack, and three of them is not that. Recorded at the floor so the
  next reader has the number rather than the idea.

  That closes the fuzzer-coverage work. Six commits: items 28-33 plus the algo-fuzz and
  gremlin-fuzz ones. The durable output is as much the two negative findings — the
  raw-vs-optimized oracle's reach (item 29) and the five-fuzzer multi-label sweep coming back
  with one gap (item 31) — as the four additions.

## 32. Fuzzer: a second oracle that can judge a PERFORMANCE decision (2026-09-27)

Item 29 established that `optimizing_preserves_rows_*` is blind to any choice where both
branches are correct — and the seek-vs-scan gate is exactly that. Seeding an index and
leaving a `Filter(Scan)` return the same rows, so the gate from item 18 shipped with no
generative coverage: making `seek_beats_scan` return `true` unconditionally passes every
seed. This is the oracle that closes it.

- [x] **[med]** engine `opt/rewrite_fuzz.rs` —
      `a_seek_is_chosen_exactly_when_the_predicate_is_selective`, added 2026-09-27. It
      asserts the DECISION rather than the rows: across ~30 `(key, op, literal)` triples on
      the indexed keys, spanning one row in 9,000 up to nearly everything, a selective
      predicate must reach the index and an unselective one must not.

  WHY IT IS NOT A TAUTOLOGY, which was the whole risk. The obvious expectation is to ask
  `seed_fraction` what the selectivity is — the planner's own estimator — so the test would
  assert only that the planner agrees with itself, and a miscounting `range_count_capped`
  would satisfy both sides. Instead the true selectivity is MEASURED BY EXECUTION: run the
  UNOPTIMIZED `Filter(Scan)` and count its rows. That is ground truth by definition, touches
  none of `seed_fraction` / `range_count_capped` / `index_bucket_len`, and avoids
  reimplementing three-valued comparison in the test, where a mistake would look like an
  engine bug. The threshold constant is shared on purpose: `SEEK_MAX_FRACTION` is a policy
  number, not a computation, and it is the MEASUREMENT that has to be independent.

  It finally gives the 9,000-node fixture from item 29 a specific job: below
  `SEEK_FLOOR_NODES` the gate returns `true` unconditionally and there is no decision to
  check, so this test asserts the fixture clears the floor.

  FOUR POISONS, and the fourth is the one that justifies it:

  ```text
  seek_beats_scan -> always true      caught (also by 3 unit tests)
  seek_beats_scan -> always false     caught (also by 3 unit tests)
  the equality half of the gate gone  caught (also by 2 unit tests)
  seed_fraction wrong ONLY for 5-12%  caught by THIS TEST ALONE — 747 others pass
  ```

  The last one is the reach a sweep has and hand-picked cases do not: the unit tests use ~1%
  and ~20-49% selectivities, which straddle a mispriced middle band without entering it. A
  cap off by one, or an estimator wrong in a narrow range, lands there.

  A band of ±0.02 around the threshold is SKIPPED rather than asserted, because the cap is a
  `ceil` and a fraction sitting on the line can round either way; encoding that as an
  expectation would be encoding an off-by-one. The test asserts at most 4 cases land in the
  band, so the skip cannot quietly swallow everything.

## 31. Fuzzer: gremlin-fuzz dropped 7% of its plans as "unbuildable" (2026-09-27)

`gremlin-fuzz` reported `24-43/400 plans skipped: could not be built or emitted as text`
every run. Its own comment named the hazard — the `catch` conflates a kind that cannot cross
the text boundary BY DESIGN with a step the builder simply refuses to construct, and cites
`order(desc)` throwing for years while every directional plan was silently dropped. Grouping
the thrown messages found two causes:

```text
28x  planToGremlin: unsupported: step simplePath
 6x  range(1, 0) is not a valid window — the high bound must be >= the low bound
```

- [x] **[med]** gremlin `src/emit.ts` — FIXED 2026-09-27. `simplePath` and `cyclicPath` are
      niladic steps implemented on BOTH engines (TS in `executor/dispatch.ts`, native in
      `gremlin.rs`), and the ONLY thing that could not handle them was the emitter, which had
      no case and fell through to the generic `unsupported: step` error. Added to `NILADIC`.
      Three `emit.test.ts` cases pin it; removing the entry turns all three red.

- [x] **[low]** native `src/gremlin-fuzz.test.ts` — the generator produced
      `range(Math.floor(r()*2), Math.floor(r()*4))`, so `range(1, 0)` about 6 times per 400.
      The high bound is now derived from the low one. A zero-width window is kept on purpose:
      it is legal, and `ZERO_SLICE` has a principled reason for skipping those.

- [x] **[med]** native `src/gremlin-fuzz.test.ts` — THE DURABLE FIX. The blanket `catch` now
      CLASSIFIES with `isTsOnly`, which `emit.ts` exports for exactly this purpose ("so the
      runner can classify (tsOnly) vs surface (emitter gap)") and which the runner never
      used. A by-design boundary case — a closure, a non-finite literal — is a skip; anything
      else, including an emitter gap or a step the builder refuses, is now a reported FINDING.
      An `order(desc)`-shaped regression can no longer hide as a skip.

  RESULT: the unbuildable skip line is GONE — 0 per run, from 24-43, across several seeds.
  The only remaining skip is the principled positional-slice one (42-51/400).

  WHAT THE RECOVERED COVERAGE IMMEDIATELY FOUND, and how it was resolved: with
  `simplePath()` rendering, native returned `E_SYNTAX` where TS succeeded — because native
  supports these steps only "over a pure vertex-hop chain (V-source + out/in/both)" and says
  so with a deliberate coded error, while the generator was placing them after `inE`, inside
  `coalesce`, and after `path()`. That is a capability gap for the deferred-feature backlog,
  not something this fuzzer decides. So `simplePath` came OUT of the general step pool and a
  dedicated `pureVertexChain` shape (one plan in eight) generates it where both engines
  implement it — which is what gives the step real end-to-end coverage instead of noise.

  POISON-VERIFIED: swapping `simplePath`/`cyclicPath` semantics in `gremlin.rs` turns
  `gremlin-fuzz` red. Before this pass the same poison passed, because no plan containing
  either step ever reached an engine.

### The multi-label sweep of the remaining fuzzers — CLOSED (2026-09-27)

Item 30 closed the `algo-fuzz` gap; the same question asked of the other four:

- `codec-fuzz` already generates a label SET of 1-3, and its docstring says why: "Generating
  exactly one label made a set and its first element indistinguishable, which is how four
  codecs came to write only an edge's first type." Same bug class, already paid for there.
- `write-fuzz` barely filters by edge label (one occurrence) — it is about writes, so the
  shape buys nothing.
- `injection-fuzz` is about hostile strings being treated as DATA, not about label filtering.
  Hostile label TEXT is already covered by `codec-fuzz`'s `LABELS` (`has;semi`, `Ünïcode`).
- `backend-parity-fuzz` compares wasm against FFI — the SAME Rust source — so a multi-label
  bug affects both identically. It is structurally incapable of judging this class, for the
  same reason `rewrite_fuzz` is (item 29). Adding the shape there would be theatre.

So the class had exactly one gap and it is closed. Recorded so the sweep is not repeated.

## 30. Fuzzer: algo-fuzz could not generate the shape its own subject's bug needed (2026-09-27)

Item 29 established that the multi-label class needs a DIFFERENTIAL oracle. So: do the
TS-vs-native fuzzers have the shape? Three of four do, and the one that does not is the one
whose subject had the bug.

```text
gremlin-fuzz       an edge labelled ["KNOWS","CREATED"], added deliberately after a drift
differential-fuzz  an edge labelled ["E","F"]
algo-fuzz          labels: ["<one of ETYPES>"] — ALWAYS exactly one
```

`algo-fuzz`'s configs include `{ edgeLabel: pick(r, ETYPES) }`, so it filters by edge label
on every run — but never over an edge carrying more than one. Item 17's bug was exactly
that: the native algorithms compared `edgeLabel` against an edge's PRIMARY type while
queries and the TS engine consult the whole set. It was caught by a hand-written conformance
fixture; the generative side could not have caught it, and still could not.

- [x] **[med]** native `src/algo-fuzz.test.ts` — FIXED 2026-09-27. A quarter of generated
      edges now carry a SECOND label, drawn from the same `ETYPES` the configs pick from —
      so a config asking for `LIKES` really can hit an edge whose primary type is `KNOWS`.
      Drawing it from a disjoint set would exercise the shape without testing what it
      decides.

  POISON-VERIFIED against the original bug: restoring `adj_wanted` to
  `w == a.etype` — dropping the secondary-label consultation, which is item 17 exactly —
  turns `algo-fuzz` red, with 115 divergences against an expected 1. It passed that poison
  before this change.

  A FLOOR at `> 5` multi-label edges per run, shown to discriminate: removing the shape
  takes it to 0 and the test fails on the floor rather than passing silently over the bug it
  exists to catch. At a quarter of edges across 25 graphs the real count is dozens, so the
  floor cannot flake.

  A PROCESS NOTE worth keeping: the first stability run used `bun run fuzz algo --no-build`
  and reported five consecutive failures, which looked like the new shape being flaky. It
  was the poisoned `.so` still in place — `--no-build` skips the rebuild, which is precisely
  the stale-artifact trap `CLAUDE.md` documents for `backend-parity-fuzz`. Rebuilt, all
  seeds pass. Do not use `--no-build` while mutating Rust.

## 29. Fuzzer: the oracle's reach is "things the optimizer rewrites" (2026-09-27)

Chasing the sub-`SEEK_FLOOR_NODES` gap turned into a finding about what
`optimizing_preserves_rows_*` can and cannot judge. That reframes the remaining fuzzer
work, so it is written down before any more of it.

THE STRUCTURAL LIMIT. The oracle compares a plan against its optimized self. It can
therefore see a bug ONLY where the two sides differ — where the optimizer changed
something, or where a fast path it routed to disagrees with the enumeration the other
side does. It is blind to:

- a bug in a primitive BOTH plans share (item 28's multi-label probe);
- a bug in a node the optimizer does not rewrite, which appears identically on both
  sides (`DistinctBy`, below);
- any PERF-only decision, since both branches are correct by construction.
