**TinkerPop ground truth** (Modern graph; software vertices lop/ripple have no `age`):

| step                           | `…by('age')` result                            | disposition                   |
| ------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------- |
| `order()`                      | 4 persons (software dropped)                   | FILTER traverser              |
| `group().by('age').by('name')` | `{27:[vadas],29:[marko],32:[josh],35:[peter]}` | FILTER                        |
| `groupCount().by('age')`       | `{27:1,29:1,32:1,35:1}`                        | FILTER                        |
| `dedup().by('age')`            | 4 persons                                      | FILTER                        |
| `select('a').by('age')`        | `[29,27,32,35]`                                | FILTER                        |
| `project('a').by('age')`       | `[{a:29},{a:27},{},{a:32},{},{a:35}]`          | OMIT the key (keep traverser) |

**Current lenke behavior** (both engines still keep the absent element; several also diverge):

| step           | lenke TS                       | lenke native                | vs TinkerPop                                |
| -------------- | ------------------------------ | --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- |
| `order()`      | 6 (unfiltered)                 | 6 (unfiltered)              | both wrong (should be 4); TS≡native         |
| `group()`      | key `"undefined"` for software | key `"null"` for software   | both wrong; TS≠native                       |
| `groupCount()` | `undefined:2`                  | `null:2`                    | both wrong; TS≠native                       |
| `dedup()`      | 5 (nulls → one group)          | 6 (both kept)               | both wrong; TS≠native                       |
| `select()`     | 6 (two `null`s)                | 6 (two `null`s)             | both wrong; TS≡native                       |
| `project()`    | `{}` for software ✓            | `{"a":null}` for software ✗ | TS already correct; native wrong; TS≠native |

**Implementation shape:** give `evalBy` a distinct `NO_VALUE` sentinel (a unique symbol in TS; an
`Option`/distinct marker in native) returned when the projection yields nothing — an absent property
(`key in props` false in TS; `has_prop`/`present_at` false in native) OR a by sub-traversal emitting
zero rows. A stored present-`null` still returns `null` (kept). Then: order/group/groupCount/dedup/
select FILTER traversers whose key is `NO_VALUE`; `project` OMITS that key. Audit ALL ~15 `evalBy`
callers (sack/math/simplePath/path/select-in-map/tree must not leak the sentinel). Mirror exactly in
native. Verify with per-step cross-engine tests (absent AND present-null cases), then the gremlin
fuzzer + `spelling_probe`.

# Sixth pass — 2026-09-05 (parallel subsystem sweep: robustness + cross-engine)

Five parallel explorer agents over GQL, Gremlin, sync/CDC, serialization/codecs, and the
native/wasm/Rust boundary — briefed on the byte-identity invariant + the settled won't-fix list.
Each finding below was verified (by me, or by the explorer running both engines).

### Fixed 2026-09-05 (the three robustness/crash items)

- **[HIGH] flat operator/step chains SIGSEGV** — `RETURN 1+1+…` / `g.V().out()×N` built an N-deep
  left-nested tree that overflowed the stack in a later recursive pass (uncatchable — not a Rust
  panic). The 128-depth guards only covered recursive re-entry; the iterative precedence loops +
  linear Gremlin step loop now charge the depth budget → `E_RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`.
  (`fix(engine): bound flat operator/step chains`).
- **[HIGH] `parse_json` unbounded recursion SIGSEGV** — deeply-nested params / mergeNdjson /
  snapshot / algo-config values now capped at `MAX_JSON_DEPTH` on both the Json and Value descents.
  (`fix(engine): cap JSON parse recursion depth`).
- **[MED] `guarded()` coverage gap** — the panic backstop now wraps parse/optimize/merge/algo/schema,
  not just exec (host-abort → coded error; native/wasm parity). (`fix(engine): extend the FFI panic
backstop …`). Latent — no currently-reachable panic there; the real crashes were the two above.

### WON'T FIX (user, sixth pass)

- **`__proto__` property silently dropped by codecs** — a FEATURE (prototype-poisoning defense), not
  data loss.
- **The whole prototype-chain / reserved-property-key class** (`toString`/`valueOf`/`key in obj`
  crashes) — not a real-world input; settled won't-fix. Do not re-flag (supersedes the §1 "headline"
  framing).

### Remaining — needs a direction decision (verified divergences, not yet built)

GQL (TS ⇄ native): scalar-function **arity** unvalidated in TS (`abs(1,2)`, `date()` — native rejects
at parse; TS ignores/nulls — native is right, systematic TS fix); top-level inline `CALL { … }`
subquery (TS accepts, native rejects); `MATCH p = <fixed-length pattern> RETURN p` (native accepts,
TS rejects — intentional TS restriction); `CALL` body must start with `MATCH` in native; `collect()`
alias (native only); unknown-procedure code (`E_UNSUPPORTED` vs `E_SYNTAX`).

Gremlin (TS ⇄ native): **`planToGremlin` drops `Scope.local`** on sum/min/max/mean/count → silent
wrong native answers (emitter bug; also blinds the fuzzer); `project().by(values(k)/count()/
constant())` rejected by native only (same family as the earlier `by(constant)` note); `min`/`max`
cross-type (native throws, TS + native `order()` total-order); native forbids a read step after a
write (`addV().id()`); `math('1/0')` throws native / null TS (GQL blast radius); pre-`emit()` form
native-rejected; `tree()` map-key serialization differs. Plus: the emitter can't render addV/addE/
drop/tree/property/hasKey/local-scope aggs, so the differential fuzzer is blind to them.

Sync/CDC: **[HIGH]** a resolved loader whose writes fail to apply wedges the collection in `loading`

- unhandled rejection (`engine.ts` `.then(onF,onR)` — the mutate throw escapes onR); **[MED]** CDC
  gap silently skips writes when `onResync` is omitted; no way to seed `writeCursor` for snapshot
  warm-boot + CDC resume; **[low]** query-with-mutating-text bypasses dedup/CDC, onDisconnect cleanup
  bypasses scope routing.

Serialization: pg-text raw `TypeError` on a nested-list value (sibling codecs throw coded
`E_UNSUPPORTED`); CSV short-row TS-rejects / Rust-absorbs; GraphSON foreign edges without `id`
collapse to one. (Arrow IPC + value round-trips across all five codecs: checked clean.)

GQL depth-limit residual: a flat expr chain of exactly 127–~10k operators — TS accepts (flat AST,
configurable ceiling), native rejects at the depth cap. Both reject far beyond it; pathological only.

### Fixed 2026-09-05 (sixth-pass, second batch — the clear cross-engine/correctness ones)

- **[HIGH] sync loader wedge** — `load().then(onOk,onErr)` couldn't catch a throw while
  APPLYING the loaded writes (invalid GQL / constraint / warm-boot `_MERGE`), so the
  collection stuck in `loading` forever + an unhandled rejection. Now `.then(onOk).catch`
  → the same error/retry path. (`fix(sync): a load whose writes fail to apply errors …`).
- **[HIGH] Gremlin emitter dropped `Scope.local`** — sum/min/max/mean/count emitted bare,
  silently turning a local aggregation global on the native round-trip. Moved to a
  scope-aware `emitScopedAgg`. (`fix(gremlin): planToGremlin keeps Scope.local …`).
- **[MED] pg-text nested-list raw `TypeError`** → coded `E_UNSUPPORTED`, like csv/records.
- **[MED] `collect()` alias** — TS now accepts it as `collect_list` (native already did).

### Fixed 2026-09-05 (seventh-pass — the driven-by-fuzzer + capability-parity batch)

- **[HIGH] GQL scalar-function arity** — TS read scalar args positionally, silently
  dropping an extra arg (`abs(x,1)`) or reading a missing one as null (`mod(x)`); native
  rejects the wrong count in its parser, before any row runs. TS now checks arity EAGERLY
  at compile against `scalarArityOk`, a byte-exact mirror of native's table, throwing
  `E_SYNTAX`. Drove it with a new wrong-arity generator in the differential fuzzer (every
  function verified, not hand-checked). (`fix(gql): reject wrong scalar-function arity …`).
- **[MED] `TRIM(chars FROM s)` desugar** — the ISO char-set BOTH/default form desugared
  to a 2-arg `trim`, which the new arity check (correctly, matching native) rejects — the
  `trim` function is strictly 1-arg, the 2-arg both-trim is `btrim`. Now desugars to
  `btrim`. A regression the arity fix exposed. (`fix(gql): TRIM(chars FROM s) … btrim`).
- **[MED] named path over a fixed-length pattern** — `MATCH p = (a)-[e]->(b) RETURN p`
  was TS-rejected (a named path needed a selector or a single var-length segment); native
  accepts it. TS now materializes the fixed-length walk as a Path (parser allows all-fixed
  patterns; `orient` is skipped when a path var is bound so the Path is built in DECLARED
  direction). Only a mixed fixed/var-length named path stays rejected.
  (`feat(gql): bind a named path over a fixed-length pattern`).

- **[MED] Gremlin `project().by(values/count/constant)`** — native rejected any `by()`
  body that was not a key/id/label token or a single navigating hop; TS accepts the bare
  `values('k')`/`count()`/`constant(x)` forms (standard TinkerPop). The native
  `project_by_body` parser now accepts them too (values → property, count → 1, constant →
  literal). (`feat(engine): project().by(values/count/constant) bare bodies`).
- **[MED] Gremlin `math()` divide-by-zero** — TS silently produced Infinity/NaN (→ null at
  egress) while native throws `E_INVALID_VALUE`; GQL arithmetic already throws on BOTH
  engines, so `math()` was the lone outlier. TS `math()` now throws on a zero divisor for
  `/` and `%`. (`fix(gremlin): math() division/modulo by zero throws E_INVALID_VALUE`).

**Verified NON-issue:** CSV short-row (a row with fewer columns than the header) — BOTH
engines absorb it identically (missing column → property omitted). The sixth-pass note
"TS-rejects / Rust-absorbs" had the direction wrong; only a trailing-newline formatting
nuance differs on NDJSON _serialize_, unrelated to the deserialize behavior.

### Fixed 2026-09-06 (CALL grammar — resolved by the ISO BNF, not by convenience)

- **[MED] `CALL` scope clause + subquery MATCH are optional** — the ISO GQL grammar
  (`ISO_IEC_39075(en).bnf.txt`) settles it: `<inline procedure call> ::= [ <variable
scope clause> ] <nested procedure specification>` and `<ambient linear query statement>
::= [ <simple linear query statement> ] <primitive result statement>` — BOTH the
  `(scope)` and the body's MATCH are bracketed (optional). So bare `CALL { … }` and a
  MATCH-less `RETURN` body are valid ISO; TS accepted them, native rejected. Native now
  accepts, byte-identical: bare/empty scope, MATCH-less body (incl. set-op arms), and the
  top-level inline form (seeded from one unit row). (`feat(engine): CALL scope clause +
subquery MATCH are optional`). Residual: a top-level `CALL { … }` with NO outer RETURN
  and a scalar-aggregate inner RETURN — TS []→ native rejects; ill-defined corner, own triage.

### Triaged 2026-09-06 (the remaining "small" items)

- **emitter-coverage pass (addV/tree/drop)** — DONE 2026-09-06. `planToGremlin` now
  renders `addV`, `tree`, and `drop` (native already parsed them), unblocking cross-engine
  testing of the write/tree families. It immediately surfaced a **silent-wrong-answer bug**:
  `tree()` built a nested JS `Map` → `JSON.stringify(Map)` is `{}`, so every tree
  serialized empty; the old tree.test.ts asserted the Map internals and never saw it.
  FIXED — `treeStep` builds nested plain objects keyed exactly as native keys them
  (element `toJSON()` string, or the by-value), byte-identical bar the unspecified V()
  root-key order. (`feat(gremlin): emit addV/tree/drop + fix tree() …`).
  - **`drop()`**: byte-identical (verified). ✓
  - **read-after-write (`addV('T').id()`)**: FIXED 2026-09-06 — native was too strict
    (user call). TinkerPop treats addV as non-terminal, so a read step now folds into the
    existing `InsertReturn` (created node at slot 0), matching TS. Byte-identical for
    `label()` / set-then-read `property()`; `id()` agrees in shape only (id value is
    engine-assigned). `drop()` stays terminal. (`feat(engine): allow a read step after
addV …`).
  - **`addE`**: RECONCILED 2026-09-06. Native `addE` was a parse-time-static,
    numeric-id-only construct that did not work on string-id graphs; it is now a
    per-traverser step (`Plan::AddEdgeStep`) creating one edge per row with runtime
    endpoints (current traverser / `V('id')` / as()-tag), byte-identical to TS on
    from/to/labels/properties (edge id is engine-assigned, excluded). Missing endpoint →
    `E_MISSING_VERTEX` on both. Emitter half `df383dc1`; native `7589eaca`. Deferred (TS
    superset, documented): a read/`property()` AFTER addE (edge read-after-write) and a
    mid-traversal `.V()` re-scan endpoint.
  - Process note: the native addE pass, by running the full `gremlin_ported` integration
    suite, caught two ported `rejects()` cases that had silently flipped under the earlier
    addV-read-after-write (`8131609a`) and `project().by(constant)` (`99a7d7cb`) fixes —
    those commits ran the lib + TS-conformance gates but not `cargo test --test
gremlin_ported`. Add that to the pre-push gate list.
- **unknown-procedure code** — LEAVE AS-IS. TS is the more-correct engine: `E_UNSUPPORTED`
  (a catalog miss is not a syntax error) PLUS a "did you mean" suggestion; native reports
  `E_SYNTAX` only because its parser maps every String error to syntax. Both reject (no
  result divergence, not fuzzer-caught). Aligning would degrade TS or need an invasive
  native parser refactor — not worth it for a cosmetic code difference.
- **GraphSON edge-id collapse** — deferred codec-parity area (see [[shared-codec-crate]]);
  not re-verified this pass. **min/max cross-type** is settled won't-fix.

## Deferred-feature backlog (Gremlin/GQL "flips the day the feature lands") — 2026-09-06

A repo-wide sweep (Rust `rejects()` markers + `#[ignore]`, TS `.skip`/`toThrow`/tsOnly)
enumerated the genuinely-unbuilt features (distinct from settled won't-fix: NaN, cross-type
compare, aggregation-over-elements, bigint, prototype-key). Working the Gremlin write/read
family; each flips the relevant `rejects()`/marker to a positive assertion + adds a
gremlin-conformance case, verified byte-identical.

### Done this pass (the tractable Gremlin read/write family — all byte-identical)

- **addE** per-traverser + endpoints + `E_MISSING_VERTEX` (`7589eaca`, emitter `df383dc1`).
- **addE(...).property(k, literal)** folds inline props onto the created edge (`8765747b`).
- **addV** per-traverser + bare `addV()` + inline props (`bec291d9`).
- **path().by(a).by(b)** multiple by() cycle positionally (`ed799aa7`).
- **has(k, not(<pred>))** negated predicate — `not(within)`≡`without`, etc. (`62f29112`).
- **E()-source path()** — CONFIRMED already working; `path().id()`/`.label()` faults are
  PERMANENT (id/label on a path is a type error, both engines), not a deferred feature.
- **read-after-write on per-step addE/addV** (`af81e6fa`), **OLAP `.with(edges,'L')`**
  (`5c083f1a`), **trim(s, null) → null** (`2319a47f`, a real byte-identity divergence the
  wrong-arity fuzzer surfaced).
- **map(<pure write>)** — `map(addV/addE)` lowers to the per-traverser write step (the one
  container that reduces to an existing write); byte-identical (`337dd856`).

### Container-body mutations — DONE (2026-09-07); write-aware execution model shipped

Delivered the write-aware container model (`d8625e13` map/repeat, `a1a8aefb` union/choose),
byte-identical to TS on the tested shapes:

- **`write_frontier(plan, &mut store)`** — the write-side counterpart of `pull`: runs a write
  step (or nested write chain) and returns the CREATED elements as a frontier batch. Handles
  `AddVertexStep`, `AddEdgeStep` (EdgeEnd Slot/ExtId, `E_MISSING_VERTEX` on unresolved), and
  `Union` (concat same-type arms; mixed addV/addE deferred); read inputs fall through to `pull`.
- **`map(<write>)`** — lowered to the per-traverser write step. DONE.
- **`repeat(addV('L')).times(N)`** — a dedicated parse branch building N chained AddVertexSteps
  (native `RepeatGroup` is edge-hop-only and can't carry a write body). Write-feeding-write.
- **`union(addV('A'), addV('B'))`** — a `Plan::Union` of AddVertexSteps sharing the input; each
  arm runs over the same input, created elements concatenate.
- **`choose(cond, addV('X'))`** (no else) — lowered to `Union { write over filter(guard),
passthrough over filter(not guard) }`; matching rows get a created vertex, the rest pass through.
- **`is_write` is now recursive** (`Union => is_write(left) || is_write(right)`) so
  `run_query`/the FFI route write-Unions to `execute` rather than the immutable pull path — the
  fix that made `gremlinJson` (not just direct `execute`) return the created elements.

DOCUMENTED LIMITATION — **scan-source write-union re-scan**: `V().union(addV, addV)` re-scans
between arms (each arm's input is re-pulled), so a scannable-label source can see the previous
arm's writes and over-count. The tested cases use `V('1')` / label-guarded sources where both
engines are finite and agree. A snapshot-once `WriteUnion` (pull the input frontier once, feed
both arms) is the follow-up for scan sources. The general write-while-scan hazard
(`V().map(addV(<scannable label>))`) INFINITE-LOOPS in TS (lazy `V()` sees new vertices) — same
class, same avoidance (fixed / label-guarded sources are finite on both engines).

EMITTER: `planToGremlin` now emits `map(<sub-plan>)` too (added with navigating-map), so
navigating-map conformance is authored as TS-builder plans; the older `map(write)` cases stay
native-text-vs-TS-builder (unchanged, still valid).

Ported tests flipped from `rejects()` to positive: `p3_subplan_repeat_body`,
`p3_subplan_map_body`, `p4_mut_repeat_*` (×3), `p4_mut_map_addv_property`, `p4_mut_adde_repeat_smoke`,
`p4_mut_union_addv`, `p4_mut_choose_gates_addv` (`p4_mut_drop_inside_choose` was already positive).

### DONE (2026-09-09) — TS↔native GQL surface divergences (native under-accepted)

A whole class of byte-identity divergences where the native GQL parser REJECTED valid
ISO-GQL that the TS engine ACCEPTS — invisible because the differential fuzzer never
generated these shapes (the same blind spot that hid the rel-var-on-var-length divergence).
Method each time: extend the differential fuzzer to generate the shape (watch it flag the
divergence), make native accept it, verify byte-identical across many random-seed runs.

- **Inline element-pattern predicate `(n WHERE …)`** in every position that rejected it —
  a continuing MATCH's bound start, a shortest-path SOURCE and ENDPOINT node, a CALL-subquery
  start (`8a928eef`, plain filters), and an OPTIONAL MATCH LANDING node (`58c4927b`, which
  needed a new `OptionalExpand.landing_pred` + two-pass exec so a source whose neighbours all
  fail the predicate NULL-fills rather than dropping). Also the inline-props `(n {k:v})` form.
- **Compound label on a CALL fresh-scan start** (`:T&U`) and **uncorrelated `OPTIONAL CALL
{ … }`** (left-outer cross-join via `NullPadIfEmpty`) — `c8a26578`.
- **Mixed hop directions / edge types in a subpath group** (`((x)-[:E]->(m)<-[:F]-(y)){n,m}`)
  — `b6842466`. Routed to the per-hop `GElem::Hop` nested-group machinery (the uniform
  `RepeatGroup` can't represent a non-uniform unit) via a parse-and-rewind fallback.

The differential fuzzer now generates ALL of these (plus `IS TYPED RECORD`, agg-in-`CALL{}`,
named-path-over-subpath — which turned out already byte-identical; the audit's "not supported"
grep over-counted by matching messages on unrelated code paths). Verified across 600k+ queries
total. This closes the surveyed native-under-accepts divergence class.

### DONE (2026-09-08) — the tractable deferred items

- **`fail([message])` step** — DONE (`d866ebd1`). New `Plan::Fail` read barrier: throws
  `E_FAIL` when a traverser reaches it, empty stream passes through. New shared error code
  `E_FAIL`; FFI whitelist + TS `LenkeError`; emitter case. Also handles `<write>.fail()`.
- **`map(<navigating traversal>)`** — DONE (`79373dec`). Reuses the per-element Coalesce
  machinery with a single `limit(1)` arm (first-result-per-element, drop empties) — no new
  IR variant, no DistinctBy value-collision hazard. Emitter now emits `map(<sub-plan>)`.
- **`path()` after nested `optional()`** (and any branch nested inside a branch arm) — DONE
  (`9151d69e`). Extracted the per-element routing into a shared `per_element_branch_exec`
  called by both `pull` and a new `pull_body` `PerElementBranch` arm, so a branch inside an
  arm evaluates identically and lineage threads through two branches.
- **otherV-after-branch-rehop-over-E()-scan lineage bug** (fuzz seeds 2977/3934) — FIXED
  (`ae6fa691` un-ignores the test); rode in on the branch/lineage refactors above. The last
  gremlin-fuzz residual is closed.
- **Relationship variable on a variable-length pattern** — DONE (`4c2193fd`). The flat
  `MATCH (a)-[r:R]->{1,2}(b)` spelling now binds `r` to the LIST of the walk's edges — in
  BOTH engines, byte-identical to each other and to the already-working subpath spelling
  `((x)-[r:R]->(m)){1,2}`. Fixed a two-way equivalent-spellings divergence (native rejected,
  TS left `r` unbound). Contract matches the subpath form: `size(r)`/`r[i].w` work, a bare
  `r.w` on the list is null. Native routes the flat quantified `bind` branch through the same
  `RepeatGroup` group-edge-list lowering; TS compiles the flat quantified rel-with-variable
  to a single-hop unit so the edge var is exposed.

### Still open — GQL, low-priority polish (deferred, 2026-09-08)

- **Mid-query `<named procedure call>` in the linear pipeline** — `call_named_form_is_deferred`.
  Top-level `CALL name(args) YIELD` already works (the algorithms catalog) and correlated
  inline `CALL (scope) { … }` subqueries already work; the only gap is a NAMED procedure call
  appearing as a `<call query statement>` MID-pipeline (after a `MATCH`, args referencing outer
  bindings). Deferred, well-justified: mid-query named-`CALL` is not table-stakes for ISO-GQL —
  Microsoft Fabric Graph (a GA'd ISO-GQL implementation) ships with NO `CALL` at all (its whole
  statement set is MATCH/LET/FILTER/ORDER BY/OFFSET/LIMIT/RETURN), so lenke already EXCEEDS the
  reference implementations on procedure support. Revisit only on a concrete use case
  (source-parameterized procedures — rooted pagerank, single-source shortest path — are the real
  per-row case).

  GRAMMAR NOTE (verified against the ISO BNF + TuGraph ANTLR grammar): the ISO
  `<named procedure call>` is `name(args) [YIELD …]` with **NO** `<variable scope clause>` — the
  `(scope)` clause attaches ONLY to the inline `{ }` form. A named call correlates via its
  ARGUMENT expressions referencing in-scope binding variables + linear composition, not via a
  scope clause (and not via Cypher's positional per-row rule — this engine targets ISO-GQL). See
  [[iso-gql-reference]].

## 89. Ordering a list of elements answered where it should fault (2026-10-03)

The first of the two divergences item 88's new `order(local)` arm found. An element has no natural
order, and the global `order()` has known that for a long time — `order_page` takes a
`fault_on_element` flag and raises "order() over graph elements is not supported" — but
`order(local)` never got it. TS raised; native answered.

### Ground truth first, and it narrowed the fix

`tinkerpop/gremlin-console`, `createModern()`:

|                                                             | TinkerPop                                          |
| ----------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- |
| `g.E().fold().order(Scope.local)`                           | THROWS `ClassCastException`                        |
| `g.V().fold().order(Scope.local)`                           | THROWS `ClassCastException`                        |
| `g.V().values('name').fold().order(Scope.local)`            | sorts                                              |
| **`g.V().fold().order(Scope.local).by('name')`**            | **SORTS** — `[v[4], v[3], v[1], v[6], v[5], v[2]]` |
| `g.V().groupCount().by('name').order(Scope.local)`          | THROWS                                             |
| `g.V().groupCount().by('name').order(Scope.local).by(keys)` | sorts                                              |

The fourth row is why a blanket fault would have been wrong: a `by` PROJECTION rescues an element
list. Native cannot express that one at all — `order(local).by('name')` is a parse error ("expected
asc/desc, got `name`") — so there is no shape where native must sort elements, and faulting is both
safe and complete for what it can say. That gap is recorded below rather than papered over.

Where all three stood before the fix:

| case                       | TinkerPop | native          | TS                 |
| -------------------------- | --------- | --------------- | ------------------ |
| element list, no `by`      | throws    | **answers**     | throws             |
| element list `.by('name')` | sorts     | **parse error** | —                  |
| `values(…).fold()` list    | sorts     | sorts           | sorts              |
| Map, no `by`               | throws    | **answers**     | **sorts by value** |
| Map `.by(keys)`            | sorts     | sorts           | sorts              |

Only the FIRST row is both a TS/native divergence and a TinkerPop violation, so that is the whole
of this item. The Map-without-`by` row is a deviation BOTH engines share, so it is a conformance
question about the pair rather than a byte-identity one — changing native alone would introduce a
divergence to fix a conformance gap.

### The fix, and what the first attempt missed

`sort_local_key_is_element` checks the keys ACTUALLY USED — a Map sorted `by(keys)` is orderable
however unorderable its values are — and the `SortLocal` path raises the global path's exact message
before sorting anything, at both sites (`pull` and the `pull_body` twin).

The first version checked for `Value::Node`/`Value::Edge` and the fault never fired, because by the
time `fold()` has built the list the elements are RENDERED: the cells are
`{id, labels, properties}` maps, not refs. Both forms reach a locally-sorted list — a `Gen` cell
from a branch carries the unboxed ref — so the check uses `vertex_map_ext_id`/`edge_map_ext_id`,
the repo's existing test for the rendered form, as well as the ref match.

One message, two moments: the global spelling is rejected at PARSE time (`g.V().order()` never
runs), the local one only at runtime, because whether a folded list holds elements is not knowable
from the text. `order_local_over_a_list_of_elements_faults` asserts the two strings are equal so
they cannot drift.

### Three lines in a 54-arm match cost 1.21x on an unrelated query

The first working version wrote the check INLINE in the `SortLocal` arm. The sweep then flagged
`query/gql|filter age>50` — `MATCH (p:Person) WHERE p.age > 50 RETURN count(*)`, a counting query
with **no local sort anywhere in it** — and it was not noise. Interleaved A/B, stash → run → pop →
run, three rounds:

| round | before | after |
| ----- | -----: | ----: |
| 1     |  309.0 | 367.9 |
| 2     |  303.8 | 367.3 |
| 3     |  309.8 | 367.9 |

Tight and non-overlapping: a real, reproducible **1.21x**, with no logical mechanism. Moving the
check behind a `#[cold] fn` in `aggregation.rs` did not help (360-368), which ruled out inline code
SIZE. What localised it was deleting only the two CALL SITES and keeping the new functions:
`filter age>50` returned to 297-310. So the cost was not the work and not the called code — it was
the three lines' presence in `pull`'s match, which has 54 arms and in which added code moves every
other arm's layout.

FIXED by going the other way: the whole `SortLocal` body — pass-through guard, element check, sort,
slot assembly — is hoisted into `sort_local_batch` / `sort_local_body` / `sort_local_slot`, and each
arm is now a single call. `pull` ends up SMALLER than before the check existed.

| `query/counts|filtered` (the same query, in the counts table) | before | after |
|---|--:|--:|
| three interleaved rounds | 308.8 / 297.2 / 304.3 | 298.0 / 297.0 / 298.4 |

Flat, marginally faster. The lesson generalises past this item: in `pull`, WHERE code goes is a
performance decision on its own, independent of what the code does, and the arm is the wrong place
for anything that is not the dispatch itself.

### Teeth

| mutant                                                              | caught by                                                                                                       |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| only the unboxed ref is detected, not the rendered element map      | `order_local_over_a_list_of_elements_faults` — this is the bug the first attempt had                            |
| the check ignores `by_key` and faults on EITHER side of a Map entry | `the_local_sort_element_check_reads_only_the_keys_it_will_use`, and nothing else                                |
| the hoisted guard passes through ALWAYS, so nothing is ever sorted  | 4 tests, incl. `order_local_still_sorts_a_real_collection` and `gremlin::tests::order_local_by_desc_on_numbers` |

The second needed a Map with element VALUES and non-element KEYS, which no query in the Gremlin
surface builds (`group().by(k)` gives `key -> [elements]`, a list value), so the helper is tested
directly — the items 81 and 86 pattern. The third is the refactor's own guard: it confirms the hoist
preserved item 87's narrowing rather than widening it back out.

### The withheld fuzzer arm is back

Item 88 held back `[fold(), order(Scope.local)]` because it was red against this. It is now in the
terminal pool as `[values('name'), fold(), order(Scope.local)]`, ascending and descending —
`values` first because a bare `fold()` folds the FRONTIER, which is a list of elements, and two
engines agreeing on an error is no coverage. Coverage rose from 13-21/12-16 to `order(local)=35/29`
of 400, against floors of 8/5. All seven fuzzers green on random seeds.

### Still open after this

- **NULL's place in the total order** (item 88's second divergence): native ranks `Null` after
  `Str` and sorts nulls last, TS sorts them first. Unchanged by this item, and the reason the new
  arm takes `values('name')` — a key every row has — rather than a drawn key some rows lack.
- **`order(local).by('<key>')` does not parse** in native, while TinkerPop sorts an element list
  that way. A capability gap, not a wrong answer: today native faults where TinkerPop would sort,
  which is loud rather than silent.
- **A Map with no `by`**: TinkerPop throws, both engines sort. Shared deviation, needs a decision
  about the pair.

## 90. `pull`'s alignment is load-bearing, and nothing in source steers it (2026-10-03)

Item 89 ended with a finding it did not chase: three lines added to one of `pull`'s 45 arms cost
a reproducible 1.21x on `query/gql|filter age>50`, a counting query with no local sort in it. That
invites an obvious hypothesis — `pull` is 1,251 lines in ONE function, so hoisting its large COLD
arms should shrink the hot dispatch and speed the hot arms up. Both ways of acting on it were
measured. Both are rejected, and the notes are in the code (at the `CallInline` arm and at the
aggregate fast-path chain) because the hypothesis is an easy one to have again.

### Hoisting the cold arms is SLOWER

`CallInline` is the biggest cold arm at 93 lines. Moved out to an `#[inline(never)] fn`, min of
three interleaved rounds over 68 query rows:

| row                | before | after |              |
| ------------------ | -----: | ----: | ------------ |
| `filter age>50`    |  303.9 | 367.4 | 1.21x SLOWER |
| `filtered`         |  297.8 | 362.5 | 1.22x SLOWER |
| `has age>50`       |  299.0 | 371.5 | 1.24x SLOWER |
| `project bool AND` |  819.3 | 896.7 | 1.09x SLOWER |

Every other row flat; the sum of all 68 rows moved 1.002x. The three leading rows are the SAME
query text in different tables.

### Pinning the fast-path chain buys nothing

If the sensitivity were the 24-deep `.or_else` chain of `try_*` fast paths being inlined into the
`Aggregate` arm, `#[inline(never)]` on all 32 of them should cost nothing (they are once-per-query
decisions, not per-row) and make the layout stable. Neither held: `filtered` 289.6 → 298.9,
`filter age>50` 292.3 → 299.0, `has age>50` 295.8 → 300.5, ~2-3% slower with only the 0.2-0.3 ns
`label` rows flagged — and re-applying item 89's inline perturbation ON TOP of the pinned build
reproduces the same regression (366.6 min of three). So it is not cross-module inlining of that
chain, and the cost is real.

### What it actually is

Five perturbations, each min-of-three interleaved, pinned the behaviour down:

| perturbation                                           | cluster           |
| ------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------- |
| +3 lines inline in the `SortLocal` arm (item 89)       | 1.21x slower      |
| −93 lines, the `CallInline` arm hoisted out            | 1.21-1.24x slower |
| TWO ONE-CHARACTER call-site edits (`&elems` → `elems`) | 1.22x slower      |
| ~20 lines replaced by a call, twice (item 91)          | 1.10x slower      |
| a dead ~20-line fn added to the MODULE, never called   | **NO CHANGE**     |

The last row is the control and it is the informative one: module SIZE does nothing, so this is
`pull`'s own codegen. The magnitude tracks neither the size nor the content of the edit, and no
perturbation has ever landed in the favourable direction — so HEAD sits on an alignment nobody
designed and no edit can keep.

Two practical consequences, now recorded on `fn pull` itself:

- A 10-24% move in that cluster alongside a change to `pull` is **the cost of editing `pull`**,
  not evidence about the change. Judge a change here on the rows its own operator touches and
  report the cluster move as the toll.
- There is a real ~20% sitting in that query shape for whoever can control the alignment
  deliberately. Nothing reachable from source did.

## 91. A constant `FOR x IN <list>` built one list per row (2026-10-03)

`Unwind` was the largest unexplained price left on `plan_cost_by_operator` at 84.63 ns/row. The
harness row used `Expr::Prop`, so it measured the row-VARYING list; the row-INVARIANT spelling —
ISO `FOR x IN [1, 2, 3]`, and `FOR id IN $ids`, the bulk-lookup shape — was unpriced. Priced, it
came out at 70.41 ns/row: essentially the same, so nothing was being hoisted.

Note the surface. `FOR … IN` is the ISO list unwind; **this engine has no `UNWIND`** (that is
Cypher, and the first bench row written here was rejected by the parser for saying so).

### Two costs, both per input row, for a list that never changes

`Expr::List` builds a separate `Value::List` for every row, and the `Unwind` arm then calls
`lists.value_at(i)`, which CLONES each one. For a 200,000-row scan of a 2-element constant list
that is 400,000 allocations to carry two numbers. On top of it, `reunfold_elements` ended in
`Col::Gen(elems.to_vec())` — a full copy of all 400,000 values — while both of its callers owned
the vector and dropped it immediately.

FIXED in two parts:

- `reunfold_elements` takes `elems` BY VALUE and returns `Col::Gen(elems)`. The fallback (a list
  of plain scalars) is the common case, and it was copying every element for nothing.
- `unwind_rows` evaluates a row-invariant list ONCE and reuses it. Invariance is `slots_read`
  reporting success with NO slots touched, which is exactly "depends on no column". Every callable
  function in this engine is deterministic — the `localtime`/`datetime` family are TYPE
  CONSTRUCTORS over their arguments, not clock reads — so one evaluation and n evaluations cannot
  disagree. A 0-row batch evaluates NOTHING, which keeps the set of queries that raise exactly as
  it was.

|                                             |     before |         after |                                |
| ------------------------------------------- | ---------: | ------------: | -----------------------------: |
| harness `Unwind (2-element CONSTANT list)`  |   70.41 ns |   **5.57 ns** |        12.6x; 115x → 8.2x Scan |
| harness `Unwind (2-element list)` (varying) |   72.50 ns |      68.36 ns | 1.06x, from the ownership half |
| bench `for-in const x3`                     | 31,388.5us | **3,364.2us** |   9.3x isolated, 6.4x in-sweep |
| bench `for-in const project`                | 33,637.7us |     5,659.4us |                       4.8-5.9x |

Both bench rows are new; the projecting one exists because a counting one could in principle be
answered by a shortcut that never enters the operator.

### The toll, and why it is the toll and not the price

`filtered`/`has age>50`/`filter age>50` move ~300 → ~330, a consistent **1.10-1.11x**. That is item
90's cluster, and it is not attributable to this change: the `render.rs` half ALONE — two
one-character call-site edits — measured 365.6, which is WORSE (1.22x) than the full change. This
is the cheapest perturbation of `pull` yet measured, and item 90's table says every perturbation
pays. Recorded rather than hidden; reversible in one command if the trade is unwanted.

### Teeth

| mutant                                                                          | caught by                                                                                  |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| invariance ignores WHICH slots are read, so a varying list collapses to row 0's | `a_row_varying_unwind_list_is_evaluated_per_row`                                           |
| the non-empty gate dropped, probing row 0 of an empty batch                     | `an_invariant_unwind_list_over_no_rows_yields_no_rows` (panics in `batch.rs` gather)       |
| the invariant branch ignores `one_based` ordinals                               | `an_invariant_unwind_list_repeats_for_every_row_with_per_row_ordinals` and `for_in_unwind` |

The first mutant SURVIVED the whole suite before these tests existed. `for_in_unwind`'s fixture
has ONE node, which cannot tell "evaluated per row" from "evaluated once at row 0" — the same
shape of blind fixture as items 81, 86 and 89, and the fourth time in this loop that the fixture,
not the assertion, was what was missing.

## 92. A stored null serialized as its column's zero value, and sometimes as another row's (2026-10-03)

Found while adding a stored null to the Gremlin fuzzer's fixture for item 93. The fixture change
went red on five plans that have nothing to do with ordering:

```text
g.V().hasLabel('SOFTWARE')
  ts:     … {"id":"5", …, "properties":{"age":null,"lang":"java","name":"ripple"}}
  native: … {"id":"5", …, "properties":{"age":0,   "lang":"java","name":"ripple"}}
```

### Where it was, and where it was not

Everything in Rust was right. `Column::read` gates on `present` and yields `Null`;
`present_at(i)` is `present || nulls`, so a stored null counts as present-but-valueless.
`node_result_value`, `render_nodes` and the `Value` tree all answered `Null`, as did
`values('k')` — `[2, null, 1]`. Only the JSON egress was wrong, and only for the NESTED map:

| on a 4-node NDJSON graph where one `k` is a stored null | native           |
| ------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------- |
| `g.V().values('k').fold()`                              | `[[2,null,1]]` ✓ |
| `g.V()`                                                 | `{"k":0}` ✗      |
| `g.V().valueMap()`                                      | `{"k":0}` ✗      |

`try_run_gremlin_json` has a fused writer that "writes the JSON straight from the columns and
skips building a `Value::Map` tree per row". Its leaf, `write_col_cell_json`, documented itself
as "the caller guarantees `present_at(i)`" and "byte-identical to `write_value(&col.read(i))`".
Those two claims are inconsistent: the guard is `present || nulls`, `read` gates on `present`
alone. So for a stored null the guard passed and the typed arms read `data[i]` out from under
it — `0` for `Num`, `false` for `Bool`, and for `Str`/`Dict` whatever sits at the default slot.

### It is a cross-row leak, not only a wrong default

With the fix removed, the test's two-node fixture renders node 2 as

```text
{"flag":false,"num":0,"txt":"x"}
```

`txt` is `"x"` — **node 1's string**. A `Dict` column reads `codes[i]`, which is 0 for a slot
that was never written, and hands back the first interned entry. So a stored null could expose a
different record's value, on the path the FFI and the whole TS-facing API return.

FIXED by checking `col.has_value_at(i)` — which is `present` alone, for every variant — and
writing `null` when there is no value. The `other => read(i)` arm was always correct.

### Why 11 byte-identity queries agreed while it was wrong

`fused_map_json_matches_value_tree_bytes` already compares the fused writer against the value
tree across 11 queries. Its fixture gave every property either a value or no key at all — never
a present-null — so the one state that distinguishes the two paths was absent. The fourth blind
fixture in this loop (items 81, 86, 89, 91) and the same shape every time: the assertion was
fine, the fixture could not reach the bug.

Adding present-nulls to THAT fixture does not fix it either, and that was measured rather than
assumed: `Builder`'s null write does not land in a typed column, so those rows take the
already-correct `Gen` arm and the mutant stays green. The new test builds its store from NDJSON,
because the bulk loader types the column from the first value it sees and records later nulls in
the presence bitsets — which is the shape that breaks.

### Teeth

| mutant                                         | caught by                                                         |
| ---------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- |
| the `has_value_at` guard removed               | `a_stored_null_property_serializes_as_null_not_as_a_zero`         |
| — the same mutant, against a `Builder` fixture | **NOTHING** (900 tests green) — which is why the test uses NDJSON |

## 93. `order(local)` sorted nulls last where Gremlin puts them first (2026-10-03)

The second of the two divergences item 88's `order(local)` arm found. The framing it was recorded
under was wrong in two ways, and measuring first is what showed that.

### It is not a contract question, and not the shared comparator

`ir::SortKey` already records the split, in its own doc comment: GQL puts NULLs last in both ASC
and DESC (`nulls_first: false`), Gremlin puts them first (`nulls_first: !descending`). The global
`order()` honours that flag in `exec::order::row_cmp`, BEFORE consulting `cmp_total`. So there was
nothing to decide, and the divergence was NOT in the shared total order:

|                             | TS             | native             | TinkerPop      |
| --------------------------- | -------------- | ------------------ | -------------- |
| `g.V().values('k').order()` | `[null,1,2]`   | `[null,1,2]`       | `[null,1,2]`   |
| `…order().by(desc)`         | `[2,1,null]`   | `[2,1,null]`       | `[2,1,null]`   |
| `…fold().order(local)`      | `[[null,1,2]]` | **`[[1,2,null]]`** | `[[null,1,2]]` |

Only the local scope was wrong, because `sort_local_cell` went straight to `cmp_total`, whose
rank puts `Null` at 7. Same shape as item 89: the global path knew a rule the local one never got.

Ground truth, a real `tinkerpop/gremlin-console` on `createModern()` — six cases in two calls:

```text
g.inject(2, null, 1).order()                   -> [null, 1, 2]
g.inject(2, null, 1).order().by(Order.desc)    -> [2, 1, null]
g.inject(2, null, 1).fold().order(Scope.local) -> [[null, 1, 2]]
g.inject("b", null, "a").order()               -> [null, a, b]
g.inject(2, null, "a").order()                 -> [null, 2, a]
g.inject(2, null, 1).min() / .max()            -> [1] / [2]   (null EXCLUDED)
```

FIXED with `cmp_local_gremlin` — null least, then `cmp_total` — and the caller's DESC reversal
carries it to the end, which is what makes DESC a plain reversal as TinkerPop has it. Native's
`min`/`max` already exclude null, matching the last line, so nothing was needed there.

GQL is untouched: `sort_local_cell` is reached only from `Plan::SortLocal`, which is Gremlin-only,
and the two sibling `cmp_total` list sorts in `evaluator.rs` sort label and property-key strings,
which are never null. `gql_order_by_keeps_nulls_last` guards the split from the other side.

### `Value::rank`'s Null position turns out to be unobservable

Moving `Null` from 7 to 0 in the shared rank passes **all 1,635 engine tests** — every path that
can see a null places it before consulting the rank. Only `cross_type_ordering_is_total_never_a_
throw`, in `lenke-engine-core`, pins it, and that crate is a separate CI job my mutation runs had
never executed. The rank's comment also claimed Null was "the greatest in the total order" while
`Node` and `Edge` sit above it; both are corrected to what is true.

### What the oracle needed, and what the generator believed

The arm was pinned to `values('name')` on the belief that other keys would inject nulls because
some vertices lack them. That was wrong: an ABSENT property SKIPS the row — `g.V().count()` 4
against `g.V().values('k').count()` 3 — exactly as TinkerPop does. Only a STORED null yields one,
and the fixture had none, so no draw could have reached null placement.

So: a stored null on vertex 5 (`age`) and vertex 2 (`lang`), mirrored on both fixture definitions;
the key draw widened from `name` to a drawn key, biased toward the two null-bearing ones; and a
third coverage counter for a locally-sorted result that actually CONTAINED a null.

Getting that counter off the floor took measurement, not guessing. Unbiased draw: 2/0/1 nulls per
400 across seeds 1/7/42 — seed 11 gave **zero**, so there was no floor to set. Doubling the
collection arm's weight (taken from the bare no-terminal tail, leaving every other terminal
unchanged) doubled generated/non-empty and left nulls at 2/2/1: the arm's share was never the
bottleneck, the 1-4 intervening steps hopping the null-bearing vertex out of the frontier was. A
dedicated shape, `nullOrderLocalChain` — `V()` straight into `values(<null key>)`, `fold()`,
`order(local)` with nothing between — puts the null in the list every time. The precedent is
`pureVertexChain`, which exists for the same reason.

Coverage is now 8-23 per 400 across thirteen seeds, floor set at >5. Reverting the fix makes the
fuzzer go RED on seeds 1 and 42, so the addition is justified by a mutant it catches.

### Teeth

| mutant                                                          | caught by                                                                                          |
| --------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| the fix reverted (local sort back on `cmp_total`)               | `order_local_sorts_nulls_first`, `order_local_desc_sends_nulls_last`, and the fuzzer on seeds 1/42 |
| nulls placed OUTSIDE the DESC reversal, so DESC puts them first | the same two tests                                                                                 |
| the WRONG FIX — the SHARED rank changed so `Null` is least      | `cross_type_ordering_is_total_never_a_throw`, in `lenke-engine-core` ONLY                          |

The third is the both-directions mutant a per-language split needs: it is the fix someone would
reach for, it breaks nothing in `lenke-engine`, and the only thing standing in its way lives in
the other crate.

## 94. Every cross-engine bench row was timing a graph decode (2026-10-04)

First iteration of a pass aimed at the TypeScript engine. The intended first step was to price
the ts-vs-native ratios the way `plan_cost_by_operator` prices operators — CLAUDE.md says the
Rust core is 1.5-4x pure-TS, so a row far outside that band is a missing path rather than an
inherent gap. The table could not answer that, because of how it was measured.

### `withGraph` loaded the graph INSIDE the timed body

```ts
const withGraph = (e, doc, fn) => {
  const g = e.load(doc);
  try {
    fn(g);
  } finally {
    e.free(g);
  }
};
```

Every row except the two `decode ndjson` ones went through that, so each measured a decode
plus the thing it was named for — and the decode dominated:

| row                            | printed |                          the named operation was |
| ------------------------------ | ------: | -----------------------------------------------: |
| `query: 1-hop traversal` (ts)  |  3894.7 | ~1031 (vs `decode ndjson (5 edges/node)` 2863.3) |
| `query: 1-hop traversal` (ffi) |   764.3 |                   ~8.5 (vs its own decode 755.8) |
| `encode ndjson` (ts)           |   313.9 |                    ~75 — the row was ~76% decode |

A `decode <fmt>` row was worse still: it timed an ndjson decode, AND an encode to that format,
AND the decode it was named for — two of which are separate rows already.

So the ratios this file printed were **decode ratios wearing other rows' names**, and they are
where the "1.5-4x pure-TS" range in CLAUDE.md came from. FIXED by giving `Case` a `setup` /
`teardown` that run outside the timed region (`onGraph(doc, fn)` loads once and times `fn`
against that one warm graph; a read-only query reusing a warm graph is also the shape a server
actually serves). Numbers from before this change are not comparable with numbers after it.

### What the band actually looks like

```text
workload                               ts        ffi       wasm        ts/ffi     ts/wasm
decode ndjson (nodes)               225.7      115.6      129.1         1.95x       1.75x
decode ndjson (5 edges/node)       2700.0      750.5      839.7         3.60x       3.22x
encode ndjson                        76.2       41.3       48.1         1.84x       1.58x
encode pg-json                       55.9       41.6       54.0         1.35x       1.04x
encode graphson                     179.8      133.4      129.7         1.35x       1.39x
encode pg-text                      113.7       41.1       58.2         2.76x       1.95x
encode csv                          139.5      134.4      159.5         1.04x       0.87x
decode pg-json                      209.9       99.0      107.8         2.12x       1.95x
decode graphson                     422.9      326.9      300.2         1.29x       1.41x
decode pg-text                      604.1      115.7      122.9         5.22x       4.92x
query: count                         79.8        0.0        0.1     11597.15x    1265.08x
query: project 3 columns            123.9       55.6       62.2         2.23x       1.99x
query: group + aggregate            164.3        1.1        1.6       150.53x     103.79x
query: 1-hop traversal              586.2        0.5        0.8      1181.78x     746.11x
```

The codec rows sit in the stated 1.5-4x band (pg-text decode a little above it). The QUERY rows
do not, by three orders of magnitude — and the cause is not codegen. Native answers these
without enumerating: `try_scan_count` and the count-shortcut ladder read exact base counts and
per-label edge counts, so a count over 200,000 nodes and a count over 1,000,000 edges are both
~0ms. **The TS engine has no equivalent and materializes the rows.** That is the missing path
this pass was looking for, and it is the next item.

VERIFIED BEFORE BELIEVING IT — `ffi 0.0` is the shape of work that did not happen, so both
engines were checked for the right answer on a 20,000-node / 100,000-edge graph:
`count(*)` → 20000 on both, the 1-hop `count(*)` → 100000 on both. The shortcut is real.

### Two reporting defects fixed alongside

- **A failing row printed a bare `n/a`**, indistinguishable from "this engine does not support
  this workload". `query: group + aggregate` sat at `n/a` on EVERY engine because its query was
  invalid — `GROUP BY` takes a bound name, not a `RETURN` alias, so
  `RETURN n.age AS a … GROUP BY a` raised `variable \`a\` is not defined`on both engines (see
[[group-by-vs-order-by-scope]]). The workload measured nothing for as long as it was there.
The query now uses`LET a = n.age`, and the harness prints the first line of each failure
  under the row.
- **The ratio column was `<engine>/ts`**, which prints `0.00x` for every row where the gap is
  large — precisely where the number matters. It is now `ts/<engine>`: how many times faster
  that engine is. The divisor is floored so a row under the clock's resolution cannot print
  `Infinityx`.

### Noted for later, not chased here

`encode csv` is 1.04x / 0.87x — native is barely faster and **wasm is slower than TypeScript**,
the only row where that happens. `decode graphson` is 1.29x / 1.41x. Both are native-side
anomalies in a band where the other codecs manage 1.8-5.2x.

## 95. A universal label made the TS 1-hop count 586ms instead of 0 (2026-10-04)

Item 94's corrected harness put `query: 1-hop traversal` at ts 586.2ms against ffi 0.5ms, a
1182x gap, and named the TS count shortcuts as the suspect. **The shortcut was there and it
was firing.** `packages/gql/src/executor/shortcuts.ts` has had a 1-hop and a 2-hop `count(*)`
shortcut all along, with a `count-shortcut.test.ts` checking both against an independent
enumeration. Only one of its two paths is O(1):

```ts
if (aLabel === undefined && bLabel === undefined && …) {
  return rowOf(types.reduce((n, t) => n + (graph.edgesByLabel.get(t)?.size ?? 0), 0));
}
return rowOf(countEdges(edgesOfTypes(graph.edgesByLabel, types), (edge) =>
  matchesLabel(out ? edge.from : edge.to, aLabel) &&
  matchesLabel(out ? edge.to : edge.from, bLabel)));
```

The bench query labels its source — `MATCH (a:Person)-[:KNOWS]->(x)` — so it took the walk.

### The cost is cache locality, not interpretation

My first guess was the `edgesOfTypes` generator, since a `yield*` per element allocates. Wrong,
and measuring was cheap. Over the bench's 1,000,000-edge `KNOWS` bucket:

|                                           |      ms |
| ----------------------------------------- | ------: |
| generator `yield*` + closure + label test |    96.2 |
| direct `Set` loop + label test            |    89.3 |
| direct `Set` loop, NO label test          | **1.1** |
| `set.size`                                |     0.0 |

The generator is ~7ms of it. The label test is ~88ms — 88ns per `edge.from.labels.has('Person')`,
which is a pointer chase to a random `Vertex` and its `Set` across a large heap. `evalLabelExpr`
reduces to `labels.has(name)` for a plain label, so there is no AST walk to remove. The fix had
to be to stop doing it per edge.

### It is the equivalent-spellings rule, not a special case

When every vertex is a `Person`, `MATCH (a:Person)-[:KNOWS]->(x)` and
`MATCH (a)-[:KNOWS]->(x)` are two spellings of ONE question, and they cost 586ms against ~0ms
because only the unlabelled spelling reached the bucket-size path. That is exactly the bug
class CLAUDE.md's "equivalent spellings must cost the same" section is about, and the native
engine already elides it — which is why it answers in ~0.5ms.

FIXED with `vacuousLabel(graph, expr)`: a plain single-label expression whose bucket holds as
many vertices as the graph has is satisfied by every vertex, so it is replaced with
`undefined` before the paths are chosen — and the existing O(1) path then takes the query. No
new path, one predicate.

Soundness is the `===`. A bucket holding exactly `vertexCount` vertices must hold all of them,
so `labels.has(name)` is universally true and dropping it cannot change a row. A stale
over-count makes the comparison FAIL and leaves the optimization off, which is the safe
direction. `and`/`or`/`not`/`%` are not attempted. An empty graph compares `0 === 0` and is
trivially vacuous — it has no edges to count either.

| bench row                     |  before |     after |
| ----------------------------- | ------: | --------: |
| `query: 1-hop traversal` (ts) | 586.2ms | **0.0ms** |
| the same row, ffi             |   0.5ms |     0.5ms |

TS now answers it FASTER than native (a `Set.size` read against native's ~0.5ms), the only
query row where that happens. Every other bench row is untouched — nothing else reaches
`buildOneHopCount`.

### Verified, because a 586ms → 0ms count is where a wrong answer hides

|                               |        |                                                |
| ----------------------------- | ------ | ---------------------------------------------- |
| all-Person, `(a:Person)->(x)` | 100000 | matches the unlabelled spelling                |
| all-Person, `(a:Absent)->(x)` | 0      | empty bucket, `0 === 20000` false → no elision |
| mixed, `(a:Admin)->(x)`       | 5      | elision must NOT fire                          |
| mixed, `(a)->(x:Admin)`       | 5      | far endpoint, same                             |
| mixed, `(a:Admin)->(x:Admin)` | 0      | both                                           |

All seven byte-identity fuzzers green on random seeds, so TS still agrees with native.

### Teeth

| mutant                                           | caught by                                                                                                       |
| ------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| every label treated as vacuous                   | `a partial or absent label is still enforced`, and the pre-existing `1-hop: bare, labeled source, labeled both` |
| `===` relaxed to `<=`, so a partial label elides | the same two                                                                                                    |

The pre-existing test had teeth here because its fixture makes `Person` universal
(`isPerson = () => true`) AND `Admin` partial, so it pins both sides. The new tests add the
equivalence itself (all four spellings of the universal question agree), the absent-label
case, and an empty graph.

### Still open

`query: count` — `MATCH (n:Person) RETURN count(*)` — is still ts 91.1ms against ffi 0.0ms,
**16125x**. The shortcuts cover 1-hop and 2-hop EDGE counts; a bare labelled NODE count has
no shortcut at all and enumerates. That is the next item, and `verticesByLabel.get(L).size` is
the whole answer for the unfiltered case.

## 96. The TS count shortcut ignored GROUP BY and HAVING (2026-10-04)

Found by adding a node-count shortcut (item 97) and watching `gql-conformance` go red on a
case that had nothing to do with it. `detectCountShortcut`'s projection guard rejects `star`,
`distinct`, `orderBy`, `skip`, `limit` and any item count but one — and does not look at
`groupBy` or `having` at all, though `Projection` carries both.

Both are WRONG ANSWERS, not slow paths. The shortcut computes one global count, so a grouped
count collapses to a single row and a `HAVING` that should drop the row never runs. Measured
against native, which is right in every case:

| query                                                                            | ts          | native              |
| -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------- | ------------------- |
| `MATCH (a:Person)-[:KNOWS]->(b) RETURN count(*) AS c GROUP BY a`                 | `[{"c":4}]` | `[[1],[1],[1],[1]]` |
| `SELECT count(*) AS c FROM MATCH (a:Person)-[:KNOWS]->(b) HAVING count(*) > 100` | `[{"c":4}]` | `[]`                |
| the 2-hop spellings of both                                                      | same shape  | same shape          |

**The 1-hop and 2-hop shortcuts have shipped with this.** Nothing I changed caused it.

### Why two suites missed it

`gql-conformance` DOES test `HAVING` on a global aggregate, and expects `[]` — but it asks it
of a BARE NODE pattern (`FROM MATCH (n:Person)`), which had no shortcut at all, so the query
went through general execution and answered correctly. Adding `buildNodeCount` routed that
exact query into the shortcut and turned the latent gap into a failing test.

A `LET` hides it the same way: `MATCH (n:P) LET k = n.k RETURN count(*) AS c GROUP BY k` is
three clauses, so `clauses.length !== 2` rejects the shortcut and the answer is correct. Only
the two-clause spelling is affected, which is why the grouped-count form had no coverage.

FIXED by adding `proj.groupBy !== undefined || proj.having !== undefined` to the guard. All
four queries above now match native.

### Teeth

| mutant                      | caught by                                            |
| --------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- |
| the `groupBy` guard removed | `a grouped count is not collapsed to one global row` |
| the `having` guard removed  | `HAVING can drop the counted row`                    |

Each new test catches exactly one, and nothing else in the file catches either. The grouped
test asserts both that there is more than one row AND that the rows sum to the independent
enumeration, so a mutant cannot satisfy it by returning one row with the right total.

## 97. A bare node count had no shortcut at all (2026-10-04)

Item 94's table left `query: count` — `MATCH (n:Person) RETURN count(*)` — at ts 91.1ms
against native's ~0.0ms, **16125x**, the widest remaining gap once the 1-hop count was fixed.
`detectCountShortcut` handled `segments.length === 1` and `=== 2` and fell through to `null`
for zero, so a bare labelled node count enumerated every vertex and built a row per match.

FIXED with `buildNodeCount`: the unlabelled form answers `graph.vertexCount`, the plain
single-label form `graph.verticesByLabel.get(name)?.size ?? 0`. `and` (`(n:A:B)`), `or`, `not`
and `%` have no single bucket and still fall through to enumeration.

Reading that size is sound rather than optimistic because **`verticesByLabel` is already
load-bearing for correctness**: `candidateVertices` enumerates that same bucket for a plain
label, so a bucket missing a vertex would already make `MATCH (n:Person)` miss rows today. The
one asymmetry is that enumeration re-checks each candidate through `matchNode` and so tolerates
a stale EXTRA, where a size read would not — `a removed vertex and a removed label both leave
the count exact` pins that there are none.

| bench row           | before |     after |
| ------------------- | -----: | --------: |
| `query: count` (ts) | 91.1ms | **0.0ms** |

### Teeth

| mutant                                                         | caught by                                                         |
| -------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- |
| the label ignored, so every node count returns `vertexCount`   | `a labelled node count counts only that label`                    |
| `verticesByLabel` read but a removed vertex left in the bucket | `a removed vertex and a removed label both leave the count exact` |

The second mutant is the one that matters: it is the assumption the optimization rests on, and
it is about the INDEX rather than the shortcut, so the test drives it through `removeVertex`
and `removeLabel` rather than asserting on a freshly built graph.

## 98. The TS row pipeline costs ~487ns before it does any work (2026-10-04)

`query: group + aggregate` was the widest remaining row at ts ~158ms against ffi 1.1ms. Unlike
the counts, this is NOT a missing shortcut on native's side doing nothing — native genuinely
tallies 200,000 nodes in 1.1ms (~5.5ns each). So the question was where TS spends ~790ns a row.

### Two hypotheses, both wrong, both cheap to kill

`valueKey` + `JSON.stringify` per row looked damning — the grouping loop builds
`JSON.stringify(groupKeys.map(fn => valueKey(...)))` as its key. Over 200,000 rows:

|                                         |    ms |
| --------------------------------------- | ----: |
| `Map.set` with the stringified key      | 14.64 |
| `Map.set` with the bare `valueKey`      |  7.22 |
| `Map.set`, count only, no binding array |  7.22 |

Real, but ~15ms of a 158ms row. Then `withBinding` — `new Map(binding)` copies the binding per
row per bound variable — looked structural. Also not it: two chained Map copies are 54ns a row,
four nested generator layers 21ns, two object spreads 11ns. ~100ns of the 487 at most.

### What the profile says

The target shape is not the group-by at all. Timing TS shapes that differ by one stage:

| TS query over 200,000 `Person` nodes |       ms |
| ------------------------------------ | -------: |
| `RETURN count(*)` (shortcut)         |      0.2 |
| **`RETURN 1`** — a constant          | **97.4** |
| `RETURN n.age`                       |    117.6 |
| `LET a = n.age RETURN a`             |    155.2 |
| grouped count                        |    144.9 |

`RETURN 1` costs 97ms: ~487ns a row to produce a constant. `bun --cpu-prof` on that query,
self-time merged:

|           |                   |
| --------: | ----------------- |
| **36.9%** | `generatorResume` |
|      9.9% | `withBinding`     |
|      8.0% | `map`             |
|      6.8% | `mapToRow`        |
|      6.6% | `projectBinding`  |

The lazy pipeline's own plumbing is the cost — ~5-8 nested generator layers, each resumed once
per row. The call tree confirms the nesting (`generatorResume → next → (anon)` repeating).

### The micro-fix was retracted

With no `ORDER BY`, every row is wrapped in `{ b, keys: [] }` — an object AND an empty array —
only for a second generator layer to unwrap it. Removing that for the no-sort case is one layer
of ~6 plus two allocations: ~5-8%, under the ~10% CLAUDE.md says needs its own isolated
harness. NOT SHIPPED; recorded here so the next reader does not re-derive it.

## 99. A grouped count had no shortcut, and only one of its two spellings was enough (2026-10-04)

The actionable version of item 98: native has `try_group_count` / `try_node_grouped_count`, TS
had nothing, so a grouped count paid the full ~487ns-a-row pipeline. `detectGroupedNodeCount`
tallies straight off the label bucket — no bindings, no rows, no generator layers.

| bench row                       |  before |                                 after |
| ------------------------------- | ------: | ------------------------------------: |
| `query: group + aggregate` (ts) | 158.6ms | **17.2ms** (9.2x; ratio 144x → 15.8x) |
| `query: project 3 columns` (ts) | 126.8ms |      120.5ms — the control, unchanged |

### Handling one spelling was worth nothing

The first version took only `MATCH (n:P) RETURN n.k AS a, count(*) AS c` — two clauses, implicit
grouping. **The bench row did not move at all**, because the bench asks the ISO spelling,
`MATCH (n:P) LET a = n.k RETURN a, count(*) AS c GROUP BY a` — THREE clauses, which the detector
rejected. That is the "check the planner reaches the shape" rule arriving as a flat number
rather than an argument, and changing the bench to the two-clause form would have been gaming
it. The detector now takes both, which is also the equivalent-spellings rule: `GROUP BY` needs a
BOUND NAME rather than a `RETURN` alias, so a `LET` is the only way to name the key, and the two
spellings are one question.

### Every semantic is borrowed, not re-derived

The property read is `propOf`, the same function the compiled `prop` expression calls, so a
MISSING property and a STORED NULL both read as `null` and land in ONE group. The group key is
`valueKey`, so `-0`/`0` share a group and `NaN` groups with itself. Groups emerge in FIRST-SEEN
order from a `Map`, as the general path gets from its own `Map<string, Binding[]>`. The key
column shows the FIRST row's raw value. Columns follow the projection's item order, so
`RETURN count(*) AS c, n.k AS a` keeps `c` first.

### Teeth, and two fixture gaps it took to get them

Tested against the GENERAL PATH as oracle rather than hand-written expectations: `WHERE true` is
rejected by the detector and cannot change which rows match, so it is the same question computed
the slow way — the strongest oracle available once both fast spellings take the shortcut.

| mutant                                       | caught by                                                                       |
| -------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| the key column shows the LAST row's value    | the differential test                                                           |
| column order ignored, key always first       | the differential test                                                           |
| group on `String(raw)` instead of `valueKey` | the differential test — **only after** adding a number `7` and the string `'7'` |
| `GROUP BY` need not name the `LET` variable  | the declines test — **only after** adding two vertices that SHARE a `k`         |

Both gaps are the same shape as items 89, 91, 95 and 96: the assertion was fine, the fixture
could not reach the bug. `String(raw)` already unifies `-0`/`0` and `NaN`, so it diverges only
on a number-vs-string collision; and a `GROUP BY` naming the node instead of the key is
indistinguishable from the right answer unless two rows share a key.

## 100. Native's CSV encoder cloned a key String per cell (2026-10-04)

With the query rows fixed, re-pricing the cross-engine table moved the anomaly to the codecs.
`encode csv` was **1.03x ffi / 0.89x wasm** — native barely faster than TypeScript, and the
wasm build actually SLOWER, in a table where every other codec manages 1.8-5.2x. It had been
that way in every run of this session, so it was not noise.

A useful bound came free: `encode pg-text` is 41ms through the same `to_graph_data` bridge while
CSV is 125ms, so the bridge is not the problem — CSV's own work is ~85ms of it.

### Three allocations per cell, one of them pure waste

|                   |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| ----------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `compute_columns` | `seen.insert(key.clone())` — `HashSet<String>::insert` takes an OWNED value, so a String was allocated for EVERY CELL to discover, after the first row, that the key was already there. 600,000 clones for 200,000 nodes at three properties each. |
| `write_row`       | `types[key]` — a `HashMap<String, ColType>` lookup per cell per row, hashing the key string to re-derive something fixed for the whole encode.                                                                                                     |
| `write_row`       | `quote_field(raw)` returns an owned String purely to be appended to `out`, and in the common case that allocation is a verbatim copy (`raw.to_string()`).                                                                                          |

FIXED: `seen.contains(key.as_str())` borrows through `Borrow<str>` and clones once per DISTINCT
key; `types` is resolved ONCE into a positional `Vec<ColType>` zipped with `keys`; and
`quote_field_into(out, raw)` writes straight into the buffer, with `quote_field` kept as a thin
wrapper for the header path.

|                                                 |        min of three |
| ----------------------------------------------- | ------------------: |
| `encode csv` (ffi) before                       |             134.4ms |
| after `quote_field_into` + positional types     |             125.3ms |
| after the `contains`-before-`clone` fix as well | **118.1ms** — 1.14x |

**The anomaly is reduced, not resolved.** Native CSV encode is still only 1.08x faster than
TypeScript; the remaining cost is `encode_cell` building a `String` per cell and the
`GraphData` bridge. Recorded as still open rather than claimed as fixed.

### Teeth, and the two decisions that had none

Byte-identity is the contract here, so the mutants target the rewritten bytes rather than the
speed. `bun run fuzz` (all seven, random seeds) and the 29 existing codec tests pass, but two
of the three mutants slipped straight through them:

| mutant                                 | caught by                                                                  |
| -------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| the surrounding quotes dropped         | `null_list_element_round_trips`, `quoting_labels_and_formulas`             |
| the embedded `"` not DOUBLED           | **nothing** — 29 tests green; only the random-seeded `codec` fuzzer failed |
| the positional types zipped in REVERSE | **nothing**, even after adding a mixed-type fixture                        |

The first gap: `quoting_labels_and_formulas` does put a `"` in a property VALUE, but a value
takes `encode_cell`'s `force_quote` branch, so the doubling loop inside `quote_field_into` is
reached only by the FIXED columns — id, labels, edge type — and no test put a quote in one.
`a_quote_in_an_id_or_label_round_trips` does.

The second gap is subtler and took two attempts. A mis-zip is invisible when the columns share
a type, so the first version of `columns_of_different_types_keep_their_own` used a number, a
string and a list — and STILL passed under the mutant, because `encode_cell` falls into a
self-describing mismatch branch when `actual != column` and that form decodes back correctly. A
round-trip assertion cannot see it; the test now asserts the encoded BYTES.

### A seventh screen false positive

`bench_all` flagged `repeat-group head(b)` at 1.18x (89,060 → 105,023us) — a row a CSV codec
change cannot reach. Interleaved min-of-three: before 89,248, after 90,038, i.e. 1.009x. That
row spans 89k-101k for the same binary. Seventh time in this loop the sweep has flagged noise.

## 101. The graphson anomaly does not exist, and CSV's real cost is the bridge (2026-10-04)

Two retractions and one located target.

### Retracted: `encode/decode graphson` is not slow

Re-pricing after item 100 left graphson looking like the worst native codec — `decode graphson`
320ms against `decode pg-json` 100ms, with a ts/ffi ratio of only 1.25-1.33x. Measuring the
DOCUMENTS first killed it:

| format   | serialized size, 200,000 nodes |
| -------- | -----------------------------: |
| graphson |                    **79.0 MB** |
| ndjson   |                        20.4 MB |
| pg-json  |                        17.6 MB |
| pg-text  |                         9.2 MB |
| csv      |                         6.2 MB |

Graphson carries 4.5x pg-json's bytes for a 3.2x slower decode, so per byte it is FASTER
(0.247 MB/ms against 0.176). A low ts/ffi ratio on graphson means TypeScript is relatively GOOD
at it, not that native is bad. No anomaly, nothing to fix.

### The ratio column cannot say whether a codec is slow

That retraction is a measurement lesson, not a one-off: `ts/<engine>` compares the two engines
and says nothing about absolute throughput. Normalizing by document size does:

| native codec    |     MB/ms |
| --------------- | --------: |
| encode graphson |     1.094 |
| encode pg-json  |     0.535 |
| encode ndjson   |     0.500 |
| decode graphson |     0.247 |
| decode pg-json  |     0.176 |
| decode pg-text  |     0.083 |
| **encode csv**  | **0.052** |

CSV encode is 10x slower per byte than ndjson encode and 21x slower than graphson's — by far
the worst, and the only row the engine-ratio view had buried.

### And CSV's remaining cost is NOT in the CSV encoder

Timing the stages of `encode_nodes` directly over 200,000 nodes: `bags` 0.4ms,
`compute_columns` 11.5ms, the row loop 28.3ms — **~40.5ms total, against the 112.7ms the bench
reports.** So ~72ms, nearly two thirds, is outside the codec.

`lenke-engine`'s own dispatch says where it goes, and says it was already known:

```rust
// pg-json streams straight from the store (no owned `GraphData` copy); the other
// formats still bridge through the neutral model until they gain a streaming path.
match format {
    "pg-json" => Ok(serialize_pg_json(store)),
    "graphson" => Ok(serialize_graphson(store)),
    "pg-text" => serialize_pg_text(store),
    _ => lenke_codec::serialize(&to_graph_data(store), format),
}
```

Three formats have a streaming path. **CSV is the only one left on `to_graph_data`**, which
materializes every node and property into owned `String`s and `Value`s before a byte is
written. That is the real missing path, it has three siblings to copy the shape from, and
`codec.rs` already carries the byte-identity test idiom for one (the pg-text streaming encoder
is asserted equal to `serialize(&to_graph_data(..), "pg-text")`). Deferred to its own item
rather than bolted onto this one.

### What did land

`write_cell` writes a cell's quoted text straight into the output buffer. The commonest cell
there is — a string in a string column — used to allocate TWICE: `scalar_to_raw` cloned the
value into `Encoded::raw`, then the `force_quote` branch called `str::replace`, which allocates
UNCONDITIONALLY even when there is no quote to double. A `Str` column always sets `force_quote`,
so that was ~400,000 Strings per encode on this fixture for nothing.

| `encode csv` (ffi), min of three |             |
| -------------------------------- | ----------: |
| before item 100                  |     134.4ms |
| after item 100                   |     118.1ms |
| after `write_cell`               | **112.7ms** |

4.8% on its own — UNDER the 10% CLAUDE.md wants an isolated harness for, and shipped only
because it is cumulative with item 100 (1.19x together) and because the diagnosis above is the
iteration's real product.

### Teeth

| mutant                                     | caught by                     |
| ------------------------------------------ | ----------------------------- |
| the escape prefix dropped in the fast path | `quoting_labels_and_formulas` |
| `push_doubling_quotes` does not double     | `quoting_labels_and_formulas` |
| the fast path omits the surrounding quotes | 3 tests                       |

### An eighth screen false positive

`bench_all` flagged `pred AND exists` at 1.16x and `repeat-group head(b)` at 1.24x — both query
rows a CSV codec change cannot reach. Interleaved min-of-three on the first: 549.1 against
547.3, flat. The second was confirmed flat one item ago (89,248 against 90,038).

## 102. The non-streaming codecs are the decode-side gap, and pg-text is the one that matters (2026-10-04)

Item 101 named a streaming `serialize_csv` as the next target. Checking the DECODE dispatch
first moved it, and the new target is both hotter and better measured.

### Only two formats stream on decode

```rust
// lenke-codec/src/decstream.rs
match format {
```
