```rust
    "pg-json" => Some(crate::pg_json::decode_into(input, sink)),
    "graphson" => Some(crate::graphson::decode_into(input, sink)),
    _ => None,   // pg-text and csv fall back to from_graph_data(deserialize(..))
}
```

`None` means the engine materializes a whole `GraphData` and then rebuilds the store from it.
pg-text and csv are the two formats on that path — and `decode pg-text` has the worst DECODE
throughput in the cross-engine table: 9.2 MB in 110.8ms (0.083 MB/ms) against `decode pg-json`'s
17.6 MB in 100.2ms (0.176). Half the bytes in the same time, and it is the one paying the bridge.

### Where its time goes, measured rather than assumed

Staging `decode pg-text` over a 9 MB / 200,000-node document, three runs:

| stage                                       |    ms |
| ------------------------------------------- | ----: |
| `pg_text::decode` → `GraphData` (the parse) |  45.7 |
| `from_graph_data` (the bridge)              |  65.4 |
| the whole `deserialize`                     | 115.6 |

**The bridge is 57% of it.** A streaming decoder removes the bridge AND the `GraphData`
materialization the parse builds, so the ceiling here is roughly 2.3x on an INGEST path — which
is why this now outranks the CSV export work item 101 pointed at.

### Why no code landed this iteration

`DecVal<'a>` is fully borrowed — `Str(&'a str)`, `Map(Vec<(&'a str, …)>)`, no `Cow` — so a
streaming pg-text decoder needs any unescaped string to live in a per-line scratch that is
filled COMPLETELY before the borrowed views are taken (a later `Vec` push would invalidate
them). `parse_labels_props` interleaves labels, keys and values in one token loop, so it has to
become two passes, with a borrowed `parse_scalar` beside the owned one. That is 80-120 lines of
delicate lifetime work on the ingest path, and the honest way to do it is with the time to
verify byte-identity properly rather than at the end of an iteration.

The design is settled, which is the part worth writing down:

- **`decode_into` becomes the only parser, and `decode` is expressed through it** via a
  GraphData-collecting sink. The alternative — a second borrowed parser beside the owned one —
  is two implementations of one grammar that can drift apart, which is the divergence class the
  byte-identity fuzzers exist to catch. One parser cannot drift.
- `parse_id` returns `Cow<str>`: borrowed when the id is unquoted (the common case), owned only
  when unescaping. It currently calls `raw.to_string()` unconditionally. NOTE this is worth
  nothing on its own — `GraphData` owns its strings, so the allocation is merely moved, not
  removed. It only pays off behind the streaming path, which is why no partial version of this
  change is worth shipping.
- `csv` gets the same treatment afterwards, and then `decstream.rs`'s `_ => None` arm is empty
  and every format streams both ways.

### A third normalization, and a third row ruled out

`decode ndjson (5 edges/node)` has the largest absolute numbers in the table (ts 2801ms, ffi
737ms) and the worst MB/ms of anything (0.028), which looks alarming until the right denominator
is used. That row carries 200,000 nodes AND 1,000,000 edges, so per ELEMENT it is 614ns against
the nodes-only row's 577ns. Edge ingest resolves two endpoints per edge; it is doing more work
per byte by construction. Not an anomaly — and bytes are the wrong unit whenever the element mix
changes, just as the engine ratio was the wrong unit in item 101.

## 103. pg-text now decodes straight into the store (2026-10-04)

Item 102's located target, built. pg-text was one of two formats (with csv) that had no
streaming decoder, so `deserialize` materialized a whole owned `GraphData` and rebuilt the store
from it — on a 9 MB / 200,000-node document the parse was 45.7ms, `from_graph_data` 65.4ms, the
whole decode 115.6ms.

| `decode pg-text` (ffi), min of three |                                        |
| ------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------: |
| before                               |                                110.8ms |
| after                                |                     **77.2ms** — 1.44x |
| throughput                           | 0.083 → 0.119 MB/ms (pg-json is 0.176) |

Short of the ~2.3x the bridge's share suggested, and the reason is worth keeping: the
`StoreSink` still does the insertion work `from_graph_data` used to do. What the change removes
is the `GraphData` in the middle — the owned `Vec<Node>` with its per-node `String` id,
`Vec<String>` labels and `Vec<(String, Value)>` props — not the store building.

### One parser, two adapters

The tempting shape is a second borrowed parser beside the owned one. That would be two
implementations of one grammar that can drift apart, which is the divergence class the
byte-identity fuzzers exist to catch. Instead the grammar is parsed ONCE into `CowVal<'a>` —
strings as `Cow<'a, str>` — and the two forms are adapters over it:

- `CowVal::into_value()` → the owned `Value`, for `decode`;
- `CowVal::as_decval()` → the borrowed `DecVal`, for `decode_into`.

Borrowing is the common case and that is the point: **`unescape` is the IDENTITY on a body with
no backslash**, so `unescape_cow` borrows a quoted token that has no escape, and an unquoted one
always borrows. Only a genuine escape allocates.

`GraphSink::node` takes `props: &[(&str, DecVal<'_>)]` with a lifetime independent of the input,
which is what makes this legal: the borrowed views point either at `input` or at the
`Cow::Owned` strings held in two locals that are complete before any reference into them is
taken and that outlive the sink call. Nothing is cloned to make the call.

### Teeth — and the differential test is blind to half of it

| mutant                                                         | caught by                                                                    |
| -------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `as_decval` swaps a temporal's `tag` and `iso`                 | `streaming_pg_text_decode_matches_the_graphdata_path`                        |
| `unescape_cow` never unescapes                                 | `lenke-codec`'s `quoted_control_chars_round_trip` — **NOT** the differential |
| the edge branch takes `&tokens[1..]` instead of `&tokens[2..]` | nothing, and correctly so — see below                                        |

The second is the instructive one. Sharing one parser removes drift, but it also means a GRAMMAR
bug makes BOTH adapters equally wrong, so a streaming-vs-bridge differential goes green through
it: 901 engine tests passed under that mutant. **The differential guards the ADAPTERS; the
round-trip tests in `lenke-codec` guard the GRAMMAR.** Both crates have to be in a mutation run
for this file, which is the [[mutation-must-include-core]] rule applying to `lenke-codec` too.

The third is an EQUIVALENT mutant, not an uncaught one: a `to` endpoint is by definition an id
token (no unquoted colon), and `parse_labels_props` skips any token without a `key:` separator,
so including or excluding it cannot change the result for a valid document. Recorded rather than
chased, and no test was contrived for it.

### Still open

`csv` is now the only format on the bridge, for both directions, so `decstream.rs`'s
`_ => None` arm has one occupant left. And on the TS side `decode pg-text` is 559-669ms against
native's 77.2 — the ratio went from 5.12x to 7.24x because native moved, making the TypeScript
pg-text decoder the widest codec gap in the table.

## 104. The TS pg-text decoder allocated eleven times a node (2026-10-04)

Item 103 left the TypeScript pg-text decoder as the widest codec gap in the table — 559.6ms
against native's 77.2 (6-7x) — and the profile named the spot immediately rather than after two
wrong guesses, which is what going to `bun --cpu-prof` first buys.

| self-time, TS `decode pg-text` |    before |
| ------------------------------ | --------: |
| `parseLabelsAndProps`          | **29.9%** |
| `tokenizeLine`                 |     13.1% |
| `#commitProperties`            |      6.1% |
| `labels`                       |      5.8% |
| `parseScalar`                  |      5.6% |
| `parseId`                      |      3.0% |

About half the decode is the parser and about a quarter is graph building.

### The Map existed only for a case that is rare

`parseLabelsAndProps` collected into a `Map<string, PropertyValue[]>` and then unwrapped it in a
second loop. That is a Map per LINE plus a one-element array per PROPERTY plus the result object
— about eleven allocations a node on a three-property document — and all of it to handle
REPEATED keys, which is the exception.

It now writes straight into the result object, and a `Set` of promoted keys is allocated lazily,
only once a key actually repeats. `promoted` mirrors the Rust decoder's own list rather than
testing `Array.isArray`: a value that is already an array must not be appended to unless this
parser is what made it one. `parseScalar` cannot return an array today, so `Array.isArray` would
be sound — but only by that incidental fact, and the lazy `Set` costs nothing in the common case.

Key ORDER is unchanged. The old code's `Map` preserved insertion order, but it was unwrapped
into a `Record` at the end, and a JS object orders integer-like keys first regardless — so the
final object was already subject to that, and writing directly into it changes nothing.

### And the unescape regex ran on every quoted token

`body.replace(/\\(.)/g, …)` scans the whole string even when it cannot match, and an escape is
the exception: every quoted id, key and value in a document paid for the scan. `unescapeBody`
guards it with `body.includes('\\')` — the same identity the Rust decoder relies on. `parseScalar`
halved, 5.6% → 2.7%.

| `decode pg-text` (ts), min of three |                     |
| ----------------------------------- | ------------------: |
| before                              |             559.6ms |
| after                               | **479.0ms** — 1.17x |
| ratio to native                     |       7.24x → 6.21x |

### Teeth

| mutant                                                                    | caught by                                                                                                          |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| promotion never recorded, so a third repeat re-wraps instead of appending | `repeated keys decode to a list…`, the 300-seed round-trip, and both streaming tests                               |
| a repeated key overwrites instead of listing                              | the same four                                                                                                      |
| `unescapeBody` never unescapes                                            | **a hardening test** — `a label or key containing a newline cannot forge a new element on decode`, plus two others |

The third is the one worth noting: dropping the unescape is not merely a wrong value, it is an
element-forgery vector, because an unescaped newline inside a label or key would split the line.
That test existed already and is exactly the right net for this change.

### What is left in that decoder

`parseLabelsAndProps` is still 25.1% of a now-smaller total: the remaining cost is two
`token.slice` allocations per property, which `parseId`/`parseScalar` need as substrings —
removing them means passing offsets instead, a more invasive change. `tokenizeLine` is 15.0%
and untouched.

## 105. The biggest cost in TS edge ingest is not the double lookup (2026-10-04)

`decode ndjson (5 edges/node)` is the largest absolute number in the cross-engine table — ts
2801ms — and NDJSON is the primary snapshot/reload format, so it is where TypeScript users
actually spend time. The profile found a textbook target immediately:

| self-time, TS NDJSON decode of 200,000 nodes + 1,000,000 edges |           |
| -------------------------------------------------------------- | --------: |
| **`indexEdgeLabel`**                                           | **22.7%** |
| `JSON.parse`                                                   |     17.3% |
| `getVertexById`                                                |     11.3% |
| `labels`                                                       |      8.1% |
| `#commitProperties`                                            |      7.6% |
| `validateLabel`                                                |      6.5% |

`indexEdgeLabel` costing more than `JSON.parse` is a striking enough result to chase, and what
it does looks indefensible: it probes FIVE maps with `has` and then `get` — about ten hash
lookups an edge where five would do, several on a vertex-id STRING key. Over a million edges
that is ~5,000,000 redundant lookups. `indexVertexLabel` has the same shape.

### It is free money and it is not there

Collapsing every pair into one `get` with an undefined check, interleaved min of three with a
full rebuild each round:

| round | before |  after |
| ----- | -----: | -----: |
| 1     | 2845.9 | 2913.9 |
| 2     | 2893.0 | 2872.7 |
| 3     | 2766.1 | 2876.7 |

2766.1 against 2872.7 at the minimum — flat to marginally WORSE, inside a band that spans ~5%
for the same build. **REJECTED**, and recorded on `indexEdgeLabel` itself so the next reader
does not re-derive it.

The likely reason is that JSC caches the key's hash, so a `get` immediately after a `has` on the
same key is nearly free — the redundancy is real but it is not where the time goes. What that
function actually costs is the `Set`/`Map` INSERTIONS and the inner `Map`/`Set` allocated the
first time a vertex id is seen, neither of which the rewrite touched. A real win there means
changing the index SHAPE (one adjacency map per vertex instead of a nested map-of-label-to-set,
say), which is a data-structure change with its own correctness surface, not a lookup tweak.

This is the fourth rejected lever recorded in this pass, after the two `pull` layout levers and
the `Keyed`-wrapper bypass — and the second where the profile's hottest frame turned out not to
be actionable. A high self-time says where the program IS, not that the code there is wasteful.

### A flaky task, not a regression

`nx run-many -t test` failed once during this iteration and nx itself labelled
`@lenke/sync:test` a flaky task; the rerun passed. `packages/sync` was then run five times on
the changed tree and five times on a stashed baseline — 176 pass, 0 fail, ten for ten. Noted
rather than chased: nothing in `Graph.ts`'s indexers changes iteration order (the same `add`
calls in the same sequence), which is the only thing a sync test could notice.

## 106. `decode csv` was never measured, and it is the worst codec path (2026-10-04)

Item 103 left csv as the only format still on the engine's `GraphData` bridge in BOTH directions,
and item 101 measured its ENCODE side. Its decode side turned out to be absent from the
cross-engine bench entirely: `DECODABLE` listed `pg-json`, `graphson` and `pg-text` and stopped,
with no comment saying why. (`ndjson` is absent for a good reason — it has two dedicated decode
rows of its own.)

Adding it took one line, and the row works in both engines, so there was no hidden reason:

|              |      ts |     ffi | ts/ffi |
| ------------ | ------: | ------: | -----: |
| `decode csv` | 358.7ms | 135.8ms |  2.63x |

The ratio is unremarkable, which is exactly why it needed the other denominator. The csv document
for this fixture is 6.2 MB — the smallest of all five formats — so per byte:

| native decode            |     MB/ms |
| ------------------------ | --------: |
| graphson                 |     0.247 |
| pg-json                  |     0.176 |
| pg-text (after item 103) |     0.118 |
| **csv**                  | **0.046** |

**The worst codec throughput in the table in either direction**, and the one path still
materializing a whole `GraphData`. Item 102's stage split for pg-text put the bridge at 57% of
that decode; csv's own encode measurement in item 101 put it at ~64% there. So csv decode is the
last and largest instance of a cost whose shape is already understood and whose fix already has
three siblings in the tree.

Verified rather than assumed: the TS side decodes all 200,000 nodes back, and the ffi side loads
without the harness printing a failure line — which it would, since item 94 made a failing row
report its reason instead of a bare `n/a`.

### Why this is its own item

`csv::decode` parses the whole document into `Vec<Vec<Cell>>` before building anything, so a
streaming decoder is a restructure of `parse_csv` into a row iterator ON TOP of the borrowed-value
work pg-text needed. That is the same size of job as item 103, which took a full pass, and the
number above is what justifies spending one on it rather than guessing it was worth it.

## 107. The csv parser allocated a String per cell (2026-10-04)

Item 106's number justified spending a pass here: `decode csv` at 0.046 MB/ms was the worst
codec throughput in the table in either direction. Staging it first, over the 6.2 MB / 200,000-node
document, changed the plan:

| stage                                   |    ms |
| --------------------------------------- | ----: |
| `csv::decode` → `GraphData` (the parse) |  79.7 |
| `from_graph_data` (the bridge)          |  61.7 |
| whole                                   | 172.4 |

Unlike pg-text, where the bridge was 57%, here the PARSE is the bigger half at 46%. So the
streaming decoder was not the first move after all — the parse was.

### The obvious thing was not the cost. Again.

`parse_csv` opened with `let chars: Vec<char> = input.chars().collect()` — the whole document
materialized at four bytes a character, ~25 MB for 6.2 MB of input, before parsing began, then
walked one `char` at a time with index arithmetic. That looks like the answer.

It is not: scanning `input.as_bytes()` for the next structural byte and appending whole RUNS with
`push_str` measured **flat** — parse 79.7ms against 80.4ms. Kept anyway, because it is the simpler
shape and it is what makes the borrowing below expressible, but it won nothing. Third time in
this pass that a structural-looking cost was not where the time went.

### What it actually was

Every cell owned a `String` — `std::mem::take(&mut field)` — so the parse allocated **once per
cell**, about a million for that document. `Cell` now carries a `Cow<'a, str>` and borrows
whenever a field's content is one contiguous span of the input: an unquoted field, or a quoted
one with no `""` inside, which is nearly all of them.

|                                                    |  before |               after |
| -------------------------------------------------- | ------: | ------------------: |
| parse                                              |  79.7ms |  **48.4ms** (1.65x) |
| whole decode, in process                           | 172.4ms |     119.4ms (1.44x) |
| bench `decode csv` (ffi), interleaved min of three | 133.6ms | **106.6ms** (1.25x) |
| throughput                                         |   0.046 |         0.058 MB/ms |

A field assembled from SEVERAL runs promotes to an owned `String` at the moment it stops being
one span, so the rare shape costs exactly what it used to.

### A measurement trap worth recording

The first bench reading of this change said 135.8 → 132.7ms, i.e. nothing — taken with
`BENCH_ENGINES=ts,ffi`, where the TypeScript engine runs first in the same process. Isolating the
engine (`BENCH_ENGINES=ffi`) and interleaving gives 133.6 → 106.6. **Running two engines in one
process perturbs the later one**, so a per-engine A/B has to measure that engine alone. That is
how a real 1.25x nearly got recorded as flat.

### Teeth, and the one that needed a weird document

| mutant                                               | caught by                                                              |
| ---------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| the `""` escape dropped instead of written           | `a_quote_in_an_id_or_label_round_trips`, `quoting_labels_and_formulas` |
| a second run REPLACES the first instead of promoting | **nothing** at first — 31 tests green                                  |
| a bare CR outside quotes kept in the field           | the same new test                                                      |

The promotion path is the genuinely new logic and nothing produced a multi-run field. Two inputs
do, and both are shapes this decoder has always accepted from a foreign file: a quote that OPENS
mid-field (`ab"cd"` is one field reading `abcd`, because the quote switches on quoting for the
rest of it rather than starting a new field), and a bare CR outside quotes, which is dropped
wherever it appears (`ef\rgh` is `efgh`, assembled from the run before and the run after).
`a_field_made_of_several_runs_still_reads_whole` pins both.

### Ninth and tenth screen false positives

`bench_all` flagged `dedup out.out` at 1.16x SLOWER and `pred AND exists` at 1.16x SLOWER — both
query rows a csv parser cannot reach. Interleaved min of three: dedup 9565.4 against 9748.9
(1.02x) and pred 546.8 against 549.5 (1.005x). `dedup out.out` flagged 1.29x FASTER in item 103's
sweep from a different baseline and spans 9565-11068 for the same binary.

### Still open

The bridge is untouched: `from_graph_data` is still ~60ms of the 119ms decode, and csv remains
the only format on it in either direction.

## 108. Every textual format now streams both ways (2026-10-04)

The last piece of the bridge story. csv was the only format left on `from_graph_data`, in either
direction — ~60ms of what item 107 had reduced to a 119ms decode, a second traversal over a
`Vec<Node>` the parse had just built and nothing else needed.

| `decode csv` (ffi), interleaved min of three, engine ISOLATED |                         |
| ------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------: |
| before                                                        |                 103.3ms |
| after                                                         |      **75.4ms** — 1.37x |
| with item 107                                                 | 133.6 → 75.4, **1.77x** |
| throughput                                                    |     0.046 → 0.082 MB/ms |

`decstream.rs`'s `_ => None` arm is now unreachable for every textual format: pg-json, graphson,
pg-text and csv all decode straight into the host's sink.

### One row walker, not two

`csv::decode` is expressed THROUGH `csv::decode_into` via a new `GraphDataSink`, so the row shape
— which cell is the id, which are fixed, where the `=== EDGES ===` split falls — is implemented
once. The alternative, a second walker beside the owned one, is two implementations of one layout
free to drift apart.

Three reusable pieces landed in `decstream.rs` to make that cheap, and they are what any future
codec needs:

- `value_to_decval(&Value) -> DecVal<'_>` — an owned value viewed as a borrowed one;
- `decval_to_value(&DecVal) -> Value` — the reverse;
- `GraphDataSink` — a sink that collects into `GraphData`.

The values handed to the sink are still built OWNED, per row, and then viewed. That is the
deliberate limit of this change: the per-cell allocation item 107 measured is untouched, and what
goes away is the whole-document materialization plus the bridge's second pass. Pushing borrowing
all the way down would mean rewriting `decode_cell`'s sentinel/override/list/temporal branches to
produce borrowed values — a much larger job for the remaining slice.

### Teeth, and the shared-adapter blindness again

| mutant                                               | caught by                                                                   |
| ---------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| `value_to_decval` swaps a temporal's `tag` and `iso` | `csv::tests::temporal_columns_round_trip` — **NOT** the engine differential |
| `decode_into` never emits the edge section           | 5 tests, including the differential                                         |
| `GraphDataSink` drops a node's labels                | the differential, and two csv round-trips                                   |

The first is the pg-text lesson (item 103) confirmed a second time, and now with a named cause:
once `decode` is expressed through `decode_into`, a bug in the SHARED adapter makes both sides
equally wrong, so a streaming-vs-bridge differential cannot see it. **The differential guards the
wiring; the codec crate's round-trips guard the values.** A mutation run for these files has to
cover both crates.

### Screen, and the count so far

`bench_all`: 70 comparable rows, two flagged — `one pred` 1.33x and `two preds` 1.32x, both
FASTER, and both query rows a csv decoder cannot reach. Noise in the harmless direction, so not
chased. For the record this pass has now seen ten screen flags that min-of-three showed to be
noise and two more in the favourable direction; the screen remains useful only as a prompt to
measure.
